Arithmetic duality for p p -adic pro-étale cohomology of analytic curves Thanks: The authors’ research was supported in part by the grant NR-19-CE40-0015-02 COLOSS and by the grant No. DMS-1928930 of the National Science Foundation while P.C. and W.N. were in residence at the Mathematical Sciences Research Institute in Berkeley, California, during the Spring 2023 semester.
Pierre Colmez, Sally Gilles, Wiesława Nizioł

Abstract
. We prove a Poincaré duality for arithmetic p p -adic pro-étale cohomology of smooth dagger curves over finite extensions of 𝐐 p {\bf Q}_{p} . We deduce it, via the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence, from geometric comparison theorems combined with Tate and Serre dualities. The compatibility of all the products involved is checked via reduction to the ghost circle, for which we also prove a Poincaré duality (showing that it behaves like a proper smooth analytic variety of dimension 1 / 2 1/2 ). Along the way we study functional analytic properties of arithmetic p p -adic pro-étale cohomology and prove that the usual cohomology is nuclear Fréchet and the compactly supported one – of compact type.



1.
Introduction

This paper is a contribution to the study of dualities in
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology of analytic varieties.

1.1.
Statement of the main theorem

Let
p
be a prime and let
K
be a finite extension of
{\bf Q}_{p}
. Our main theorem is the following duality result.

Theorem 1.1
.

(Arithmetic Poincaré duality)
Let
X
be a smooth, geometrically irreducible, dagger variety
1
1

1

Dagger varieties, introduced by Grosse-Klönne in
[
22
]
, are rigid analytic varieties with overconvergent structure sheaves.
of dimension
1
over
K
. Assume that
X
is proper, Stein, or affinoid. Then:

(1)

There is a natural trace map isomorphism of solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces
2
2

2

See Chapter
5
for a definition of compactly supported pro-étale cohomology.

(1.2)

{\rm Tr}_{X}:\,H^{4}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

(2)

The pairing

H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{4-i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j)){\to}H^{4}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{X}}{\bf Q}_{p}

is a perfect duality, i.e., we have induced isomorphisms of solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces

\displaystyle\gamma_{X,i}:H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{4-i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*},

\displaystyle\gamma_{X,i}^{c}:H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{4-i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*},

where
(-)^{*}
denotes
\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(-,{\bf Q}_{p})
.

Remark 1.3
.

(i)
If the curve
X
is proper then the cohomology groups
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are of finite
rank over
{\bf Q}_{p}
;
this is also the case if
j\neq 1
and
if
X
is affinoid (or Stein with
H^{1}_{\rm dR}(X)
finite dimensional).

(ii) If
X
is Stein,
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is a
nuclear
3
3

3

Nuclear in the classical sense not in the solid sense.
Fréchet
and
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

is a space of compact type; if
X
is a dagger affinoid – it is the other way around.

Remark 1.4
.

If
X
is proper or Stein we have a more general derived duality: the duality map

(1.5)

{\gamma_{{X}}}:{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\xrightarrow{}{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4]),

where
{\mathbb{D}}(-)={\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(-,{\bf Q}_{p})
, is a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})
.
The isomorphisms
\gamma_{X,i}
are obtained from it because all the higher
\operatorname{Ext}
groups involved vanish since
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is of compact type.
The isomorphisms
\gamma^{c}_{X,i}
are obtained from
\gamma_{X,i}
by dualizing using the fact that the spaces
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are (solid) reflexive.

We venture to conjecture an arithmetic Poincaré duality in any dimension:

Conjecture 1.6
.

Let
X
be a smooth Stein dagger variety over
K
, geometrically irreducible, of dimension
d
. Then:

(1)

The cohomology groups
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
and
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are nuclear Fréchet and of compact type, respectively.

(2)

We have (quasi-)isomorphisms in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1-j))[2d+2]),

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\simeq H^{2d+2-i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1-j))^{*},

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\simeq H^{2d+2-i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1-j))^{*}.

Example 1.7
.

The starting point of our study of dualities for
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology of analytic spaces was the computation for
X=D
, the open unit disc. For example, we computed that:

\displaystyle H^{1}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(1))

\displaystyle\simeq({\mathscr{O}}(D)/K)\oplus H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1)),

\displaystyle H^{3}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(1))

\displaystyle\simeq({\mathscr{O}}(\partial D)/{\mathscr{O}}(D))\oplus H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}),

where
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}=\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)
and
\partial D:=\varprojlim_{n}D\mathbin{\fgebackslash}D_{n}

is the “ghost circle”,
boundary of the unit disk
(here,
D_{n}
is the closed ball
\{v_{p}(z)\geq\frac{1}{n}\}
).
The splitting is not canonical but it is compatible with products.
This computation showed that there is a duality of the form stated in Theorem
1.1
and, moreover, that it is induced by Galois duality and coherent duality (i.e., Serre duality):

\displaystyle H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle\simeq H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))^{*},

\displaystyle H^{0}(D,\Omega^{1}_{D})

\displaystyle\simeq H^{1}_{c}(D,{\mathscr{O}}_{D})^{*}.

We used here the isomorphisms (the first one is almost the
definition of
H^{1}_{c}(D,{\mathscr{O}}_{D})
, taking into account vanishing of

H^{2}(D,{\mathscr{O}}_{D})
; the second is induced by
f\mapsto df
: that it is an isomorphism
is equivalent to the fact that
H^{1}_{\rm dR}(D)=0
):

(1.8)

H^{1}_{c}(D,{\mathscr{O}}_{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}{\mathscr{O}}(\partial D)/{\mathscr{O}}(D),\quad H^{0}(D,\Omega^{1}_{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}{\mathscr{O}}(D)/K.

Since
[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]<\infty
, the above coherent
K
-duality can be turned into a
{\bf Q}_{p}
-duality by
composing with
{\rm Tr}_{K/{\bf Q}_{p}}
.

Remark 1.9
.

The above results for the open disk do not involve the solid formalism, and
we attempted at first to write this paper using classical functional analysis as in
[
15
]
.
We passed to solid formalism because of two reasons.
One was a need for a derived dual that would work with Mayer-Vietoris sequences and which does not seem to exist in the classical world in the generality we wanted.
The second one was
a construction of topological Hochschild-Serre spectral sequences which we did not know how to do in the classical set-up.

1.2.
The proof of Theorem
1.1

An analog of Theorem
1.1
holds for schemes and the proof uses Galois descent from geometric, i.e., over
C:=\widehat{\overline{K}}
, Poincaré duality and Galois duality (the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence does not degenerate at
E_{2}
but it does degenerate at
E_{3}
).
An analogous argument, starting with geometric Poincaré duality due to Zavyalov
[
34
]
, Gabber, and Mann
[
25
]
, yields Theorem
1.1
for proper rigid analytic varieties (in fact, in any dimension).

We use here a similar strategy.
In our setting we do not yet have the geometric duality so we replace it with comparison isomorphisms.
They allow us to pass from
geometric
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology to de Rham data and the terms of the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence can be identified with coherent cohomology and Galois cohomology of
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces with some finiteness properties.
Our Poincaré duality is now deduced, via this spectral sequence, from coherent and Galois dualities.

\bullet

Geometric comparison results.

Recall that, for smooth Stein rigid analytic varieties over
K
,
we have comparison theorems between geometric
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology and geometric syntomic cohomology.
In the case of usual cohomology they are due to Colmez-Dospinescu-Nizioł
[
15
]
and Colmez-Nizioł
[
18
]
; in the case of compactly supported cohomology – to Achinger-Gilles-Nizioł
[
1
]
.

In the case where
X
is a smooth, geometrically irreducible, Stein curve over
K
, they yield:

(1)

Vanishings:

(1.10)

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})=0,\quad\mbox{ for }i\neq 0,1,

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})=0,\quad\mbox{ for }i\neq 1,2.

(2)

Isomorphisms:

H^{0}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq{\bf Q}_{p},\quad H^{1}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\xrightarrow{\sim}{\rm HK}^{1}_{c}(X_{C},1),

where
4
4

4

Here
\breve{F}
is the completion of the maximal unramified extension of the fraction field
F
of the Witt vectors of the residue field of
K
and
\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}}
is the semistable period ring of Fontaine in its Banach form.

{\rm HK}^{j}_{*}(X_{C},i):=(H^{j}_{{\rm HK},*}(X_{C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{i}}
and
H^{i}_{{\rm HK},*}(X_{C})
, for
*=[\phantom{x}],c
, is the Hyodo-Kato cohomology.

(3)

Exact sequences:

(1.11)

\displaystyle 0\to{\mathscr{O}}(X_{C})/C\to

\displaystyle H_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}^{1}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{C},1)\to 0

\displaystyle{\rm HK}^{1}_{c}(X_{C},2)\to H^{1}{\rm DR}_{c}(X_{C},2)\to

\displaystyle H^{2}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\to{\bf Q}_{p}(1)\to 0,

where we set

\displaystyle{\rm DR}_{c}(X_{C},i):=(H^{1}_{c}(X,{\mathscr{O}}_{X}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/F^{i})\to H^{1}_{c}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/F^{i-1}))[-1],

The last sequence yields the definition of the geometric trace map

{\rm Tr}_{X_{C}}:H^{2}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to{\bf Q}_{p}.

The arithmetic trace map (
1.2
)
is defined as the composition

{\rm Tr}_{X}:H^{4}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\simeq H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2)))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\rm Tr}_{X_{C}}(1)}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\rm Tr}_{K}}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

\bullet

Galois descent.
For
s\in{\bf Z}
, we have Hochschild-Serre spectral sequences

(1.12)

E^{i,j}_{2}=H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{j}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},*}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(s)))\Rightarrow H^{i+j}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},*}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(s)).

The computations done above show that to understand the terms of the spectral sequences (
1.12
) we need to understand Galois cohomology of

(1.13)

\displaystyle H^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C(s),\quad H^{1}_{c}(X,{\mathscr{O}}_{X}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{K}C(s),\quad{\rm HK}^{1}_{c}(X_{C},1)(s).

We used here the isomorphisms (
1.8
).
We claim that, in the case
H^{1}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{C})
is of finite rank over
\breve{F}
, this cohomology is or trivial or isomorphic to

(1.14)

H^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}),\quad H^{1}_{c}(X,{\mathscr{O}}_{X}),\quad H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},V),

where
V
is a finite rank
{\bf Q}_{p}
-Galois representation. Indeed, note that the last group in (
1.13
) is an almost
C
-representation
[
20
]

(a finite rank
C
-vector space plus/minus a finite rank
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space). Our claim then follows
easily from a generalization of Tate’s computations:

H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},C(j))\simeq\begin{cases}K&\mbox{ if }j=0,i=0,1;\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

By the vanishing results (
1.10
), the spectral sequences (
1.12
) have only two nontrivial columns, hence degenerate at
E_{2}
.
Looking at the terms of the spectral sequences in (
1.14
), we see that if we knew that the arithmetic pro-étale product is compatible with the coherent and Galois products we would have our duality (at least in the case the Hyodo-Kato cohomology is of finite rank but we can always reduce to that case).
However, we have found it very difficult to check this compatibility for a general curve
X
as above: the main difficulty arises from the exact sequence (
1.11
) that does not behave well with respect to products.
We decided thus to pass from
X
to simpler curves.

Nevertheless, the above computations imply the following functional analytic result:

Theorem 1.15
.

Let
X
be a smooth Stein dagger curve over
K
, geometrically irreducible. Then
the cohomology groups
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
and
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are nuclear Fréchet and of compact type, respectively.

This is because we know these properties for
H^{0}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X})
and
H^{1}_{c}(X,{\mathscr{O}}_{X})
, respectively, hence for the terms of the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequences, and the extension problems arising from the spectral sequences can be solved.

\bullet

Reductions.
By a simple limit argument, to prove the derived Poincaré duality (
1.5
), we can pass from a general Stein curve to a wide open curve, i.e., to a complement of a finite number of closed discs in a proper curve (we allow for a finite field extension here). For wide open curves we use a Mayer-Vietoris argument to reduce to proper curves, open discs, and open annuli. For proper curves we know the result, for open discs and annuli we reduce the computation to the one of their boundaries.

\bullet

Arithmetic duality for ghost circle.
We show that, in duality theory, the boundary of an open disc, a ghost circle, behaves like a proper rigid analytic variety over
K
of dimension
1/2
.

Theorem 1.16
.

(Arithmetic duality for ghost circle)
Let
D
be an open disc over
K
. Let
Y:=\partial D
be the boundary of
D
. Then:

(1)

There is a natural trace map isomorphism of solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces

{\rm Tr}_{Y}:\,H^{3}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

(2)

The pairing

(1.17)

H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{3-i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))\to H^{3}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\bf Q}_{p}

is a perfect duality, i.e., we have the induced isomorphism of solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces

\displaystyle\gamma_{Y}:\quad H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{3-i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}.

We will now sketch the proof of this theorem. We define an ascending filtration on
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
:

F^{2}_{i,j}=H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\supset F^{1}_{i,j}\supset F^{0}_{i,j}\supset F^{-1}_{i,j}=0,

such that

\displaystyle F^{2}_{i,j}/F^{1}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)),

\displaystyle F^{1}_{i,j}/F^{0}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})/C(j-1)),

\displaystyle F^{0}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

It is helpful to visualize this filtration in the following way (with
H^{i}(-{\bf Q}_{p}(j)):=H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(-{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
):

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{i,j}:=H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{1}_{i,j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C}(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{2}_{i,j}:=H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

The middle exact row comes from the filtration induced by the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence; the right exact column is induced by the syntomic filtration from the analog
5
5

5

Take this sequence for an annuli and go to the limit towards the boundary.
of the exact sequence (
1.11
). The term
F^{1}_{i,j}
is defined as a pullback of the top right square.

Now, the key computation is the following:

Theorem 1.18
.

(Explicit Reciprocity Law)

We have:

(1)

The pairing (
1.17
) is compatible with the above filtration.

(2)

On the associated grading the pairing (
1.17
) yields a pairing induced by the Galois cohomology pairing and coherent pairing.

The proof of this Theorem interpolates between syntomic and
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-techniques.

This finishes the proof of Theorem
1.16
and hence of Theorem
1.1
, the main theorem of this paper.

1.3.
Conjectural geometric duality

We finish the introduction with a discussion of what we think happens over
C
.
Let us start with the example that guided our study.

Example 1.19
.

Let
D
be the open unit disc over
C
. The nontrivial cohomology groups are:

\displaystyle H^{0}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq{\bf Q}_{p}(j),\quad

\displaystyle H^{1}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq({\mathscr{O}}(D)/C)(j-1),

\displaystyle H^{2}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\simeq{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)\oplus({\mathscr{O}}(\partial D)/{\mathscr{O}}(D))(j-1).

It looks like we have the right groups for a duality (for appropriate choices of
j
):

{\mathscr{O}}(D)/C
and
{\mathscr{O}}(\partial D)/{\mathscr{O}}(D)
are
C
-vector spaces
in duality via coherent duality and
{\bf Q}_{p}(j)
and
{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)
can be put in duality.
But the degrees are wrong for a Poincaré-type duality to work:
coherent duality adds up to degree
3
but
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space duality adds up to degree
2
.
Also, since
[C:{\bf Q}_{p}]=\infty
, it is impossible to turn a
C
-duality into a
{\bf Q}_{p}
-duality.

To find a set-up in which a duality could be restored,
we turned to the category of Vector Spaces,
i.e.,
v
-sheaves of topological
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces on the category
{\rm Perf}_{C}

of perfectoid spaces over
C
(that it is not unreasonnable to do so
is due to the fact that there is a natural way
[
17
]

to turn pro-étale geometric cohomology groups into VS’s).
There we have the following computation
[
11
, Prop. 10.16]

of
\operatorname{Ext}
-groups:

\displaystyle{\rm{Hom}}_{\rm VS}({\bf Q}_{p},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\simeq{\bf Q}_{p}(1),\quad\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\bf Q}_{p},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))=0,

\displaystyle{\rm{Hom}}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{G}}_{a},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))=0,\quad\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}({\mathbb{G}}_{a},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\simeq C.

Moreover,
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}
, for
i\geq 2
, vanish by
[
2
, Th. 3.8]
.
The nontrivial
\operatorname{Ext}
-group in the second row is generated
by the fundamental exact sequence of Banach-Colmez spaces

0\to{\bf Q}_{p}(1)\to{\mathbb{B}}_{\operatorname{cr}}^{+,\varphi=p}\to{\mathbb{G}}_{a}\to 0

The above computations, ignoring functional analytic questions, yield a Verdier duality isomorphisms
(with
H^{i}_{*}(-{\bf Q}_{p}(j)):=H^{i}_{{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}},*}(-{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
):

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}(H^{2}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j)),{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle H^{1}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),

\displaystyle 0\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\rm VS}(H^{1}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j)),{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to

\displaystyle H^{2}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\rm{Hom}}_{\rm VS}(H^{0}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j)),{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to 0

Guided by this, we venture a conjectural statement of geometric duality for
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology in any dimension (again, ignoring topology):

Conjecture 1.20
.

(Geometric Verdier duality)

Let
X
be a smooth Stein rigid analytic variety over
C
, connected, of dimension
d
. There is a natural quasi-isomorphism

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq{\mathrm{R}}{\rm{Hom}}_{\rm VS}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1-j))[2d],{\bf Q}_{p}(1)).

The interested reader can find more details in
[
14
]
.

Notation and conventions.

Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\bf Q}_{p}
, with ring of integers

{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
and residue field
k
.
Let
\overline{K}
be an algebraic closure of
K
and let
{\mathscr{O}}_{\overline{K}}
denote the integral closure of
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
in
\overline{K}
. Set
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}=\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)
. Let
C=\widehat{\overline{K}}
be the
p
-adic completion of
\overline{K}
. Let

W(k)
be the ring of Witt vectors of
k
with
fraction field
F
(i.e.,
W(k)={\mathscr{O}}_{F}
); let
e=e_{K}
be the ramification index of
K
over
F
.
Let
\breve{F}:=W(\overline{k})[\frac{1}{p}]

denote the completion of the maximal unramified extension of
F
and
let
\varphi
be the absolute
Frobenius on
W(\overline{k})
.

We will denote by
{\bf A}_{\rm inf},{\bf A}_{\operatorname{cr}},{\bf B}_{\operatorname{cr}},\widehat{{\bf B}}_{\operatorname{st}},{\bf B}_{\operatorname{dR}}
the Witt, crystalline, semistable, and de Rham period rings of Fontaine, respectively.

All rigid analytic spaces and dagger spaces considered will be over
K
or
C
. We assume that they are separated, taut, and countable at infinity.

Since the only cohomology with
{\bf Q}_{p}(r)
-coefficients that we consider is the pro-étale
cohomology, we will remove “
{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}
” from the notations, i.e. write:

H^{i}(-,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)):=H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(-,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)),\quad H^{i}_{c}(-,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)):=H^{i}_{{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}},c}(-,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)).

2.
Functional analysis

2.1.
Classical functional analysis

We gather here some basic facts from classical
p
-adic functional analysis that we use in the paper.

2.1.1.
Locally convex vector spaces

Our cohomology groups will be equipped with a canonical topology. To talk about it in a systematic way, we will work in the category
C_{K}
of locally convex
K
-vector spaces. For details the reader may consult
[
15
, Sec. 2.1, 2.3]
. To summarize quickly:

C_{K}

is a quasi-abelian category.
We will denote the category of left-bounded complexes of
C_{K}
by
C(C_{K})
and the associated derived
\infty
-category by

{\mathscr{D}}(C_{K})
.
A morphism of complexes that is a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}(C_{K})
, i.e., its mapping cofiber is strictly exact, will be called a
strict quasi-isomorphism
.
The associated cohomology objects are denoted by
6
6

6

LH
stands for “left heart”.

\widetilde{H}^{n}(E)\in{LH}(C_{K})
:

(2.1)

\widetilde{H}^{n}(E):=\tau_{\leq n}\tau_{\geq n}(E)=({\rm coim}(d_{n-1})\to\ker(d_{n})),\quad E\in C(C_{K});

they are called
classical
if the canonical map
\widetilde{H}^{n}(E)\to{H}^{n}(E)
is an isomorphism
7
7

7

In our situations this is usually equivalent to
H^{n}(E)
being separated.
. Classical objects are closed under extensions in
{\rm LH}(C_{K})
.

2.1.2.
Hausdorff locally convex vector space

Our main reference here is
[
28
, Sec. 3.1]
. We will denote by
C^{H}_{K}
the category of Hausdorff locally convex
K
-vector spaces. It is stable under direct sums and direct products and if
V\subset W
are two locally convex
K
-vector spaces then
W/V
is Hausdorff if and only if
V
is closed in
W
.
The category
C^{H}_{K}
is quasi-abelian: kernels and coimages are defined as in
C_{K}
; cokernels and images are defined using closures of images in
C_{K}
. A sequence in
C^{H}_{K}
is strictly exact if and only if it is strictly exact in
C_{K}
.

2.1.3.
Duality

If
V,W\in C_{K}
, we denote by
{\mathscr{L}}(V,W)
the space of continuous linear maps from
V
to
W
. We write
{\mathscr{L}}_{s}(V,W)
and
{\mathscr{L}}_{b}(V,W)
for the vector space
{\mathscr{L}}(V,W)
equipped with the weak and strong topologies (i.e., the topology of pointwise convergence and the topology of bounded convergence), respectively.
For
V\in C_{K}
, we set
V^{\prime}_{s}:={\mathscr{L}}_{s}(V,K)
and
V^{*}:=V^{\prime}_{b}:={\mathscr{L}}_{b}(V,K)
. Both of these dual spaces are Hausdorff.
If
V
is a Hausdorff space it is called
reflexive
if the duality map
V\to(V^{\prime}_{b})^{\prime}_{b}
is a topological isomorphism.

We will also use
stereotype dual

V^{\star}
of
V\in C_{K}
. It is defined as
{\mathscr{L}}(V,K)
equipped with the topology of compactoid convergence
8
8

8

See Section
2.1.5
for a definition of compactoids.

of
V
. We have continuous maps

V^{\prime}_{b}\to V^{\star}\to V^{\prime}_{s}.

If
V
is a Banach space then they are not topological isomorphisms unless
V
has finite dimension.
For any Banach space, its stereotype dual is a Smith space
9
9

9

A
Smith space
is a complete compactly generated locally convex vector space
V
having a universal compact set, i.e., a compact set
K
, which absorbs every other compact set
T\subseteq V
(i.e.,
T\subseteq\lambda\cdot K
, for some
\lambda>0
).
, and vice versa, for any Smith space, its stereotype dual is a Banach space,
and
(V^{\star})^{\star}=V
if
V
is Banach or Smith.
If we write a Banach space
V\in C_{K}
as
V\simeq(\widehat{\oplus_{I}{\mathscr{O}}_{K}})[1/p]
then its stereotype dual is the Smith space
V^{\star}\simeq(\prod_{I}{\mathscr{O}}_{K})[1/p]
. A Banach space is a Smith space if and only if it is finite dimensional.

2.1.4.
Hausdorff compactly generated locally convex vector spaces

Recall that a topological space
T
is
compactly generated
if a map
f:T\to T^{\prime}
to another topological space is continuous as soon as the composite
S\to T\to T^{\prime}
is continuous for all compact Hausdorff spaces
S
mapping to
T
. The inclusion of compactly generated spaces into all topological spaces admits a right adjoint
T\mapsto T^{\rm cg}
that sends a topological space
T
to its underlying set equipped with the quotient topology for the map
\coprod_{S\to T}S\to T
, where the disjoint union runs over all compact Hausdorff spaces
S
(alternatively, profinite sets
S
) mapping to
T
.

Any first-countable space (in particular, any metrizable topological space) is compactly generated (see
[
8
, Remark 1.6]
for a proof). So, for example, Fréchet spaces are compactly generated. The category of compactly generated spaces is closed under taking coproducts, closed subspaces and quotients by closed subspaces. Hence a colimit of Fréchet spaces is compactly generated if it is Hausdorff; this applies, in particular, to locally convex vector spaces of compact type
10
10

10

See Section
2.1.5
below for the definition of spaces of compact type.
since they are Hausdorff and can be written as a countable colimit of Banach spaces.

The category
C^{\rm Hcg}_{K}
of Hausdorff compactly generated locally convex vector spaces over
K
is quasi-abelian: kernels and coimages are defined as in
C_{K}
; cokernels and images are defined using closures of images in
C_{K}
. A sequence in
C^{\rm Hcg}_{K}
is strictly exact if and only if it is strictly exact in
C_{K}
.

2.1.5.
Spaces of compact type and nuclear spaces

Our references for this section are
[
30
, IV.19]
,
[
31
, Ch. 1]
,
[
27
, Ch. 8]
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
V,W\in C_{K}
be Hausdorff.

(1)

A subset
B\subset V
is called
compactoid
if for any open lattice
L\subset V
there are finitely many vectors
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\in V
such that
B\subset L+{\mathscr{O}}_{K}v_{1}+\cdots+{\mathscr{O}}_{K}v_{m}
. Compactoids are preserved by maps in
C_{K}
.

(2)

A continuous linear map
f:V\to W
is called
compact
if there is an open lattice
L
in
V
such that the closure of
f(L)
in
W
is compactoid and complete. If
W
is quasi-complete (in particular, if it is complete) then this is equivalent to
f(L)
being compactoid.

(3)

V
is called of
compact type
if it is the inductive limit of a sequence

V_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{1}}}{{\to}}V_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{2}}}{{\to}}V_{3}\to\cdots

of Hausdorff spaces
V_{n}\in C_{K}
, for
n\in{\bf N}
, with injective compact linear maps. By the proof of
[
30
, Prop. 16.10]
, we may assume the
V_{n}
’s to be Banach spaces.

We will use often the following facts (see
[
30
, Rem. 16.7]
).

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
g:V\to W
be a compact map.
Then

(1)

If
h:V_{1}\to V
and
f:W\to W_{1}
are arbitrary continuous linear maps then the map
fgh:V_{1}\to W_{1}
is compact.

(2)

If the image of
g
is contained in a closed subpace
W_{0}\subset W
then the induced map
g:V\to W_{0}
is compact.

We will denote by
C_{c,K}
the full subcategory of
C_{K}
consisting of spaces of compact type.
Spaces of compact type are Hausdorff, complete,
and reflexive. Their strong duals are Fréchet and satisfy
V^{\prime}_{b}=\mathop{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\hfill{\lim}\hfil$\crcr\kern 1.0pt\cr\leftarrowfill\crcr\kern-3.0pt\cr}}}\limits_{n}(V^{\prime}_{n})_{b}
. Moreover,
a closed subspace of a space of compact type is also of compact type and so is the relevant quotient;
C_{c,K}
is closed under countable direct sums.

Definition 2.4
.

The space
V\in C_{K}
is called
nuclear
if for any open lattice
L\subset V
there exists another open lattice
M\subset L
such that the canonical map between completions
\widehat{V}_{M}\to\widehat{V}_{L}
is compact
(i.e., the image of
M\to L/p^{L}
is, for any
n
, contained in a module of finite type over
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
).

Nuclear Banach spaces are finite dimensional. Nuclear Fréchet spaces are reflexive. Moreover, a subspace of a nuclear space is nuclear and if this subspace is closed the relevant quotient is nuclear. A countable inductive limit of nuclear spaces is nuclear (see
[
27
, Th. 8.5.7]
). By loc. cit., projective limits
of nuclear spaces are nuclear. Any
compact projective or inductive limit
11
11

11

In the sense of
[
30
, Cor. 16.6, Prop. 16.10]
.
of locally convex
K
-vector spaces is nuclear. A Fréchet space is the strong dual of a space of compact type if and only if it is nuclear.

The following lemma will be essential for us.

Lemma 2.5
.

(1)

Every strict exact sequence of spaces from
C_{K}

0\to V\to W\to W^{\prime}\to 0

such that
V
is of finite rank and
W^{\prime}
is Hausdorff splits (and
W\simeq V\oplus W^{\prime}
).

(2)

In a strict extension of spaces from
C_{K}

(2.6)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
V
is of finite rank over
K
and
W^{\prime}
is nuclear Fréchet,
W
is also nuclear Fréchet. Same holds for spaces of compact type.

Proof.

In the first claim, we may assume that
V
is of rank
1
. Note that
W
is Hausdorff. Take a continuous
linear form
\lambda:W\to K
, not identically
0
on
V
(such a form exists by
[
30
, Cor. 9.3]
). It suffices to show that
the canonical map
V\oplus{\rm Ker}\,\lambda\to W
is a topological isomorphism.

It is an algebraic isomorphism and it is continuous. Hence we just need to check
that it is open. So, let
U_{\lambda}
be an open lattice in
{\rm Ker}\,\lambda
and let
V_{0}

be an open lattice in
V
. We need to show that
U_{\lambda}+V_{0}
is open
in
W
and it is enough to do it
for open sub-lattices of
U_{\lambda}
and
V_{0}
.

Any
neighborhood of
0
in
{\rm Ker}\,\lambda
contains an open of the
form
U\cap({\rm Ker}\,\lambda)
, where
U
is an open lattice
in
W
. Hence we can assume
U_{\lambda}
to be of this form. By construction,
we have an exact sequence
0\to U_{\lambda}\to U\to\lambda(U)\to 0
.

Now,
U
contains
p^{N}V_{0}
for
N
big enough, since it is a lattice.
Then
U_{\lambda}+p^{N}V_{0}
is the inverse image of
p^{N}\lambda(V_{0})

in
U
by
\lambda
, hence it is open as
\lambda
is continuous
and
p^{N}\lambda(V_{0})
is open in
K
. We proved what we wanted, up to
replacing
V_{0}
by its sub-lattice
p^{N}V_{0}
.

The second claim follows immediately from the first one.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

If we have a map of strict exact sequences of complete Hausdorff spaces from
C_{K}

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{V}}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{W}}
\textstyle{W^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{W^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{1}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

such that
V,V_{1}
are of finite rank over
K
, the bottom sequence splits, and the map
f_{W^{\prime}}
is compact then
the map
f_{W}
is compact as well.

Proof.

Take an open lattice
L_{W^{\prime}}
in
W^{\prime}
such that
f_{W^{\prime}}(L_{W^{\prime}})
is compactoid. Let
L_{W}
be the preimage of
L_{W^{\prime}}
in
W
.
Choose a section
s:W_{1}^{\prime}\to W_{1}
of the projection
\pi:W_{1}\to W_{1}^{\prime}
and consider the continuous map

g:W\to V_{1},\quad g(w):=f_{W}(w)-s\pi(f_{W}(w)).

Take a compact lattice
L_{V_{1}}
in
V_{1}
, its preimage (via
g
) in
W
, and then change
L_{W}
to its intersection with that preimage.

Now, we see that
f_{W}(L_{W})
is compactoid: we have

f_{W}(L_{W})\subset g(L_{W})+sf_{W^{\prime}}(L_{W^{\prime}})

and both
g(L_{W})
and
sf_{W^{\prime}}(L_{W^{\prime}})
are compactoid in
W_{1}
. This concludes the proof of our lemma.
∎

Let
C_{nF,K}
denote the full subcategory of
C_{K}
consisting of nuclear Fréchet spaces. The functor

C_{c,K}\to C_{nF,K},\quad V\mapsto V^{\prime}_{b},

is an anti-equivalence of categories. For any two
V,W\in C_{c,K}
the natural linear map
{\mathscr{L}}_{b}(V,W)\to{\mathscr{L}}_{b}(W^{\prime}_{b},V^{\prime}_{b})
is a topological isomorphism.

Lemma 2.8
.

If
X\in C_{K}
is nuclear Fréchet or of compact type then the canonical map
X^{\prime}_{b}\to X^{\star}
is a topological isomorphism.

Proof.

In both cases the space
X
is reflexive, hence Montel (see
[
27
, Cor. 8.4.22]
). But in Montel spaces, by
[
27
, Th. 8.4.5]
,
every bounded subset is compactoid (and vice versa, of course) thus the strong
topology on the algebraic dual of
X
coincides with the topology of compactoid convergence, as wanted.
∎

2.2.
Solid functional analysis

We will review here briefly results from solid functional analysis that we will need. Our main references are
[
5
]
,
[
29
]
,
[
8
]
,
[
9
]
.

2.2.1.
Basic properties of condensed sets

Let Cond denote the category of
condensed sets
, i.e., sheaves of sets on the site of pro-finite sets with coverings given
by finite families of jointly surjective maps
12
12

12

We refer the reader to
[
8
, Lecture III]
for a discussion of set theoretical issues involved in this definition.
or, equivalently, on the pro-étale site
*_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}
of a geometric point. We define similarly condensed groups, rings, etc.

We will denote by
{\rm CondAb}
the category of condensed abelian groups. It is an abelian category
[
8
, Thm. 2.2]
. It has all limits and colimits. Arbitrary products, arbitrary direct sums and filtered colimits are exact. It is generated by compact projective objects.
For a condensed commutative
ring
A
, we will write
{\rm Mod}^{\rm cond}_{A}
for the category of
A
-modules in
{\rm CondAb}
and

\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{A}(-,-)
for its internal
{\rm{Hom}}
(in the case
A={\bf Z}
, we will often omit the
subscript
{\bf Z}
).

Remark 2.9
.

Because of set theoretical issues this definition of the category
{\rm Cond}
is not sensu stricto correct. The correct definition of
{\rm Cond}
is given in
[
8
, Lecture II]
. In this paper we will use the latter though we find it helpful to keep in mind its simplified version given above.

For a condensed set
X
, we think of
X(*)
as the underlying set of
X
and about
X(S)
as the continuous maps from
S
to
X
. A quasi-separated
13
13

13

A condensed set
X
is called
quasi-compact
if there is a profinite set
S
and a surjective map
{S}\to X
; it is called
quasi-separated
if, for any pair of profinite sets
S
and
S^{\prime}
over
X
the fiber product
{S}\times_{X}{S}^{\prime}
is quasi-compact.

condensed set
X
is trivial as soon as
X(*)
is trivial (a fact that is false for a general
X
, see

[
9
, Lecture I]
).

Let
{\rm Top}
denote the category of T1 topological spaces
14
14

14

A topological space is called T1 if all its points are closed.
.
We have a functor

(\underline{\phantom{x}}):{\rm Top}\to{\rm Cond},\quad T\mapsto\underline{T}={\mathscr{C}}(S,T),

where
{\mathscr{C}}(S,T)
denotes the
set of continuous functions from
S
to
T
. Condensed sets that come from topological spaces we will call
classical
.
We quote:

Proposition 2.10
.

(Clausen-Scholze,
[
9
, Prop. 1.2]
)
. The functor
(\underline{\phantom{x}})
:

(1)

has a left adjoint
X\mapsto X(*)_{\rm top}
sending any condensed set
X
to the set
X(*)
equipped
with the quotient topology arising from the map

\coprod\nolimits_{S,a\in X(S)}S\overset{a}{\to}X(*).

(2)

restricted to compactly generated topological spaces is fully faithful.

(3)

induces an equivalence between the category of compact Hausdorff spaces and qcqs (i.e., quasi-compact quasi-separated) condensed sets.

(4)

induces a fully faithful functor from the category of compactly generated weak Hausdorff
15
15

15

A topological space is called
weak Hausdorff
if the image of every continuous map from a compact Hausdorff space into the space is closed. In particular, every Hausdorff space is weak Hausdorff. Every weak Hausdorff space is a T1 space.

spaces, to quasi-separated condensed sets. The category of quasi-separated condensed sets is equivalent
to the category of ind-compact Hausdorff spaces “
\operatorname{colim}_{n}
”
X_{n}
, where all transition maps are
closed immersions. If
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
is a ind-system of compact Hausdorff spaces with closed
immersions and
X=\operatorname{colim}_{n}X_{n}
as topological spaces, then the canonical map

\operatorname{colim}_{n}\underline{X_{n}}\to\underline{X}

is an isomorphism of condensed sets. In particular,
\operatorname{colim}_{n}\underline{X_{n}}
is classical, i.e., it comes from a topological space.

Remark 2.11
.

(1) For
T\in{\rm Top}
, the counit
\underline{T}(*)_{\rm top}\to T
of the adjunction agrees with the counit
T^{\rm cg}\to T
of the adjunction between compactly generated spaces and all topological spaces by
[
8
, Prop. 1.7]
. In particular,
\underline{T}(*)_{\rm top}\simeq T^{\rm cg}
.

(2) For a finite extension
K
of
{\bf Q}_{p}
, we will abbreviate the notation
{\rm Mod}^{\rm cond}_{\underline{K}}
to
{\rm Mod}^{\rm cond}_{K}
; we will call the elements of this category
condensed
K
-vector spaces
.

2.2.2.
Solid modules

We will briefly review basic facts concerning solid modules.

(
\bullet
)
Analytic rings.
We start with analytic rings.

(
i
) (Clausen-Scholze,
[
8
, Th. 5.8]
) The analytic ring
{\bf Z}_{\Box}=({\bf Z},{\mathscr{M}}_{{\bf Z}})
is defined as the ring
{\bf Z}
equipped with the functor of measures
{\mathscr{M}}_{{\bf Z}}
sending an extremally disconnected set
S=\lim_{i}S_{i}
, where each
S_{i}
is a finite set, to the condensed abelian group

{\bf Z}_{\Box}:=\lim_{i}{\bf Z}[S_{i}]
.

(
ii
) (Clausen-Scholze,
[
8
, Prop. 7.9]
) Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\bf Q}_{p}
. There is an analytic structure on the condensed rings
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
and
K
given by sending an extremally disconnected set
S=\lim_{i}S_{i}
,
where each
S_{i}
is finite, to

{\mathscr{O}}_{K,\Box}[S]:=\lim_{i}{\mathscr{O}}_{K}[S_{i}],\quad K_{\Box}[S]:=K\otimes_{{\mathscr{O}}_{K}}{\mathscr{O}}_{K,\Box}[S].

The first analytic ring structure is induced from the analytic ring structure of
{\bf Z}_{\Box}
by base change to
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
.

(
\bullet
)
Solid modules.
Now we pass to solid modules.

Proposition 2.12
.

(Clausen-Scholze
[
8
, Prop. 7.5]
)
Let
A=(A,{\mathscr{M}}_{A})
be one of the analytic rings above.

(1)

The full subcategory of solid
A
-modules

(2.13)

{\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{A}\subset{\rm Mod}^{\rm cond}_{A}

consists of all
A
-modules
M
such that, for all extremally disconnected sets
S
, the maps

{\rm{Hom}}_{A}({\mathscr{M}}_{A}[S],M)\to{\rm{Hom}}_{A}(A[S],M)

are isomorphisms. It
is an abelian subcategory, stable under all limits, colimits, and extensions. The
inclusion (
2.13
) admits a left adjoint

(2.14)

{\rm Mod}^{\rm cond}_{A}\to{\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{A}:\quad M\mapsto M\otimes_{A}(A,{\mathscr{M}}_{A}),

which preserves all colimits and is symmetric monoidal.

(2)

The functor

(2.15)

{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{A})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Mod}^{\rm cond}_{A})

is fully faithful. Its essential image is stable under all limits and colimits. It is given by complexes
M\in{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Mod}^{\rm cond}_{A})
such that the map

{\mathrm{R}}{\rm{Hom}}_{A}({\mathscr{M}}_{A}[S],M)\to{\mathrm{R}}{\rm{Hom}}_{A}(A[S],M)

is a quasi-isomorphism for all extremally disconnected sets
S
.

A complex
M\in{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Mod}^{\rm cond}_{A})
is in
{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{A})
if and only if
H^{i}(M)

is in
{\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{A}
, for all
i
. The functor (
2.15
) admits a left adjoint

(2.16)

{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Mod}^{\rm cond}_{A})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{A}):M\mapsto M\otimes^{\rm L}_{A}(A,{\mathscr{M}}_{A}),

which is the left derived functor of (
2.14
). It is symmetric
monoidal.

(3)

For
M,N\in{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{A})
, we have the derived internal Hom

{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{A}(M,N)\in{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{A}).

The natural map

{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{(A,{\mathscr{M}}_{A})}(M,N)\to{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{A}(M,N)

is a quasi-isomorphism.

Notation 2.17
.

(1) We write
{\rm Solid}:={\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{({\bf Z},{\mathscr{M}}_{\bf Z})}
for the category of solid
abelian groups; we write

{\rm CondAb}\to{\rm Solid}:M\mapsto M^{\Box}

for the functor (
5.22
) of Proposition
2.12
, and call it
solidification
, and we denote by

\otimes^{\Box}_{\bf Z}
the unique
symmetric monoidal tensor product making the solidification functor symmetric monoidal.

(2) For a finite extension
K
of
{\bf Q}_{p}
, we write
{\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{{\mathscr{O}}_{K}}
and
{\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{K}
for the categories of solid
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
-modules and
K
-vector spaces, respectively. We will denote
by
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{O}}_{K,\Box})
and
{\mathscr{D}}(K_{\Box})
the corresponding derived
\infty
-categories.

(3) For a finite extension
K
of
{\bf Q}_{p}
and a commutative solid
K
-algebra
A
, we write
\otimes^{\Box}_{A}
for the symmetric monoidal tensor product
\otimes_{(A,{\mathscr{M}}_{A})}
.

2.2.3.
Locally convex and condensed vector spaces

Consider the functor

{\rm CD}:=(\underline{\phantom{x}}):\quad C^{\rm Hcg}_{K}\to{\rm Mod}^{\rm cond}_{{K}},\quad V\mapsto\underline{V}.

We will denote in the same way its extension to the category of complexes. By Lemma
2.18
below, the functor
{\rm CD}
preserves (strict) quasi-isomorphisms
of complexes of Fréchet spaces or spaces of compact type and if
V
is such a complex then

{\rm CD}(\widetilde{H}^{i}(V))\simeq H^{i}({\rm CD}(V)).

Lemma 2.18
.

The functor
(\underline{\phantom{x}})
maps strict exact sequences of Fréchet spaces over
K
to exact sequences of condensed
{K}
-vector spaces. Similarly, for strict exact sequences of spaces of compact type over
K
.

Proof.

Since the functor
V\mapsto\underline{V}
is left exact, it suffices to show that a strict surjection
V\to W
of Fréchet spaces or of spaces of compact type is carried to a surjection
\underline{V}\to\underline{W}
. For that, it suffices to show that, for an extremally disconnected set
S
, we
have
{\mathscr{C}}(S,V)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(S,W)
, i.e.,
given
g\in{\mathscr{C}}(S,W)
, there exists
{g}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{C}}(S,V)
making the following diagram commute

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{{g}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{W}

Assume first that both
V
and
W
are Fréchet. In that case we have the following argument of Guido Bosco
[
5
, Lemma 1.A.33]
: Since
g(S)
is compact in
W
, by
[
33
, Lemma 45.1]
, it is the image
f(H)
of a compact subset
H

of
V
. We conclude by recalling that the extremally disconnected sets are the projective objects of
the category of compact Hausdorff topological spaces.

Assume now that both
V
and
W
are of compact type. Since
g(S)
is compact in
W
, if we write
W=\operatorname{colim}_{n}W_{n}
, for an ind-system
\{W_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
of Banach spaces over
K
with compact injective transition maps, then
g(S)\subset W_{m}
, for some
m\in{\bf N}
(see
[
30
, Lemma 16.9]
). By
[
29
, Lemma 3.39]
, we have a commutative diagram of solid arrows

\textstyle{V_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{m}}
\textstyle{W_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{W}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{g_{W_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{{g}^{\prime}}

for a
K
- Banach space
V_{m}
. As above, it follows that the dashed arrow exists, which concludes our proof of the lemma.
∎

2.2.4.
Solid Fréchet spaces and solid spaces of compact type

We will define solid Fréchet spaces and solid spaces of compact type as the images in the category of solid
K
-vector spaces of their classical analogs. See
[
29
, Ch. 3]
for alternative definitions.

(i)
Solid Fréchet spaces.
A
solid Banach space
is a solid
K
-vector space of the form
\underline{V}
, for a classical Banach space
V
.
A
solid Fréchet space
is a solid
K
-vector space of the form
\underline{V}
, for a classical Fréchet space
V
. It is called
nuclear
if
V
is nuclear.
The functor
(\underline{\quad})
identifies the categories of classical and solid Fréchet spaces (identifying also exact sequences by Lemma
2.18
). If this does no confusion we will use the term "Fréchet spaces" for elements of both of these categories.

Warning
. The definition of nuclear used here is stronger than the definition of nuclear in the solid formalism. To distinguish, we will call the latter "solid nuclear".

Let
V
be a solid Fréchet space written as a limit
V=\lim_{n}V_{n}
of Banach spaces
V_{n}
with dense transition maps. Then:

(1)

(Topological Mittag-Leffler) Then

{\mathrm{R}}^{j}\lim_{n}V_{n}=0,\quad j\geq 1.

In particular,
V\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{K}(V^{*},K)
.

(2)

If
W
is a Banach space, then
\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{K}(V,W)=\operatorname{colim}_{n}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{K}(V_{n},W)
.

(ii)
Solid spaces of compact type.

A
solid space of compact type
is a solid
K
-vector space of the form
\underline{V}
, for a classical space of compact type
V
.
The functor
(\underline{\quad})
identifies the categories of classical and solid spaces of compact type (identifying also exact sequences by Lemma
2.18
). If this does no confusion we will use the term "spaces of compact type" for elements of both of these categories.

The following two lemmas will be needed later.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
V\in C_{K}
be of compact type. Write it as
V\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{n}V_{n}
with
V_{n}
,
n\in{\bf N}
, Hausdorff and injective compact transition maps. Then
we have a canonical isomorphism of condensed
K
-modules

\operatorname{colim}_{n}\underline{V_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\underline{V}.

In particular, the condensed
K
-module
\operatorname{colim}_{n}\underline{V_{n}}
is classical.

Proof.

Since compact sets are bounded, this follows immediately from
[
30
, Lemma 16.9]
, which states that any bounded subset of
V
comes from some
V_{n}
.
∎

Lemma 2.20
.

If a sequence of solid Fréchet spaces over
K
is exact then the classical sequence is strictly exact. Similarly, for a sequence of solid spaces of compact type over
K
.

Proof.

We start with an exact sequence of solid Fréchet spaces over
K

0\to\underline{V}_{1}\to\underline{V}_{2}\to\underline{V}_{3}\to 0.

We need to show that the sequence of classical Fréchet spaces

(2.21)

0\to{V}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\to}}{V}_{2}\to V_{3}\to 0

is strictly exact.
But the sequence

(2.22)

0\to\underline{V}_{1}(*)_{\rm top}\to\underline{V}_{2}(*)_{\rm top}\to\underline{V}_{3}(*)_{\rm top}\to 0

maps, via topological isomorphisms
\underline{V}_{i}(*)_{\rm top}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}V_{i}
(since
V_{i}
is compactly generated), to the sequence (
2.21
). Since
\underline{V_{3}}
is quasi-separated, the map
f_{1}
is a closed immersion. In particular we have a strict exact sequence of Fréchet spaces

0\to{V}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\to}}{V}_{2}\to V^{\prime}_{3}\to 0

and a continuous injection
f_{2}:V^{\prime}_{3}\to V_{3}
. We need to prove that the map
f_{2}
is a topological isomorphism. Since
f_{2}
is a map between two Fréchet spaces, by the Open Mapping Theorem, it suffices to show that
f_{2}
is an algebraic isomorphism. But, by Lemma
2.18
, we have the exact sequence

0\to\underline{V}_{1}\to\underline{V}_{2}\to\underline{V}^{\prime}_{3}\to 0.

Hence the canonical map
\underline{f}_{2}:\underline{V}^{\prime}_{3}\to\underline{V}_{3}
is an isomorphism, which yields that so is the map
f_{3}
(by the faithfullness of the
(\underline{\phantom{x}})
functor.

The argument for spaces of compact type is similar (the Open Mapping Theorem is valid for LB spaces).
∎

2.2.5.
Solid tensor product

We list properties of the solid tensor product that we will often use.

(1)

(
[
5
, Prop. A.68]
) Let
V,W
be Fréchet spaces over
K
. Then we have a natural isomorphism of solid
K
-vector spaces

\underline{V}\otimes_{K}^{\Box}\underline{W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\underline{V\widehat{\otimes}_{K}W},

where
V\widehat{\otimes}_{K}W
denotes the projective tensor product in the category
C_{K}
.

(2)

(
[
5
, Cor. A.65]
) Any Fréchet space over
K
is acyclic for the tensor product
\otimes^{\Box}_{K}
. That is, if
V
is a Fréchet space over
K
then
(-)\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{K}V\simeq(-)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}V
.

(3)

(
[
5
, Cor. A.67]
)

(a)

Let
\{V_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
be a pro-system of solid nuclear
K
-vector spaces and let
W
be a Fréchet vector space over
K
. Then we have an isomorphism

\lim_{n}(V_{n}\otimes_{K}^{\Box}W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}(\lim_{n}V_{n})\otimes_{K}^{\Box}W.

(b)

Let
\{V_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
be a pro-system in
{\mathscr{D}}(K_{\Box})
of complexes of solid nuclear
K
-vector spaces. Let
W
be a complex of
K
-Fréchet spaces. Then we have a quasi-isomorphism

{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}(V_{n}\otimes_{K}^{{\rm L}\Box}W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}({\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}V_{n})\otimes_{K}^{{\rm L}\Box}W.

The above properties also hold if we replace
K
with
\breve{F}
(with the same references).

3.
Galois cohomology of
K

This chapter gathers together a number of properties of Galois cohomology that we will need later.

3.1.
Preliminaries

We record here few basic facts about Galois cohomology seen via condensed formalism.

3.1.1.
Condensed Galois cohomology

Let
G
be a condensed group. A condense
G
-module is a condensed abelian group endowed with a
{\bf Z}[G]
-module structure. The condensed group cohomology of
G
with values in a condense
G
-module
V
is defined as

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(G,V):={\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Z}[G]}({\bf Z},V)\in{\mathscr{D}}({\rm CondAb}).

Notation 3.1
.

Let
G
be a profinite group.

(a) The
Iwasawa
algebra of
G
is the solid ring

\displaystyle{\mathscr{O}}_{K,\Box}[G]

\displaystyle:=\lim_{H\subset G}{\mathscr{O}}_{K}[G/H]\in{\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{{\mathscr{O}}_{K}},

\displaystyle K_{\Box}[G]={\mathscr{O}}_{K,\Box}[G][1/p]

\displaystyle:=\big(\lim_{H\subset G}{\mathscr{O}}_{K}[G/H]\big)[1/p]\in{\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{K},

where
H
runs over all open and normal subgroups of
G
.

(b) A solid
G
-module over
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
(or a solid
{\mathscr{O}}_{K,\Box}[G]
-module) is a solid abelian group endowed with an
{\mathscr{O}}_{K,\Box}[G]
-module structure. The category of solid

{\mathscr{O}}_{K,\Box}[G]
-modules will be denoted by
{\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{{\mathscr{O}}_{K,\Box}[G]}
and its derived
\infty
-category by
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{O}}_{K,\Box}[G])
.
Similarly, for solid
K_{\Box}[G]
-modules.

We list the following properties of
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(G,-)
:

(1)

(
[
5
, Prop. B.2]
) Let
G
be a profinite group and let
V
be a
{G}
-module in solid abelian groups. Then

(a)

The complex
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},V)
is quasi-isomorphic to the complex of solid abelian groups

(3.2)

V\to\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}[{G}^{1}],V)\to\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}[{G}^{2}],V)\to\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}[{G}^{3}],V)\cdots.

(b)

If
V=\underline{V_{\rm top}}
, with
V_{\rm top}
a T1 topological
G
-module over
{\bf Z}
, then, for all
i\geq 0
, we have a natural isomorphism of abelian groups
16
16

16

The second cohomology group is the continuous group cohomology.

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},V)(*)\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},V_{\rm top}).

(2)

(
[
5
, Prop. B.3]
) For
n\in{\bf N}
, let
\Gamma:={\bf Z}_{p}^{n}
, and let
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}
denote the generators of
\Gamma
. Let
V
be a
\Gamma
-module in
{\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{{\bf Z}_{p}}
. Then we have a quasi-isomorphism

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\Gamma,V)\simeq{\rm Kos}_{\gamma}(V):={\rm Kos}_{V}(\gamma_{1}-1,\ldots,\gamma_{n}-1),

the Koszul complex of
V
with respect to the elements
\gamma_{1}-1,\ldots,\gamma_{n}-1
.

(3)

(
[
29
, Lemma 5.2]
) Let
G
be a profinite group. There is a solid projective resolution of the trivial representation

\cdots\to K_{\Box}[{G}^{n+1}]\to K_{\Box}[{G}^{n}]\to\cdots\to K_{\Box}[{G}^{1}]\to K\to 0.

In particular, if
V
is a
{G}
-module in solid modules over
K
, one has that

{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{K_{\Box}[G]}(K,V)\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},V).

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
G
be a profinite group and let
V
be a finite rank
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space equipped with a continuous action of
G
. Then

(1)

we have
a quasi-isomorphism and isomorphisms

{\rm CD}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},V))\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},\underline{V}),\quad{\rm CD}(\widetilde{H}^{i}({G},V))\simeq H^{i}({G},\underline{V}),\,i\geq 0.

(2)

we have a quasi-isomorphism
17
17

17

Topology on
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},V)
is defined using continuous cochains. See the proof of the lemma for details.
in
{\mathscr{D}}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},\underline{V})(*)_{\rm top}\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},V).

Proof.

For claim (1) we compute

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},V)\simeq C(G,V),\quad n\mapsto{\mathscr{C}}(G^{n-1},V);

\displaystyle{\rm CD}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},V))(S):n\mapsto{\mathscr{C}}(S,{\mathscr{C}}(G^{n-1},V))\simeq{\mathscr{C}}(S\times G^{n-1},V),

where
S
is a profinite set and
{\mathscr{C}}(-,-)
denotes the space of continuous maps equipped with compact open topology (note that
{\mathscr{C}}(S\times G^{n-1},V)
, since
S\times G^{n-1}
is compact, is a
{\bf Q}_{p}
-Banach space).
We also have

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},\underline{V}):n\mapsto\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}[{G}^{n-1}],\underline{V});

\displaystyle\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}[{G}^{n-1}],\underline{V})(S)={{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}[{S}]\otimes{\bf Z}[{G}^{n-1}],\underline{V})\simeq{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}[{S\times G^{n-1}}],\underline{V}).

Since
{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}[{S\times G^{n-1}}],\underline{V})\simeq{\mathscr{C}}(S\times G^{n-1},{V})
, we get claim (1) of the lemma.

Claim (2) follows from the fact that the complex of continuous cochains representing
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({G},V)
is a complex of Banach spaces and
[
29
, Prop. 3.5]
.
∎

3.1.2.
Poitou-Tate duality

Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\bf Q}_{p}
and let
V
be a continuous, finite rank
{\bf Q}_{p}
-representation of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
.
Recall that the Galois pairing

H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},V)\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},V^{*}(1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cup}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{K}}{\bf Q}_{p}

is a perfect pairing (by Poitou-Tate duality). Hence, for
i\in{\bf N}
,

H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},V)
and
H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},V^{*}(1))

are natural duals (via the above pairing). In particular, we have

\displaystyle H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Q}_{p}&{\text{if $j=0$,}}\\
0&{\text{otherwise;}}\end{cases}\quad H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Q}_{p}&{\text{if $j=1$,}}\\
0&{\text{otherwise.}}\end{cases}

3.2.
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules and Galois cohomology

In the next two sections,
we will briefly recall and refine the relationship between
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules and Galois cohomology.

3.2.1.
Notations

If
n\geq 1
, let
F_{n}={\bf Q}_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{p^{n}})
and
let
F_{\infty}:=\cup_{n}F_{n}
be the cyclotomic extension of
{\bf Q}_{p}
.
Let
\chi:{\mathscr{G}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}\to{\bf Z}_{p}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
be the cyclotomic character.
Then
\chi
factors through
\Gamma:={\rm Gal}(F_{\infty}/{\bf Q}_{p})
and induces
an isomorphism
\chi:\Gamma\overset{\sim}{\to}{\bf Z}_{p}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
.

If
\Delta
is the torsion subgroup of
\Gamma
,
then
\chi^{|\Delta|}
takes
values in
1+p{\bf Z}_{p}
(resp.
1+8{\bf Z}_{2}
) if
p\neq 2
(resp.
p=2
).
Let
\tau:{\mathscr{G}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}\to{\bf Z}_{p}
be defined by

\tau=\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{p\,|\Delta|}\log\chi^{|\Delta|}&{\text{if $p\neq 2$,}}\\
\tfrac{1}{4\,|\Delta|}\log\chi^{|\Delta|}&{\text{if $p=2$.}}\end{cases}\quad{\text{i.e., }}\tau=\tfrac{1}{p^{c(p)}}\log\chi,\ {\rm with}\ {c(p)}=\begin{cases}1&{\text{if $p\neq 2$,}}\\
2&{\text{if $p=2$.}}\end{cases}

Let
F_{\infty}^{\prime}:=F_{\infty}^{\Delta}
be the cylotomic
{\bf Z}_{p}
-extension of
{\bf Q}_{p}
.
Then
\tau
factors through
\Gamma^{\prime}:={\rm Gal}(F_{\infty}^{\prime}/{\bf Q}_{p})
and induces
an isomorphism
\tau:\Gamma^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}{\bf Z}_{p}
.

Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\bf Q}_{p}
. If
n\in{\bf N}
, let
K_{n}=K({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{p^{n}})
and
let
K_{\infty}:=\cup_{n}K_{n}
be the cyclotomic extension of
K
.
Let
\Gamma_{K}:={\rm Gal}(K_{\infty}/K)
; then
\chi
induces an isomorphism
from
\Gamma_{K}
to an open subgroup of
{\bf Z}_{p}^{\boldsymbol{*}}
. Let
\Delta_{K}
be
the torsion subgroup of
\Gamma_{K}
, let
K^{\prime}_{\infty}:=K_{\infty}^{\Delta_{K}}

be the cylotomic
{\bf Z}_{p}
-extension of
K
. Then
\Gamma_{K}^{\prime}:={\rm Gal}(K_{\infty}^{\prime}/K)=\Gamma_{K}/\Delta_{K}
and
\tau
induces
an isomorphism
\tau:\Gamma_{K}^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}p^{n(K)}{\bf Z}_{p}
for some
n(K)\in{\bf N}
.

Let
\gamma_{K}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{K}
be the element verifying
\tau(\gamma_{K})=p^{n(K)}
.
Then
\gamma_{K}
has a unique lifting in

\Gamma_{K}
whose image by
\chi
belongs to
1+p^{n(K)+c(p)}{\bf Z}_{p}
; we denote this lifting
also by
\gamma_{K}
. Then
\Gamma_{K}=\gamma_{K}^{{\bf Z}_{p}}\times\Delta_{K}
.

Let
L=K({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{p^{c(p)}})
. Then
{\rm Gal}(L/K)=\Delta_{K}
and
\Gamma_{L}=\gamma_{K}^{{\bf Z}_{p}}
.

Remark 3.4
.

(i)
Let
F:=K\cap F_{\infty}
. Then
[K_{\infty}:F_{\infty}]=[K:F]

and
\Gamma_{F}=\Gamma_{K}=\Delta_{K}\times p^{n(K)}{\bf Z}_{p}
;
hence
[F:{\bf Q}_{p}]=p^{n(K)}\frac{|\Delta|}{|\Delta_{K}|}
and

[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]=[K_{\infty}:F_{\infty}]\cdot p^{n(K)}\frac{|\Delta|}{|\Delta_{K}|}
.

(ii) We have
\tau(\gamma_{K})=p^{n(K)}
,
hence
\log\chi(\gamma_{K})=p^{n(K)+c(p)}
.

For
0<u\leq v\in v(K_{\infty}^{\flat})
, let

{\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}:=(W({\mathscr{O}}_{K_{\infty}}^{\flat})[\tfrac{p}{[\alpha]},\tfrac{[\beta]}{p}]^{\wedge_{p}})[\tfrac{1}{p}],\quad v(\alpha)=\tfrac{1}{v},\ v(\beta)=\tfrac{1}{u}.

It is a Banach space over
{\bf Q}_{p}
. Let
\varphi
denote the Frobenius morphism acting on
{\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}
and
\psi
its left inverse. Let
U^{[u,v]}
be the corresponding open set of the Fargues-Fontaine curve over

K_{\infty}
.
Take
u=\frac{p-1}{p}
,
v=p-1
if
p\neq 2
, and
u=\frac{2}{3}
,
v=\frac{4}{3}
if
p=2
.
Then
{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}/t=\widehat{K}_{\infty}
and
t
is a unit
in
18
18

18

That is, the intersection of
U^{[u,v]}
with the orbit under

\varphi
of the point
\infty
of the
Fargues-Fontaine curve is reduced to
\{\infty\}
and
U^{[u,v/p]}
does not intersect this orbit.

{\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{K_{\infty}}
. We write
\theta
the canonical map
{\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}\to\widehat{K}_{\infty}
with
{\rm Ker}(\theta)=t{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}
.

3.2.2.
Galois cohomology

For
j\in{\bf Z}
, the theory of
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules yields quasi-isomorphisms

(3.5)

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}:{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{L},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}:{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))^{\Delta_{K}}\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),

where the
(\varphi,\gamma)
-Koszul complexes (of Banach spaces over
{\bf Q}_{p}
) are defined by:

\displaystyle{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))

\displaystyle:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 27.63347pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-27.63347pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big(({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.03796pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(\varphi-1,\gamma_{K}-1)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.31259pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 70.31259pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{({\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))\oplus({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 179.03304pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-(\gamma_{K}-1)+(\varphi-1)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 228.71742pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 228.71742pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{({\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))\big)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

and the complex
{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))^{\Delta_{K}}

is obtained by taking fixed points under
\Delta_{K}
of each of the terms of the complex.

Set
[\Delta_{K}]:=\sum_{\sigma\in\Delta_{K}}\sigma
.
The following commutative diagram allows to deduce results for
K
from results for
L
,
i.e. we can often assume that
\Delta_{K}=1
in the proofs,

\textstyle{{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{[\Delta_{K}]}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{L},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm cor}_{L}^{K}}
\textstyle{{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))^{\Delta_{K}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\rm id}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm res}_{K}^{L}}

Remark 3.6
.

If
n
is big enough so that
K_{n}
has enough roots of unity in the sense of
[
16
]
,
there exist normalized trace maps
{\rm Res}_{p^{-n}{\bf Z}_{p}}:{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}\to{\bf B}_{K_{n}}^{[u,v]}
where
{\bf B}_{K_{n}}^{[u,v]}:=\varphi^{-n}({\bf B}_{K}^{[u/p^{n},v/p^{n}]})

which commute with
\Gamma_{K}
and verify
\varphi\circ{\rm Res}_{p^{-n-1}{\bf Z}_{p}}={\rm Res}_{p^{-n}{\bf Z}_{p}}\circ\varphi
.
These decompletion maps play a big role in the proofs because

{\bf B}_{K_{n}}^{[u,v]}
is a much nicer ring than
{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}
(it is
a ring of analytic functions on an annulus, whereas the later is a ring of analytic functions
on a perfectoid annulus).

Applying
{\rm Res}_{p^{-n}{\bf Z}_{p}}
to each of the terms of the above complex produces
a quasi-isomorphic complex with rings related to
K_{n}
instead of
K_{\infty}
,
which is closer to the complexes used in the theory of
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules,
like in Herr’s thesis
[
23
]
or in
[
6
]
.
Going from these rings to the usual rings uses
the standard techniques of
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules, i.e.,
normalized trace
{\rm Res}_{p^{-n}{\bf Z}_{p}}
,
bijectivity of
\gamma_{K}-1
on
({\bf B}^{[u/p^{n},v/p^{n}]}_{K}(j))^{\psi=0}
, etc., as in
[
16
]
.

3.2.3.
Examples

Denote by

(3.7)

h^{i}_{K}:Z^{i}({\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))^{\Delta_{K}})\to H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

the map
induced by quasi-isomorphism (
3.5
); it factorizes as

h^{i}_{K}:Z^{i}({\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))^{\Delta_{K}})\to H^{i}({\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j))^{\Delta_{K}})\simeq H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

If
(a,b)\in Z^{1}({\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j))^{\Delta_{K}})

and
u\in{\bf B}_{\overline{K}}^{[u,v]}(j)

satisfy
(\varphi-1)u=a
, then

(3.8)

h^{1}_{K}(a,b)={\rm cl}\big(\sigma\mapsto\tfrac{\sigma-1}{\gamma_{K}-1}b-(\sigma-1)u\big).

(In this expression,
\sigma
acts through its image in
\Gamma^{\prime}_{K}
on
b
,
and we think of
\tfrac{\sigma-1}{\gamma_{K}-1}
as an element of
{\bf Z}_{p}[[\Gamma^{\prime}_{K}]]
;
the expression between parenthesis
is a
1
-cocycle on
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
with values in
{\bf Q}_{p}(j)
,
and
{\rm cl}
denotes its image in
H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
.)

\bullet

The case
j=0
.
We can apply the formula (
3.8
) to
j=0
and cocycle
(1,0)
. Then
the corresponding
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-cocycle factors through

{\rm Gal}(\overline{\bf F}_{p}/k_{K})
since the solution
of
(\varphi-1)u=1
belongs to
W(\overline{\bf F}_{p})
,
and sends the relative Frobenius to
f:=f(K/{\bf Q}_{p})

(because
(\sigma-1)u
is equal to
(\varphi^{f}-1)u=f
since
\varphi(u)=u+1
).
Since the relative Frobenius corresponds to a uniformizer of
K^{\boldsymbol{*}}
, it follows that
the element

\lambda
of
{\rm Hom}(K^{\boldsymbol{*}},{\bf Q}_{p})
which corresponds to
h^{1}_{K}(1,0)

is
f(K/{\bf Q}_{p})v_{K}=v_{p}\circ{\rm N}_{K/{\bf Q}_{p}}

(where
v_{K}
is the valuation on
K
with image
{\bf Z}
).

Starting with
(0,p^{n(K)+c(p)})
, formula (
3.8
) with
u=0
yields a group
morphism
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}\to{\bf Z}_{p}
which factors through
\Gamma^{\prime}_{K}
and has value
\log\chi(\gamma_{K})

at
\gamma_{K}
; it follows that this morphism is
\log\chi
.

\bullet

The case
j=1
.
Let
n=n(K)+c(p)
.
We have
(\varphi-1)\tfrac{1}{\pi}\in({\bf B}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}^{(0,v]})^{\psi=0}

and there exists
a\in({\bf B}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}^{(0,v/p^{n}]})^{\psi=0}\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi}
such that

(\gamma_{K}-1)a=((\varphi-1)\tfrac{1}{\pi})\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi}
.
Then,

(\varphi^{-n}(a),\varphi^{-n}(\tfrac{1}{\pi}\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi}))\in Z^{1}({\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}_{F_{n}}^{[u,v]}(1)))\quad{\rm and}\quad h^{1}_{F_{n}}(\varphi^{-n}(a),\varphi^{-n}(\tfrac{1}{\pi}\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi}))={\rm cl}(\zeta_{p^{n}}-1).

(This follows from point iii) of
[
6
, Prop. V.3.2]
, using the constructions
leading to
[
6
, th. II.1.3]
and the (obvious) fact that Coleman power series
attached to
(\zeta_{p^{n}}-1)_{n\geq 1}
is just
T
.)

3.2.4.
Cup-products

We define compatible cup products (
\alpha\in{\bf Q}_{p}
)

\displaystyle\cup_{\alpha}:\quad{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j_{1}))^{\Delta_{K}}

\displaystyle\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j_{2}))^{\Delta_{K}}\to{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}(j_{1}+j_{2}))^{\Delta_{K}}

using (twice) the formulas from Section
7.3.2
.

Lemma 3.9
.

On the level of cohomology, the quasi-isomorphism (
3.5
) is compatible with products.

Proof.

This is easy to check for the pairing of
0
- and
2
-cocycles.
For the pairing of two
1
-cocycles,
this amounts to checking that

(3.10)

h^{1}_{K}(a,b)\cup h^{1}_{K}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})=h^{2}_{K}(b\otimes\gamma_{K}(a^{\prime})-a\otimes\varphi(b^{\prime})).

(For the product on the Koszul complexes, we
used here (twice) the formulas from Section
7.3.2
with
\alpha=1
.)
But equality (
3.10
) is standard and was checked by Herr and Benois
in
[
23
,
3
]
.
∎

3.3.
Residues and duality

3.3.1.
The trace map

We will describe the trace isomorphism

{\rm Tr}_{K}:H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\overset{\sim}{\to}{\bf Q}_{p}

in terms of
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules (cf.
[
23
]
,
[
3
]
).
For this, we start with the description of
{\rm Tr}_{K}
given by local class field theory.
We have isomorphisms

{\rm cl}:{\rm Hom}(K^{\boldsymbol{*}},{\bf Q}_{p})\overset{\sim}{\to}H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p})\quad{\rm and}\quad{\bf Q}_{p}\widehat{\otimes}K^{\boldsymbol{*}}\overset{\sim}{\to}H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))

and
{\rm Tr}_{K}
is given by the formula

{\rm Tr}_{K}({\rm cl}(\tau)\cup{\rm cl}(\alpha))=\tau(\alpha),\quad{\text{ if
$\tau\in{\rm Hom}(K^{\boldsymbol{*}},{\bf Q}_{p})$ and $\alpha\in K^{\boldsymbol{*}}$.}}

3.3.2.
Trace map and residues

Let
\pi:=[\varepsilon]-1\in{\bf B}_{F_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}
. We have
\gamma(\pi)=(1+\pi)^{\chi(\gamma)}-1

if
\gamma\in\Gamma
, and we make
\varphi
and
\Gamma
act on

\frac{d\pi}{1+\pi}
by
\varphi(\frac{d\pi}{1+\pi})=\frac{d\pi}{1+\pi}

and
\gamma(\frac{d\pi}{1+\pi})=\chi(\gamma)\frac{d\pi}{1+\pi}
, respectively (this allows us to identify

\Lambda(1)
with
\Lambda\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi}
).
Then there exists a unique continuous linear map

{\rm res}_{\pi}:{\bf B}_{F_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi}\to{\bf Q}_{p}

which commutes with
\varphi
and
\Gamma
, and sends
\sum_{k\in{\bf Z}}a_{k}\pi^{k}\,d\pi
to
a_{-1}
.
(See
[
13
, Prop. IV.3.3]
for the existence of
{\rm res}_{\pi}
.)

Set

{\rm Tr}_{K_{\infty}/F_{\infty}}:=\sum\nolimits_{\sigma\in G_{F_{\infty}}/G_{K_{\infty}}}\sigma

(well defined on modules on which
{\mathscr{G}}_{K_{\infty}}
acts trivially).

Proposition 3.11
.

We have

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{K}\circ h^{2}_{K}=\tfrac{1}{|\Delta_{K}|}\,{\rm res}_{\pi}\circ{\rm Tr}_{K_{\infty}/F_{\infty}}\quad{\rm on}\ ({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v/p]}\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi})^{\Delta_{K}}

Proof.

If
\alpha={\rm res}_{\pi}\circ{\rm Tr}_{K_{\infty}/F_{\infty}}
, then

\alpha(\varphi(x))=\alpha(x)
and
\alpha(\sigma(x))=\alpha(x)
,
for all
\sigma\in{\mathscr{G}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}
.
In particular,
\alpha
factors through

H^{2}({\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(1)))
, which is
of dimension
1
over
{\bf Q}_{p}
.
This proves the result up to multiplication by a constant
and to show that this constant is

1
, it suffices to show that the two terms coincide on an element on which one of the two is nonzero.

Moreover,
we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v/p]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{2}_{L}}
\scriptstyle{[\Delta_{K}]}
\textstyle{H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{L},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm cor}_{L/K}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{L}}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v/p]})^{\Delta_{K}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{2}_{K}}
\textstyle{H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{K}}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}}

Hence
{\rm Tr}_{L}\circ h^{2}_{L}=|\Delta_{K}|\,{\rm Tr}_{K}\circ h^{2}_{K}
on

({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v/p]})^{\Delta_{K}}
.
It follows that the result holds for
K
if and only if it holds for
L
,
and we can assume that
\Delta_{K}=1
, i.e.
K=K_{n}
, with

n=n(K)+c(p)
.

Let
\lambda=v_{p}\circ{\rm N}_{K/{\bf Q}_{p}}
as in the case
j=0
in section
3.2.3
.
It follows, using the identity
{\rm N}_{F_{n}/{\bf Q}_{p}}(\zeta_{p^{n}}-1)=p

(resp.
=-2
if
p=2
), that

\displaystyle{\rm cl}(\lambda)\cup{\rm cl}(\zeta_{p^{n}}-1)

\displaystyle=v_{p}({\rm N}_{K/{\bf Q}_{p}}(\zeta_{p^{n}}-1))

\displaystyle=[K_{n}:F_{n}]\,v_{p}({\rm N}_{F_{n}/{\bf Q}_{p}}(\zeta_{p^{n}}-1))=[K_{n}:F_{n}]=[K_{\infty}:F_{\infty}]

But formula (
3.10
) gives us

{\rm cl}(\lambda)\cup{\rm cl}(\zeta_{p^{n}}-1)={\rm Tr}_{K}\circ h^{2}_{K}\big(\varphi^{1-n}\big(\tfrac{1}{\pi}\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi}\big)\big)

Since
{\rm Tr}_{K_{\infty}/F_{\infty}}
and
x\mapsto{\rm res}_{\pi}x\frac{d\pi}{1+\pi}

commute
with
\varphi
and
\Gamma_{K}
, we have

{\rm res}_{\pi}\circ{\rm Tr}_{K_{\infty}/F_{\infty}}\big(\varphi^{1-n}\big(\tfrac{1}{\pi}\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi}\big)\big)=[K_{\infty}:F_{\infty}]\,{\rm res}_{\pi}(\tfrac{1}{\pi}\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi}\big)=[K_{\infty}:F_{\infty}]

Our proposition follows.
∎

3.3.3.
An alternative description of the trace map

Let

{\rm Tr}:\overline{\bf Q}_{p}\to{\bf Q}_{p}

be the unique

{\mathscr{G}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}
-equivariant projection
(if
[L:{\bf Q}_{p}]<\infty
, the restriction of
{\rm Tr}
to
L
coincides with

\frac{1}{[L:{\bf Q}_{p}]}{\rm Tr}_{L/{\bf Q}_{p}}
). Then
{\rm Tr}
extends by continuity
to
\widehat{K}_{\infty}

(normalized Tate trace; it does not extend to
{\bf C}_{p}
),
and
{\rm Tr}\circ\theta
gives a well defined map
{\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}\to{\bf Q}_{p}
.

Proposition 3.12
.

If
\alpha\in{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}
,

{\rm Tr}_{K}\circ h^{2}_{K}((\varphi-1)\tfrac{\alpha}{t}\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi})=-\tfrac{[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}{\rm Tr}\circ\theta(\alpha)

Proof.

Denote by

\delta_{K}:{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}\to{\bf Q}_{p}

the map

\alpha\mapsto\delta_{K}(\alpha):={\rm Tr}_{K}\circ h^{2}_{K}((\varphi-1)\tfrac{\alpha}{t}\otimes\tfrac{d\pi}{1+\pi})
.
Then

\delta_{K}
is identically
0
on
{\rm Ker}\,\theta=t{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}

(because
(\varphi-1)\tfrac{\alpha}{t}
is then a coboundary),
hence it factors through
{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}/t=\widehat{K}_{\infty}
. It commutes
with the action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}
(i.e.,
\delta_{\sigma(K)}\circ\sigma=\delta_{K}
, for all
\sigma\in{\mathscr{G}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}
). So there exists
c(K)
such that

\delta_{K}=c(K){\rm Tr}\circ\theta
.

To determine
c(K)
, it is enough to compute the value
of the term on the left-hand side for
\alpha=1
,
which can be done using Proposition
3.11
.
We have
(\varphi-1)\frac{1}{t}=\frac{1-p}{p\,t}
,
and
{\rm Tr}_{K_{\infty}/F_{\infty}}
is multiplication
by
[K_{\infty}:F_{\infty}]
on
{\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{F_{\infty}}
(which contains
\frac{1}{t}
).
Finally,
\frac{1}{t}\frac{d\pi}{1+\pi}=(\frac{1}{\pi}+\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}\pi^{n})\,d\pi
in

{\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}\otimes\frac{d\pi}{1+\pi}
,
and
{\rm res}_{\pi}(\frac{1}{t}\frac{d\pi}{1+\pi})=1
.

We get
c(K)=\frac{1-p}{p\,|\Delta_{K}|}\,[K_{\infty}:F_{\infty}]
, and
we use (i) and (ii) of Remark
3.4
to get

c(K)=-\tfrac{[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}
which concludes the proof.
∎

3.4.
Tate’s formulas

We will present now a generalization of Tate’s computations of the Galois cohomology of
C
.

3.4.1.
Classical Tate’s formulas

We will often use the following well-known isomorphisms:

(3.13)

\displaystyle H^{i}({{\mathscr{G}}}_{{K}},{C}(j))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\begin{cases}{K}&{\text{if $j=0$ and $i=0,1,$}}\\
0&{\text{otherwise.}}\end{cases}

For
i=0
the above isomorphism is given by the canonical map
{K}\to H^{0}({{\mathscr{G}}}_{K},{C})
; for
i=1
– by the
{K}
-linear map sending
1
to the
1
-cocycle
\log\chi
, where
\chi
is the cyclotomic character.

Remark 3.14
.

The following rescaling of the map from (
3.13
) will be useful later. Consider the composition

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}:{\rm Kos}_{\gamma}({{K}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}{\rm Kos}_{\gamma}({\widehat{K}}_{\infty})\xleftarrow[\sim]{\lambda}C(\Gamma^{\prime}_{K},\widehat{K}^{\prime}_{\infty})\xrightarrow[\sim]{}C(\Gamma_{K},\widehat{K}_{\infty})\xrightarrow[\sim]{}C({\mathscr{G}}_{K},C),

where the last three complexes are complexes of nonhomogeneous condensed
cochains
19
19

19

Nonhomogeneous version of (
3.2
).
, and we have

{\rm Kos}_{\gamma}({\widehat{K}}_{\infty})=[{\widehat{K}}^{\prime}_{\infty}\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\gamma_{K}-1}}{\widehat{K}}^{\prime}_{\infty}],\quad{\rm Kos}_{\gamma}({K}):=[{K}\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\gamma_{K}-1}}{K}]=[{K}\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{0}}{K}].

Map
\lambda
is given by the identity in degree
0
, evaluation on
\gamma_{K}
in degree
1
, and
0
in higher degrees.
On cohomology level
\alpha_{0}
yields: for
i=0
, the canonical map
{K}\to H^{0}({{\mathscr{G}}}_{K},{C})
; for
i=1
, the
{K}
-linear map sending
1
to the
1
-cocycle

\frac{\log\chi}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}
.

3.4.2.
Generalized Tate’s formulas

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
W\in C_{K}
be a Banach space, nuclear Fréchet, or a space of compact type equipped with a trivial action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
. Then we have isomorphisms:

(3.16)
