\displaystyle H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},W(j){\otimes}^{\Box}_{K}C)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\begin{cases}W&{\text{if $j=0$ and $i=0,1$},}\\
0&{\text{otherwise.}}\end{cases}

For
j=0
, the maps are defined in the obvious way in the case
i=0

and as the cup product with the class of
\frac{\log\chi}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}
in
H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},C)
in the case
i=1
.

Proof.

Assume first that
W
is a Banach space. We will use the fact that,
if
X
is profinite and
Y
is a Banach space,
all continuous functions from
X
to
Y^{0}/p^{n}
(where
Y^{0}
is the unit ball of
Y
)
are locally constant. We can apply this to
X={\mathscr{G}}_{K}\times\cdots\times{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
and use original Tate’s arguments to deduce that
the inflation map

H^{i}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{K},\widehat{K}^{\prime}_{\infty}\otimes^{\Box}_{K}W)\to H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},C\otimes^{\Box}_{K}W)
is an isomorphism.
Then, we use the fact that
\widehat{K}^{\prime}_{\infty}=K\oplus R

with
\gamma_{K}-1
invertible with a continuous inverse on

R
. It follows that
H^{i}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{K},R\otimes^{\Box}_{K}W)=0
, for all
i
, and
that the canonical map
H^{i}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{K},W)\to H^{i}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{K},\widehat{K}^{\prime}_{\infty}\otimes^{\Box}_{K}W)

is an isomorphism.
We have proved our proposition in the Banach case.

Assume now that
W
is a nuclear Fréchet space. Write
W\simeq\lim_{n}W_{n}
, for a projective system
\{W_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
, of Banach spaces with compact transition maps and such that the projections
p_{s}:\lim_{n}W_{n}\to W_{s}
,
s\in{\bf N}
, have dense images (see
[
30
, Ch. 16]
for why this is possible). We have

(3.17)

\displaystyle H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},W(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\lim_{n}W_{n}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C)\simeq H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\lim_{n}(W_{n}(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}(W_{n}(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\lim_{n}H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},W_{n}(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C).

The third isomorphism follows from the fact that the pro-system
\{W_{n}(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
is Mittag-Leffler (it is a pro-system of
C
-Banach spaces with projection maps
p_{s}{\otimes}{\rm Id}
having dense images).
The fourth isomorphism follows from the fact that
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},W_{n}(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C)=0
since the pro-system
\{H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},W_{n}(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C)\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
is Mittag-Leffler: by (
3.13
), this system is or trivial or isomorphic to the pro-system
\{W_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
, with projection maps
p_{s}
having dense images maps.

Having the topological isomorphisms (
3.17
), by (
3.13
) we have,
in the nontrivial cases, topological isomorphisms

\lim_{n}H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},W_{n}(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\lim_{n}W_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}W,

as wanted.

Finally, assume that
W
is of compact type. We write
W\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{n}W_{n}
, for an inductive system
\{W_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
, of Banach spaces with injective, compact transition maps. Then

(3.18)

\displaystyle H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},W(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\operatorname{colim}_{n}W_{n}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C)\simeq H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\operatorname{colim}_{n}(W_{n}(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\operatorname{colim}_{n}H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},W_{n}(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C).

The third isomorphism follows from the fact that
{\bf Z}[{\mathscr{G}}_{K}]
is a compact object in
{\rm CondAb}
.

Having the isomorphisms (
3.18
), by (
3.13
) we have in the nontrivial cases isomorphisms

\operatorname{colim}_{n}H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},W_{n}(j)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}C)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\operatorname{colim}_{n}W_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}W,

as wanted.
∎

4.
Pro-étale cohomology

In this chapter, we study the properties of pro-étale cohomology of smooth dagger curves over
K
. Moreover we assume that the curve is either proper, or Stein, or a dagger affinoid.

4.1.
Topology on
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology

Let
X
be a rigid analytic variety over
K
or
C
.

4.1.1.
The condensed approach

Definition 4.1
.

(1)

We define the pro-étale site of
X
as

X_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}:=X^{\Diamond}_{\operatorname{qpro\acute{e}t}},

where
X^{\Diamond}
is the diamond associated to
X
and
X^{\Diamond}_{\operatorname{qpro\acute{e}t}}
denotes the quasi-pro-étale site of
X^{\Diamond}

[
32
, Def. 14.1]
.

(2)

For a sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}
on
X_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}
with values in
{\mathscr{D}}({\rm CondAb})
, the pro-étale cohomology complex

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\Box}(X,{\mathscr{F}})\in{\mathscr{D}}({\rm CondAb}).

This is because the category
{\rm CondAb}
is closed under all limits and colimits (see Section
2.2.1
).
The pro-étale cohomology groups
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\Box}(X,{\mathscr{F}})
, for
i\geq 0
, are objects of
{\rm CondAb}
. Similarly, for a sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}
with values in
{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Mod}^{\rm cond}_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
.

If a sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}
in Definition
4.1
has values in
{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Solid})
then
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\Box}(X,{\mathscr{F}})
has values in
{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Solid})
as well because the category
{\rm Solid}
is closed under all limits and colimits (see Section
2.2.2
). Similarly, for the category
{\rm Mod}^{\rm solid}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}
.

4.1.2.
Comparison with the classical approach

Recall that
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\in{\mathscr{D}}(C^{\rm Hcg}_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
. Locally it is a complex of Banach spaces over
{\bf Q}_{p}
; globally – of Fréchet spaces over
{\bf Q}_{p}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

(1)

We have a natural quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\rm Mod}^{\operatorname{cond}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}})

{\rm CD}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}))\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\Box}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}).

(2)

We have a natural isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\Box}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})(*)_{\rm top}\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}).

(3)

We have a natural quasi-isomorphism in
{\rm Mod}^{\operatorname{cond}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}

{\rm CD}(\widetilde{H}^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}))\simeq H^{i}_{\Box}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}).

Proof.

Claims (1) and (2) follow from the fact that
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})
is represented locally by Galois cohomology of the fundamental group and we have Lemma
3.3
. Claim (3) follows from claim (1) and Section
2.2.3
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

By the same arguments, the analog of Lemma
4.2
holds for de Rham cohomology (de Rham complex) as well as for Hyodo-Kato cohomology (see
[
17
, Sec. 4.2]
for the definition of the latter).

4.2.
Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence

We record here the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence for pro-étale cohomology.

Lemma 4.4
.

(Bosco,
[
5
, Prop. 4.12]
)

Let
X
be a rigid analytic variety over
K
. There is a natural Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence

(4.5)

E^{a,b}_{2}=H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b}_{\Box}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\Rightarrow H^{a+b}_{\Box}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

Proof.

We pass to the world of diamonds.
Since
X_{C}\to X
is a
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-torsor, we have isomorphisms

(4.6)

(X_{C}/X)^{n}\simeq X_{C}\times{\mathscr{G}}_{K}^{n-1},\quad n\geq 1.

It suffices thus to show that, for any adic space
Y
over
{\rm Spa}(K,{\mathscr{O}}_{K})
and any profinite set
S
,
we have a natural quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\rm CondAb})
(take
Y=X_{C}
and
S={\mathscr{G}}_{K}^{n-1}
in the notation from (
4.6
))

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(Y\times S,{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}_{\Box}[S],{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(Y,{\bf Q}_{p})).

This is local on
Y
, hence we may assume that

Y
is a w-contractible space over
{\rm Spa}(K,{\mathscr{O}}_{K})
.
Then we have the following natural quasi-isomorphisms

(4.7)

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(Y\times S,{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq\underline{{\bf Q}_{p}(Y\times S)}\simeq\underline{{\mathscr{C}}(S,{\bf Q}_{p}(Y))}\simeq\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}[S],\underline{{\bf Q}_{p}(Y)})\simeq\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}_{\Box}[S],\underline{{\bf Q}_{p}(Y)}).

To see the first quasi-isomorphism note that, for any profinite set
T
, we have

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(Y\times T,{\bf Z}/p^{n})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(Y\times T,{\bf Z}/p^{n})\simeq{\bf Z}/p^{n}(Y\times T),

where the last quasi-isomorphism holds because
Y\times T
is a strictly totally disconnected perfectoid space (by
[
32
, Lemma 7.19]
), and the pro-system
\{{\bf Z}/p^{n}(Y\times T)\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
is Mittag-Leffler. The second quasi-isomorphism in (
4.7
) follows from the fact that, for any profinite set
S^{\prime}
,

{\mathscr{C}}(S^{\prime},{\mathscr{C}}(S,{\bf Q}_{p}(Y)))\simeq{\mathscr{C}}(S^{\prime}\times S,{\bf Q}_{p}(Y)).

Now, since
{\bf Z}_{\Box}[S]
is an internally projective object in Solid, the right-hand side of (
4.7
)
identifies with

{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}_{\Box}[S],\underline{{\bf Q}_{p}(Y)})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}({\bf Z}_{\Box}[S],{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(Y,{\bf Q}_{p})),

as wanted.
∎

4.3.
Cohomology of Stein curves

We will now discuss arithmetic and geometric pro-étale cohomology of smooth Stein curves.

4.3.1.
Geometric cohomology of Stein varieties

We start with geometric pro-étale cohomology.
Let
X
be a smooth Stein variety over
K
, geometrically irreducible. By
[
18
, Th. 5.14]
,

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\in{\mathscr{D}}(C_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
has classical cohomology. Moreover,
H^{i}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
, for
i\geq 0
, is Fréchet and we have a Galois equivariant strict map of strictly exact sequences of Fréchet spaces

(4.8)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega^{i-1}(X_{C})/\ker d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(i))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{(H^{i}_{\rm HK}(X_{C})\widehat{\otimes}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rm HK}\otimes\theta}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega^{i-1}(X_{C})/\ker d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\Omega^{i}(X_{C})^{d=0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Warning:
These spaces are not nuclear Fréchet over
{\bf Q}_{p}
(
C
is not a nuclear Banach space over
{\bf Q}_{p}
because it is not finite dimensional over
{\bf Q}_{p}
) !

Similarly, in the condensed language, we have the following:

Lemma 4.9
.

The cohomology
H^{i}_{\Box}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
, for
i\geq 0
, is Fréchet and we have a Galois equivariant map of exact sequences of Fréchet spaces

(4.10)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega^{i-1}(X_{C})/\ker d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{\Box}^{i}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(i))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{(H^{i}_{{\rm HK},\Box}(X_{C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\rm HK}\otimes\theta}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega^{i-1}(X_{C})/\ker d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\Omega^{i}(X_{C})^{d=0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR},\Box}(X_{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Proof.

We apply the functor
{\rm CD}(-)
to the diagram (
4.8
). Since all the spaces in that diagram are Fréchet, by Lemma
2.18
, we obtain a map of exact sequences. By Lemma
4.2
and Remark
4.3
, it remains to show that

{\rm CD}((H^{i}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{C})\widehat{\otimes}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{i}})\simeq(H^{i}_{{\rm HK},\Box}(X_{C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{i}}.

Or, since the functor
{\rm CD}(-)
is left exact, that

{\rm CD}(H^{i}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{C})\widehat{\otimes}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})\simeq(H^{i}_{{\rm HK},\Box}(X_{C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}}).

But, since
H^{i}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{C})\simeq\lim_{n}H^{i}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{n,C})
, where
H^{i}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{n,C})
are of finite rank over
\breve{F}
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm CD}(H^{i}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{C})\widehat{\otimes}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm CD}(\lim_{n}H^{i}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{n,C})\widehat{\otimes}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})\simeq\lim_{n}{\rm CD}(H^{i}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{n,C})\widehat{\otimes}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})

\displaystyle\simeq\lim_{n}({\rm CD}(H^{i}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{n,C})){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})\simeq\lim_{n}(H^{i}_{{\rm HK},\Box}(X_{n,C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})

\displaystyle\simeq(H^{i}_{{\rm HK},\Box}(X_{C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}}),

The second isomorphism follows from the fact that the functor
{\rm CD}(-)
commutes with limits; the third one – from the fact that
H^{i}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{n,C})
is of finite rank. The last one – from the fact that
\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}}
is Banach and
H^{i}_{{\rm HK},\Box}(X_{C})
is a product of spaces of finite rank.
∎

Notation:
From now on we will omit the
\Box
in
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\Box}(-,-)
and other cohomologies. This should not cause confusion.

4.3.2.
Arithmetic cohomology of Stein curves

We pass now to arithmetic pro-étale cohomology.
Let
X
be a smooth Stein curve over
K
, geometrically irreducible.
We will look at its arithmetic pro-étale cohomology complex

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\in{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box}).

Theorem 4.11
.

(1)

The cohomology of
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),j\in{\bf Z},
is nuclear Fréchet.

(2)

Let
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
be a strictly increasing open covering of
X
by Stein varieties and let
i,j\in{\bf Z}
. Then
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}H^{i}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))=0
. Hence we have an isomorphism

H^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\lim_{n}H^{i}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

Proof.

Claim (1).
By (
4.5
), we have the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence

(4.12)

E^{a,b}_{2}=H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\Rightarrow H^{a+b}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

From diagram (
4.10
), we know that
the only nontrivial cohomology groups of
X_{C}
are in degrees 0,1. Hence, from the spectral sequence (
4.12
), we get that
H^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))=0
, for
i\geq 4
, and we have the long exact sequence

(4.13)

\displaystyle 0\to

\displaystyle H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{0}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{1}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{2}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle H^{3}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{3}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0

(i)
The groups
H^{0}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
and
H^{3}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
.

Diagram (
4.13
) yields the isomorphisms

(4.14)

\displaystyle H^{0}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))={\bf Q}_{p},

\displaystyle H^{3}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))).

The top line of diagram (
4.10
) gives the exact sequence

0\to{\mathscr{O}}(X_{C})/C\to H^{1}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{C},1)\to 0

Applying Galois cohomology to it we get the exact sequence (we set
s:j-1
)

(4.15)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},({\mathscr{O}}(X_{C})/C)(s))\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))(s))\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{C},1)(s))

\displaystyle\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},({\mathscr{O}}(X_{C})/C)(s))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))(s))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{C},1)(s))

\displaystyle\to H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},({\mathscr{O}}(X_{C})/C)(s))\to H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))(s))\to H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{C},1)(s))\to 0.

Using it, the isomorphisms (
4.14
), and the generalized Tate’s formulas (
3.16
), we get the isomorphism

(4.16)

H^{3}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{C},1)(s)).

(ii)
Key lemma.
Claim (1) of Theorem
4.11
follows from the following fact.

Let
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
be a strictly increasing covering of
X
by adapted naive interiors of affinoids, i.e., there exists a strictly increasing (Stein) covering
\{\overline{X}_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
of
X
such that
X_{n+1}
is a naive interior in
\overline{X}_{n+1}
adapted to
X_{n}
.

Remark 4.17
.

By definition, a
naive interior
of a smooth (dagger) affinoid is a Stein subvariety whose complement is open and quasi-compact. It is easy to see that, for a pair of (dagger) affinoids

X_{1}\Subset X_{2}
there exists a naive interior
X_{2}^{0}\subset X_{2}
such that
X_{1}\subset X_{2}^{0}\subset X_{2}
. We will say that
X_{2}
is
adapted
to
X_{1}
.

Lemma 4.18
.

The transition maps

(4.19)

f_{i,n}:\quad H^{i}(X_{n+1},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),\quad n\geq 0,

are compact maps of (nuclear) Fréchet spaces.

Proof.

By the computations in (i), which can be applied to each
X_{n}
since that variety is Stein, this is clear for
i=0
.

For
i=3
, the isomorphism (
4.16
) above combined with the fact that
{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{n,C},1)
is
an almost
C
-representation (because
H^{1}_{\rm HK}(X_{n,C})
is of finite rank over
\breve{F}
since
H^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{n,C})
is of finite rank over
C
) yields that
H^{3}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is a finite rank
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space. Hence the maps
f_{3,n}
are as wanted.

It remains to treat the cases of
i=1,2
.
We start with showing that the spaces

H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))),\quad a,b\in{\bf Z},

appearing the spectral sequence (
4.12
) are nuclear Fréchet. To see that, we apply Galois cohomology to the top row of diagram (
4.10
) for
X_{n}
and obtain the exact sequence (we set
s:=j-b
;
{\rm HK}^{j}(X_{n,C},i):=(H^{j}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{n,C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{i}}
)

(4.20)

\displaystyle\to H^{a-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},

\displaystyle{\rm HK}^{b}(X_{n,C},b)(s))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{a-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n,C})/\ker d)(s))

\displaystyle\to H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{b}(X_{n,C},b)(s))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{a}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{a+1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n,C})/\ker d)(s))\to

We claim that
the spaces
H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n,C})/\ker d)(s))
and
H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{b}(X_{n,C},b)(s))
are nuclear Fréchet. Indeed, for the first one this follows from the generalized Tate’s isomorphism (
3.16
):
if nontrivial

H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n,C})/\ker d)(s))\simeq\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n})/\ker d

since
\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n,C})/\ker d\simeq(\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n})/\ker d)\otimes_{K}^{\Box}C
;
and the fact that
\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n})/\ker d
is a nuclear Fréchet.
For the second one, we use the fact that
H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{b}(X_{n,C},b)(s))
is a finite rank
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space by the isomorphism (
3.16
) (since
{\rm HK}^{b}(X_{n,C},b)
is an almost
C
-representation).

The above computations imply that
the maps
\partial_{a-1}
and
\partial_{a}
in (
4.20
) are between nuclear Fréchet spaces hence
H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))
is an extension of two nuclear Fréchet spaces. In fact, it is an extension of a finite rank
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space by a nuclear Fréchet space hence a nuclear Fréchet space.

We proceed now to prove Lemma
4.18
for
i=1,2
. From (
4.13
), we get a long exact sequence

(4.21)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{1}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2,n}}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{2}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0

(a)
Case
j\neq 1
.
In this case, we have
H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))=0
. Thus, it suffices to show that the spaces
H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))
, for
i=0,1
, are of finite rank over
{\bf Q}_{p}
. For that, since
the vector spaces
H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{n,C},1)(j-1))
, for
i=0,1
, are of finite rank over
{\bf Q}_{p}
, it suffices to notice that
H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},({\mathscr{O}}(X_{n,C})/C)(j-1))=0
, for
i=0,1
, by the generalized Tate’s formulas (
3.16
).

(b)
Case
j=1
.
To start, we claim that the spaces
H^{i}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))
,
i=1,2
are nuclear Fréchet. Indeed, we have shown above that the spaces
H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))
are Fréchet. We also know that we have exact sequences

0\to V_{0,i,n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{i,n}}}{{\to}}V_{1,i,n}\to H^{i}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to 0,

with
V_{0,i,n},V_{1,i,n}
solid Fréchet
K
-vector spaces. Moreover,
H^{i}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is quasi-separated since it is an extension of quasi-separated solid
K
-vector spaces (see (
4.21
)). It follows that the map
g_{i,n}
is quasi-compact and, hence, the induced map

g_{i,n}:V_{0,i,n}(*)_{\rm top}\to V_{1,i,n}(*)_{\rm top}
is a closed embedding. As a result,
V_{1,i,n}(*)_{\rm top}/V_{0,i,n}(*)_{\rm top}
is a (classical) Fréchet space and then this implies that
H^{i}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq{\rm CD}(V_{1,i,n}(*)_{\rm top}/V_{0,i,n}(*)_{\rm top})
is Fréchet, as wanted. By Lemma
2.5
, as an extension of a nuclear Fréchet space by a finite rank vector space, it is nuclear.

We will show below (in fact (c)) that the pro-systems
\{H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
have compact transition maps (we will call such systems
compact
). Then the pro-system
\{\ker d_{2,n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
is also compact. And, by Lemma
2.7
, the maps
f_{i,n}
,
i=1,2
, from our theorem are compact, as wanted.

(c)
The pro-systems
\{H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))(s))\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
, for
i=0,1
, are compact.

To prove that, note that
the vector spaces
H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{n,C},1)(s))
, for
i=0,1,
are of finite rank over
{\bf Q}_{p}
. Hence, by (
4.15
), it suffices to show that the pro-systems

\{H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},({\mathscr{O}}(X_{n,C})/C)(s))\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
are compact. But this is clear since, by the generalized Tate’s formulas (
3.16
) (note that
{\mathscr{O}}(X_{n})/K
is a nuclear Fréchet), these pro-systems are or trivial or we have isomorphisms

\{H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},({\mathscr{O}}(X_{n,C})/C)(s))\}_{n\in{\bf N}}\simeq\{{\mathscr{O}}(X_{n})/K\}_{n\in{\bf N}}.\qed

This finishes the proof of claim (1) of the theorem.

Claim (2).
It suffices to show that

H^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\lim_{n}H^{i}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

Or that
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}H^{i}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))=0
,
i,j\in{\bf Z}
. Since the spectral sequence (
4.12
) degenerates at
E_{3}
it suffices to show that
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}E^{a,b}_{3}(X_{n})=0
. Since cohomological dimension of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
is
2
, we have

E_{3}^{a,b}(X_{n})=\begin{cases}\ker d_{2}^{0,b}(X_{n})&\mbox{ if }a=0,\\
E_{2}^{1,b}(X_{n})&\mbox{ if }a=1,\\
\operatorname{coker}d_{2}^{2,b+1}(X_{n})&\mbox{ if }a=2,\end{cases}

where
d_{2}^{0,b}(X_{n}):H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b-1}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))
is the only nontrivial differential in the spectral sequence (
4.12
). Hence, using the computations from claim (1), we get
immediately that
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}E^{1,b}_{3}(X_{n})=0
and, since

{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b-1}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))=0,

that
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}E^{2,b}_{3}(X_{n})=0
. It remains to show that

{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}E^{0,b}_{3}(X_{n})={\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}\ker d_{2}^{0,b}(X_{n})=0.

From the exact sequence (
4.20
), since
H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{b}(X_{n,C},b)(s))
is finite over
{\bf Q}_{p}
(because
{\rm HK}^{b}(X_{n,C},b)(s)
is an almost
C
-representation), it suffices to show that
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n,C})/\ker d)=0
. But, by the generalized Tate’s isomorphism (
3.16
),

H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n,C})/\ker d)\simeq\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n})/\ker d
, so it suffices to show that
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}\Omega^{b-1}(X_{n})=0
but this is known.
∎

4.4.
Filtration

Let
X
be a smooth geometrically irreducible Stein analytic curve over
K
.
Let
i,j\in{\bf Z}
. Under certain conditions, there exists an ascending filtration on
H^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
:

F^{2}_{i,j}=H^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\supset F^{1}_{i,j}\supset F^{0}_{i,j}\supset F^{-1}_{i,j}=0,

such that we have isomorphisms

\displaystyle F^{2}_{i,j}/F^{1}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)),

\displaystyle F^{1}_{i,j}/F^{0}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(X_{C})}{C}(j-1)),

\displaystyle F^{0}_{i,j}/F^{-1}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{C},1)(j-1)),

where we set
{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{C},1)=(H^{1}_{{\rm HK}}(X_{C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p}
.
We can visualize this filtration in the following way:

(4.22)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{i,j}:=H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{1}_{i,j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(X_{C})}{C}(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{2}_{i,j}:=H^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{C},1)(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}(X_{C},1)(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

The above diagram is a map of exact sequences. The right column is induced by the syntomic filtration (see diagram (
4.10
)).
The middle row comes from
the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence (
4.5
) (we note that
{\bf Q}_{p}(j)\simeq H^{0}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
) and the vanishing of geometric cohomologies obtained from diagram (
4.10
). We assume that the middle row and the right column are exact.
The term
F^{1}_{i,j}
is defined as a pullback of the top right square.

4.5.
Arithmetic cohomology of dagger affinoids

Let
X
be a smooth geometrically connected dagger affinoid over
K
. We will now study its arithmetic pro-étale cohomology. They key tool is the (studied above) arithmetic pro-étale cohomology of smooth Stein curves.

Proposition 4.23
.

The cohomology of
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),j\in{\bf Z},
is of compact type.

Proof.

Let
\{X_{h}\}
be the dagger presentation of the dagger structure on
X
. Denote by
X_{h}^{0}
a naive interior of
X_{h}
adapted to
\{X_{h}\}
. The canonical quasi-isomorphism

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{h}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),\quad j\in{\bf Z}

yields an isomorphism

H^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{colim}_{h}H^{i}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),\quad i,j\in{\bf Z}.

We note that
X^{0}_{h}
,
h\in{\bf N}
, is a smooth Stein variety. By Lemma
4.18
, the ind-systems
\{H^{i}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\}_{h\in{\bf N}}
, for
i\in{\bf N}
, have compact transition maps (between Fréchet spaces).
This proves our proposition.
∎

4.6.
Examples

In this section we will compute
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology of open discs,
annuli, and their boundaries – the basic building blocks of analytic curves.

4.6.1.
Open disc

Let
D
be an open disc over
K
.

Lemma 4.24
.

(Geometric cohomology)
Let
j\in{\bf Z}
.
We have
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant isomorphisms

(4.25)

H^{i}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Q}_{p}(j)&\mbox{ if }i=0,\\
({\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})/C)(j-1)&\mbox{ if }i=1,\\
0&\mbox{ if }i\geq 2.\end{cases}

Moreover,
d:{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})/C{\to}\Omega^{1}(D_{C})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The Hyodo-Kato isomorphism
\iota_{\rm HK}:H^{i}_{\rm HK}(D_{C}){\otimes}_{\breve{F}}^{{\Box}}C\simeq H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(D_{C})
and the fact that

H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(D_{C})=0
, for
i\geq 1
, yield
that
H^{i}_{\rm HK}(D_{C})=0
, for
i\geq 1
.
Now, since
D
is Stein, our lemma follows from diagram (
4.10
).

The last claim is equivalent to
H^{1}_{\rm dR}(D_{C})=0
.
∎

Consider now the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence (from Lemma
4.4
):

(4.26)

E^{a,b}_{2}=H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\Rightarrow H^{a+b}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

By Lemma
4.24
, the only nonzero rows are those of degrees
b=0,1
. We get:

Lemma 4.27
.

(Arithmetic cohomology)
Let
i\geq 0,j\in{\bf Z}
.
We have exact sequences

\displaystyle 0\to H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},({\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})/C)(j-1))\to 0.

Proof.

The second exact sequence is a translation of the first, granted formula (
4.25
).
The spectral sequence (
4.26
) yields the exact sequence

(4.28)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{0}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle{\to}H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{1}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{2}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0

First, we prove that it splits into short exact sequences. If
j\neq 1
, this is trivial as the last terms of the first two lines are
0
.

Let us now assume that
j=1
. We need to show that
the differential
d_{2}
in (
4.28
) is
0
or, equivalently,
that the canonical map

H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{2}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is injective. But this map is induced by the projection
D\to K
and any rational point in
D
yields a section (such a point always exists).
∎

Remark 4.29
.

We note that the groups
H^{i}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
and
H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are Fréchet and nuclear Fréchet spaces, by Lemma
4.8
and Theorem
4.11
, respectively.

4.6.2.
Open annulus

Let
A
be an open annulus over
K
.

Lemma 4.30
.

(Geometric cohomology)
Let
j\in{\bf Z}
.

(1)

We have
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant isomorphisms

(4.31)

H^{i}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Q}_{p}(j)&\mbox{ if }i=0,\\
0&\mbox{ if }i\geq 2.\end{cases}

(2)

We have a
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant exact sequence

(4.32)

0\to({\mathscr{O}}(A_{C})/C)(j-1)\to H^{1}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)\to 0

admitting a
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant
{\bf Q}_{p}
-linear splitting.

Proof.

Since
A
is Stein we can use
the Galois equivariant map of strictly exact sequences (
4.8
) for
X=A
.
From the Hyodo-Kato isomorphism
\iota_{\rm HK}:H^{i}_{\rm HK}(A_{C}){\otimes}_{\breve{F}}^{{\Box}}C\simeq H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(A_{C})
and the fact that
H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(A_{C})\simeq C
, for
i=0,1
, and
H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(A_{C})=0
, for
i\geq 2
, we see that

H^{0}_{\rm HK}(A_{C})\simeq\breve{F},\quad H^{1}_{\rm HK}(A_{C})\simeq\breve{F},\quad H^{i}_{\rm HK}(A_{C})\simeq 0,\,i\geq 2.

The group
H^{1}_{\rm HK}(A_{C})
is generated by the Hyodo-Kato symbol
c_{1}^{\rm HK}(z)
, for an arithmetic coordinate
z
of the annulus. Frobenius acts on
c_{1}^{\rm HK}(z)
by multiplication by
p
and monodromy is trivial. This implies that

(H^{i}_{\rm HK}(A_{C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{i}}\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Q}_{p}&\mbox{if $i=0,1,$}\\
0&\mbox{if $i\geq 2$.}\end{cases}

This yields the isomorphisms in our lemma.

Assume now that
i=1
. Then the diagram (
4.8
) becomes

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{O}}(A_{C})/C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{can}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{O}}(A_{C})/C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}(A_{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

The top row yields the exact sequence (
4.32
). The term
{\bf Q}_{p}
comes from a Hyodo-Kato term and is generated by the Hyodo-Kato symbol
c_{1}^{\rm HK}(z)
. The term
C
comes from de Rham cohomology and is generated by the de Rham symbol
c_{1}^{\rm dR}(z)
. These symbols are compatible with each other (via the Hyodo-Kato isomorphism
\iota_{\rm HK}
) and are also compatible with the pro-étale symbol
c_{1}^{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(z)
. Sending
c_{1}^{\rm HK}(z)
to
c_{1}^{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(z)
yields the wanted splitting of the exact sequence (
4.32
).
∎

Take now the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence:

(4.33)

E^{a,b}_{2}=H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\Rightarrow H^{a+b}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

By (
4.31
), the only nonzero rows are those of degrees
b=0,1
. We get:

Lemma 4.34
.

(Arithmetic cohomology)

We have exact sequences

\displaystyle 0\to H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},({\mathscr{O}}(A_{C})/C)(j-1))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))\to 0.

Proof.

The second sequence is obtained from the (split) exact sequence (
4.32
).

For the rest, we argue exactly as in the case of an open disc (see the proof of
Lemma
4.27
) with the exception of the triviality of the map
d_{2}
in the exact sequence
4.28
when
j=1
: in this case, a rational point in
A
does not always exist but it does after taking
a base change to a finite extension
L
of
K
. Then the map
H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{L},H^{0}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{2}(A_{L},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is injective and the triviality of
d_{2}
follows.
∎

Remark 4.35
.

We note that the groups
H^{i}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
and
H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are Fréchet and nuclear Fréchet spaces, by Lemma
4.8
and Theorem
4.11
, respectively.

4.6.3.
Ghost circle

Take now the ghost circle
Y:=\partial D
.

Lemma 4.36
.

(Geometric cohomology)

Let
i\in{\bf N}
,
j\in{\bf Z}
.

(1)

We have
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant isomorphisms

H^{i}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Q}_{p}(j)&\mbox{ if }i=0,\\
0&\mbox{ if }i\geq 2.\end{cases}

(2)

We have a
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant (split) exact sequence

(4.37)

0\to({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})/C)(j-1)\to H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)\to 0.

Proof.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon<1}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(D_{C}\mathbin{\fgebackslash}D_{C}(\varepsilon),{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon<1}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(A_{C}(\varepsilon),{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),

where
D_{C}(\varepsilon)
is the closed disc of radius
\varepsilon
(over
C
) and
A_{C}(\varepsilon):=D_{C}\mathbin{\fgebackslash}D_{C}(\varepsilon)
.

Applying Lemma
4.30
, we get immediately that
{H}^{i}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))=0
, for
i\geq 2
, and

{H}^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon<1}{H}^{1}(A_{C}(\varepsilon),{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

From the exact sequence (
4.32
), we get the exact sequence

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon<1}({\mathscr{O}}(A_{C}(\varepsilon)/C)(j-1))\to\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon<1}{H}^{1}(A_{C}(\varepsilon),{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon<1}{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)\to 0.

Since
\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon<1}({\mathscr{O}}(A_{C}(\varepsilon)/C)(j-1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})/C
, we get that
{H}^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
fits into the exact sequence (
4.37
).

From Lemma
4.30
,
we also get the isomorphism

\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon<1}{H}^{0}(A_{C}(\varepsilon),{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{H}^{0}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

From it and the exact sequence (
4.32
), we get the isomorphism

{\bf Q}_{p}(j)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{H}^{0}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.38
.

(Arithmetic cohomology)
Let
i\in{\bf N},j\in{\bf Z}
.
We have exact sequences

\displaystyle 0\to H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})/C)(j-1))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))\to 0.

Proof.

The second exact sequence is obtained from the (split) exact sequence from Lemma
4.36
.

For the first exact sequence, we write

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon_{K}<1}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(D\mathbin{\fgebackslash}D(\varepsilon_{K}),{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon_{K}<1}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(A(\varepsilon_{K}),{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),

where
\varepsilon_{K}
are chosen so that the annuli
A(\varepsilon_{K})
are defined over
K
. By Lemma
4.34
, this yields the exact sequence

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon_{K}<1}H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(A(\varepsilon_{K})_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon_{K}<1}H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(A(\varepsilon_{K})_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0.

Hence it suffices to show that

(4.39)

\displaystyle\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon_{K}<1}H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(A(\varepsilon_{K})_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))),

\displaystyle\operatorname{colim}_{0<\varepsilon_{K}<1}H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(A(\varepsilon_{K})_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))).

But this is clear since
{\bf Z}[{\mathscr{G}}_{K}]
is a compact object in
{\rm CondAb}
.
∎

4.6.4.
Boundary of an open annulus

Let
A
be an open annulus over
K
.

Corollary 4.40
.

(Geometric cohomology)

Let
i\in{\bf N},j\in{\bf Z}
.
There is a
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant canonical isomorphism

H^{i}(\partial A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{i}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))^{\oplus 2}.

Hence we have
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant isomorphisms

H^{i}(\partial A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Q}_{p}(j)^{\oplus 2}&\mbox{if }i=0,\\
0&\mbox{if }i\geq 2,\end{cases}

and a
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant (split) exact sequence:

0\to({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})/C)(j-1)^{\oplus 2}\to H^{1}(\partial A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)^{\oplus 2}\to 0

Proof.

We write
A=\{z\in K\;:\;|a|<|z|<|b|\}

with
a,b\in K
. Then

\partial A\simeq\varinjlim_{|a|<\delta\leq\varepsilon<|b|}A\mathbin{\fgebackslash}A(\delta,\varepsilon)=Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{b},

where
A(\delta,\varepsilon):=\{z\in K\;:\;\delta\leq|z|\leq\varepsilon\}
and
Y_{a},Y_{b}
are the two ghost circles at the boundary of
A
. Our corollary now follows from Lemma
4.36
.
∎

Corollary 4.41
.

(Arithmetic cohomology)

We have a canonical isomorphism

H^{i}(\partial A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))^{\oplus 2}

and exact sequences

\displaystyle 0\to H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(\partial A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{i}(\partial A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(\partial A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A_{C})}{C^{\oplus 2}}(j-1))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(\partial A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}^{\oplus 2}(j-1))\to 0.

Proof.

Since
\partial A
is a disjoint union of two ghost circles, this follows immediately from Lemma
4.38
.
∎

Remark 4.42
.

The arithmetic pro-étale cohomology
H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
and
H^{i}(\partial A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are direct sums of nuclear Fréchet spaces and spaces of compact type (both over
{\bf Q}_{p}
) (see Proposition
7.25
for an explicit splitting).

5.
Pro-étale cohomology with compact support

In this chapter we will study properties of compactly supported pro-étale cohomology of smooth dagger curves.

5.1.
Compactly supported cohomology

We start with briefly reviewing the definition of pro-étale cohomology with compact support from
[
1
]
.

5.1.1.
Partially proper varieties

Let
X
be a smooth partially proper rigid analytic variety over
K,C
. We define its
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology with compact support by:

(5.1)

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)):=[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))]\in{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p}),\quad r\geq 0,

with

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)):=\operatorname{colim}_{Z}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X\mathbin{\fgebackslash}Z,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\in{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p}),

where the colimit is taken over admissible quasi-compact opens
Z\subset X
.
From the definition, we get a distinguished triangle

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)).

By
[
1
, Sec. 2.1]
,
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))
is a cosheaf for the analytic topology on
X
.

If
X
is a proper variety then the definition (
5.1
) yields that we have the canonical isomorphism

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))

and the cohomology groups of
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are classical: they are finite dimensional vector spaces over
{\bf Q}_{p}
equipped with their canonical Hausdorff topology. By Lemma
4.2
so is the cohomology of the complex
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\Box}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
(which can be identified with the cohomology of
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
via the functor
{\rm CD}
).

Remark 5.2
.

The de Rham and Hyodo-Kato cohomologies with compact support
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X)
and
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{{\rm HK},c}(X)
can be defined in an analogous way (see
[
7
]
).

5.1.2.
Dagger affinoids

Let
X
be a smooth dagger affinoid over
K,C
with a presentation
\{X_{h}\}
. We set

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)):={\mathrm{R}}\lim_{h}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\in{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box}),

where
X^{0}_{h}
denotes a naive interior
20
20

20

See Remark
4.17
for a discussion.
of
X_{h}
adapted to the presentation
\{X_{h}\}
. This definition is independent of the interiors chosen. Alternatively, we can set

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\widehat{X}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\in{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box}).

This is independent of
h
.

5.2.
Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence

We record the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence for pro-étale cohomology with compact support.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
X
be a smooth partially proper variety over
K
. There is a spectral sequence

(5.4)

E^{a,b}_{2}=H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\Rightarrow H^{a+b}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

Proof.

By definition (see (
5.1
)), we have

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle=[{\rm R}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\rm R}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p})],

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{colim}_{Z}{\rm R}\Gamma(X\mathbin{\fgebackslash}Z,{\bf Q}_{p}),

where the colimit is taken over admissible quasi-compact opens
Z
in
X
. This yields
natural quasi-isomorphisms

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle=[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p})]

\displaystyle\simeq[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}))\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}))]

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{K},[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})])

\displaystyle={\rm R}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm R}\Gamma_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})).

The second quasi-isomorphism needs a justification. The quasi-isomorphism involving
X
follows from Lemma
4.4
. For
\partial X
we have quasi-isomorphisms

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{colim}_{Z}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X\mathbin{\fgebackslash}Z,{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{Z}{\rm R}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma((X\mathbin{\fgebackslash}Z)_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}))

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\operatorname{colim}_{Z}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma((X\mathbin{\fgebackslash}Z)_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}))\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})).

The second quasi-isomorphism follows from Lemma
4.4
; the third one from the fact that
{\bf Z}[{\mathscr{G}}_{K}^{i}]
is a compact object in
{\rm CondAb}
.
∎

5.3.
Compactly supported cohomology of Stein curves

We turn now to the study of compactly supported pro-étale cohomology of smooth Stein curves.

5.3.1.
Geometric compactly supported cohomology

We will briefly review here computations of geometric compactly supported cohomology from
[
1
, Sec. 8.2]
.

Let
X
be a geometrically connected smooth Stein curve over
K
. Its geometric compactly supported pro-étale cohomology

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\in{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})
is studied in loc. cit. by using
a Galois equivariant exact sequence (
i\geq 0
)

\textstyle{H^{i-1}{\rm HK}_{c}(X_{C},i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}{\rm DR}_{c}(X_{C},i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(i))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}{\rm HK}_{c}(X_{C},i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}{\rm DR}_{c}(X_{C},i),}

where we set

\displaystyle{\rm HK}_{c}(X_{C},i){\rm DR}_{c}(X_{C},i)

\displaystyle:=({\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X){\otimes}^{{\rm L}\Box}_{K}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{i}

\displaystyle\simeq\big(H^{1}_{c}(X,{\mathscr{O}}_{X}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/F^{i})\to H^{1}_{c}(X,\Omega^{1}_{X}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/F^{i-1})\big)[-1].

This sequence is obtained from a comparison theorem between pro-étale cohomology and syntomic cohomology. It yields the following facts:

Lemma 5.5
.

We have

(1)

vanishings:
H^{i}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})=0
for
i\neq 1,2
.

(2)

an isomorphism:

(5.6)

H^{1}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\xrightarrow{\sim}(H^{1}_{{\rm HK},c}(X_{C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=1}.

(3)

an exact sequence:

(5.7)

\textstyle{H^{1}{\rm HK}_{c}(X_{C},2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}{\rm DR}_{c}(X_{C},2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

The above map
{\rm Tr}_{X_{C}}:H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\to{\bf Q}_{p}(1)
is
the geometric trace map
.

5.3.2.
Arithmetic compactly supported cohomology

Let
X
be a geometrically connected smooth Stein curve over
K
. We will now look at its arithmetic compactly supported pro-étale cohomology complex

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\in{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box}).

Theorem 5.8
.

The cohomology of
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is of compact type.

Proof.

The only nontrivial geometric cohomology groups are in degrees 1,2 hence, from the spectral sequence (
5.4
), we get that
H^{0}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))=0
and we have the long exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to

\displaystyle H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{1}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{2}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{3}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle H^{3}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{4}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0

It follows that

\displaystyle H^{0}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))=0,

\displaystyle H^{1}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))),

\displaystyle H^{4}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))).

Hence, by (
5.6
),

H^{1}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}_{c}(X_{C},1)(j-1)),

where we set
{\rm HK}^{1}_{c}(X_{C},1):=H^{1}{\rm HK}_{c}(X_{C},1)
. It follows that
H^{1}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is a colimit of finite rank
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces hence of compact type.
By
[
1
, Sec. 8.3]
, we have that

H^{4}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Q}_{p}&\mbox{ if }j=2,\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

It remains to treat
H^{i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
, for
i=2,3
. We have a long exact sequence

(5.9)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{2}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{3}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0

(
\bullet
)
Case
j\neq 2
.
We claim that in this case
H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))=0
. Indeed, we have

H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\simeq H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}_{c}(X_{C},1)(j-1)).

Now the slopes of Frobenius on
H^{1}_{{\rm HK},c}(X_{C})
are between
0
and
1
,
which implies that, in the case this group is of finite rank,
{\rm HK}^{1}_{c}(X_{C},1)
is an extension of
an unramified finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-representation
V
of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}

by the
C
-points
W
of a connected BC. Since we have

H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},W(j-1))=0
, for any
j
, and
H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},V(j-1))=0
, for
j\neq 2
, this implies
H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}_{c}(X_{C},1)(j-1))=0
, as wanted. The general case is obtained by writing
H^{1}_{{\rm HK},c}(X_{C})
as a colimit of groups of finite rank.

(i)
First sequence.
Let us now look at the first exact sequence from (
5.9
).

(5.10)

0\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{2}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0

Let
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
be a strictly increasing open covering of
X
by adapted naive interiors of dagger affinoids. We have an isomorphism

\operatorname{colim}_{n}H^{i}_{c}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),\quad i,j\in{\bf Z}.

We have analogs of sequence (
5.10
) for
X_{n}
’s.

It suffices to prove the following result.

Lemma 5.11
.

The transition maps
f_{2,n}:H^{2}_{c}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{2}_{c}(X_{n+1},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are compact maps between spaces of compact type.

Proof.

Consider the canonical transition map

\displaystyle f_{1,n}:H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{n+1,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))).

We have

H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\simeq H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}_{c}(X_{n,C},1)(j-1)).

Hence it is of finite rank over
{\bf Q}_{p}
. This implies, by
Lemma
2.5
, Lemma
2.7
, and the exact sequence (
5.10
), that it suffices to show that
the canonical transition map

\displaystyle f_{3,n}:H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{n+1,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

is a compact map of spaces of compact type.

By (
5.7
), we have the exact sequence, for
s=n,n+1
,

0\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\operatorname{coker}g(X_{s}))(j-2)){\to}H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{s,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(2))(j-2))\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))\to,

where
g(X_{s}):H^{1}{\rm HK}_{c}(X_{s,C},2)\to H^{1}{\rm DR}_{c}(X_{s,C},2)
is the canonical map. But

\operatorname{coker}g(X_{s})\simeq\operatorname{coker}((H^{1}_{{\rm HK},c}(X_{s,C}){\otimes}_{\breve{F}}^{\Box}t\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{2}}\to H^{1}_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X_{s,C}){\otimes}_{K}^{\Box}C(1)\hookrightarrow H^{1}_{c}(X_{s},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{s}}){\otimes}_{K}^{\Box}C(1)).

Now, using generalized Tate’s formulas (see (
3.16
)), we easily see that
H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\operatorname{coker}g(X_{s}))(j-2))
is of finite rank over
{\bf Q}_{p}
unless
j=1
. Hence, for
j\neq 1
, the map
f_{3,n}
is a map between finite rank vector spaces over
{\bf Q}_{p}
; hence it is compact.

Assume now that
j=1
. It suffices to show that the transition maps between the spaces
H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\operatorname{coker}g(X_{n}))(-1))
are compact and the spaces themselves are of compact type. From the definition of the map
g(X_{n})
, we get an exact sequence

(5.12)

0\to A_{n}\to H^{1}_{c}(X_{n},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{n}}){\otimes}_{K}^{\Box}C\to(\operatorname{coker}g(X_{n}))(-1)\to 0,

where
A_{n}
is an almost
C
-representation. Applying Galois cohomology to this sequence, we get the exact sequence

0\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},A_{n})\to H^{1}_{c}(X_{n},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{n}})\to H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\operatorname{coker}g(X_{n}))(-1))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},A_{n}).

Since, by generalized Tate’s theorem (see (
3.16
)),
H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},A_{n})
and
H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},A_{n})
are of finite rank over
{\bf Q}_{p}
, it suffices to show that the canonical map
f:H^{1}_{c}(X_{n},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{n}})\to H^{1}_{c}(X_{n+1},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{n+1}})
is compact and the cokernel of the map
g:H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},A_{n})\to H^{1}_{c}(X_{n},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{n}})
is of compact type.
For that, choose rigid analytic affinoids
Y_{1}
,
Y_{1}
such that

X_{n}\subset Y_{1}\Subset Y_{2}\subset X_{n+1}.

Then we have maps

(5.13)

\Omega(X_{n+1})\to\Omega(Y_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{f}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega(Y_{1})\to\Omega(X_{n}).

The spaces
\Omega(Y_{i})
,
i=1,2
, are Banach and the map
\tilde{f}
is compact. Taking the strong duals of the terms of (
5.13
) and using Serre’s duality for the end terms we get maps

H^{1}_{c}(X_{n},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{n}}){\to}\Omega(Y_{1})^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{f}^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega(Y_{2})^{*}\to H^{1}_{c}(X_{n+1},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{n+1}})

factorizing the map
f
. By
[
30
, Lemma 16.4]
, the map
\tilde{f}^{*}
is compact; by
[
30
, Remark 16.7]
, so is the map
f
, as wanted.

The statement about the cokernel of the map
g
follows from the fact that the space
H^{1}_{c}(X_{n},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{n}})
is of compact type and the
image of
g
is finite dimensional.
∎

(ii)
Second sequence.
We pass now to the second exact sequence from (
5.9
) which became the isomorphism

\displaystyle H^{3}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))).

We cover
X
with a strictly increasing system
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
of adapted naive interiors of dagger affinoids.
It suffices to show that the transition maps

f_{n}:\quad H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{{n+1},C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))

are compact maps between spaces of compact type.

By (
5.7
), we have the exact sequence

H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\operatorname{coker}g(X_{s}))(j-2)){\to}H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{s,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(2))(j-2))\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)).

Using the generalized Tate’s formulas (see (
3.16
)), we easily see that
H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\operatorname{coker}g(X_{s}))(j-2))
is of finite rank over
{\bf Q}_{p}
unless
j=1
. Hence, for
j\neq 1
, the map
f_{n}
is compact as a map between finite rank vector spaces over
{\bf Q}_{p}
.

Assume now that
j=1
. It suffices to show that the transition maps between the spaces
H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\operatorname{coker}g(X_{n}))(-1))
are maps of compact type between spaces of compact type. By generalized Tate’s theorem (see (
3.16
)), from the exact sequence (
5.12
), we get the exact sequence

\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},A_{n})\to H^{1}_{c}(X_{n},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{n}})\to H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\operatorname{coker}g(X_{n}))(-1))\to H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},A_{n})\to.

Since
H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},A_{n})
and
H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},A_{n})
are of finite rank over
{\bf Q}_{p}
, it suffices to show that the canonical map
H^{1}_{c}(X_{n},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{n}})\to H^{1}_{c}(X_{n+1},{\mathscr{O}}_{X_{n+1}})
is a compact map of spaces of compact type but this we have done above.

(
\bullet
)
Case
j=2
.
We cover
X
with a strictly increasing system
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
of adapted naive interiors of dagger affinoids. It suffices to show that all the terms in the exact sequence (
5.9
) on level
X_{n}
are of finite rank over
{\bf Q}_{p}
(this is true more generally for
j\neq 1
).
But, by (
5.7
), since
H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))=0
, we have

H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\operatorname{coker}g(X_{n}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X_{n,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(2))).

And we have shown above that this is of finite rank over
{\bf Q}_{p}
.
∎

5.4.
Arithmetic cohomology of dagger affinoids

Let
X
be a smooth geometrically connected dagger affinoid over
K
. We will now study its arithmetic pro-étale cohomology. They key tool is the (studied above) arithmetic pro-étale cohomology of smooth Stein curves.

Proposition 5.14
.

The cohomology of
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),j\in{\bf Z},
is nuclear Fréchet. Moreover,
if
\{X_{h}\}
is the dagger presentation of the dagger structure on
X
, then we have an isomorphism

H^{i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\lim_{h}H^{i}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),\quad i,j\in{\bf Z},

where
X_{h}^{0}
is a naive interior of
X_{h}
adapted to
\{X_{h}\}
.

Proof.

Let
\{X_{h}\}
be the dagger presentation of the dagger structure on
X
. Denote by
X_{h}^{0}
a naive interior of
X_{h}
adapted to
\{X_{h}\}
.
Note that
X^{0}_{h}
is a smooth and Stein rigid analytic variety over
K
.
From Section
5.3.2
, we have

\displaystyle H^{0}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))=0,

\displaystyle H^{1}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(X^{0}_{h,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\simeq H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm HK}^{1}_{c}(X^{0}_{h,C},1)(j-1)),

\displaystyle H^{4}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X^{0}_{h,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Q}_{p}&\mbox{ if }j=2,\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

These are finite rank
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces. Moreover, by Lemma
5.11
, the transition maps

H^{i}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i}_{c}(X^{0}_{h+1},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),\quad i=2,3,

are compact maps between spaces of compact type.

By
[
30
, discussion after Prop. 16.5]
, since the pro-system

\{H^{i}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\}_{h\in{\bf N}}
,
i\geq 0
, is compact, it is equivalent to a pro-system of Banach spaces with dense transition maps. Hence it is Mittag-Leffler by Section
2.2.4
and we have
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{h}H^{i}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))=0
,
i\geq 0
. It follows that

H^{i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\lim_{h}H^{i}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

and the cohomology of
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),j\in{\bf Z},
is nuclear Fréchet,
as wanted.
∎

5.5.
Examples

We will now determine the compactly supported
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology groups of an open disc and an open annulus.

5.5.1.
Open disc

We start with the cohomology
H^{i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
of an open disc
D
over
K
.
It immediately follows from Lemma
4.24
and Lemma
4.36
, and the definition of compactly supported cohomology that we have:

Lemma 5.15
.

(Geometric cohomology)

Let
i\in{\bf N},j\in{\bf Z}
. Then:

(1)

The canonical maps
H^{i}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are injective; hence we have a
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant isomorphism

H^{i}_{c}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}H^{i-1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))/H^{i-1}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

(2)

The groups
H^{i}_{c}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are
0
unless
i=2
, in which case there is a
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant (split) exact sequence:

0\to({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})/{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C}))(j-1)\to H_{c}^{2}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)\to 0.

Now we pass to the arithmetic cohomology. The canonical maps

(5.16)

H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

are injective; this follows from the descriptions of
these groups in Lemma
4.27
and Lemma
4.38
.
Hence we have an isomorphism

(5.17)

H^{i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}H^{i-1}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))/H^{i-1}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

Lemma 5.18
.

(Arithmetic Cohomology)

Let
i\in{\bf N},j\in{\bf Z}
. There is an exact sequence:

0\to H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})}(j-1))\to H^{i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))\to 0

Proof.

Consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))}
\textstyle{0}

The first two rows are exact by Lemma
4.27
and Lemma
4.38
, respectively. The middle column is exact by formula (
5.17
). We claim that the third column is exact, the map
f_{1}
is injective, and the map
f_{2}
is surjective. Indeed, by Lemma
5.15
, Lemma
4.24
, and Lemma
4.36
, it suffices to show that the canonical map

H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})}{C}(j-1))\to H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C}(j-1))

is injective. But,
by the generalized Tate’s formula (
3.16
), this map is either a map between trivial objects or is isomorphic (for
j=1
and
i=2,3
) to the canonical map
{\mathscr{O}}(D)/K\to{\mathscr{O}}(Y)/K.

And that map is clearly injective.

The above discussion shows that we have
an isomorphism:

(5.19)

H_{c}^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H_{c}^{2}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))),\quad\text{for all $i\in{\bf N},j\in{\bf Z}$.}

Now we evoke
Lemma
5.15
.
∎

5.5.2.
Open annulus

Let
A
be an open annulus over
K
.
We start with the geometric cohomology.

Lemma 5.20
.

(Geometric cohomology)

(1)

Let
i\in{\bf N},j\in{\bf Z}
. The canonical maps

H^{i}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i}(\partial A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are injective. Hence we have
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant isomorphisms

\displaystyle H^{i}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}H^{i-1}(\partial A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))/H^{i-1}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),

\displaystyle H^{i}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle=0,\quad\mbox{if }i>2.

(2)

We have
a
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariant isomorphism and a (split) exact sequence:

(5.21)

\displaystyle H^{1}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq{\bf Q}_{p}(j),

\displaystyle 0\to\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A_{C})}{{\mathscr{O}}(A_{C})\oplus C}(j-1)\to

\displaystyle H^{2}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)\to 0.

Proof.

This follows from the description of
the groups involved in (
4.31
), (
4.32
) and Corollary
4.40
.
∎

Now we pass to arithmetic cohomology. The canonical maps

H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i}(\partial A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

are injective. This follows from the description of
the groups involved in Lemma
4.34
and Corollary
4.41
.
Hence we have an isomorphism

(5.22)

H^{i}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}H^{i-1}(\partial A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))/H^{i-1}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

Lemma 5.23
.

(Arithmetic Cohomology)

Let
i\in{\bf N}
,
j\in{\bf Z}
. There is an exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{i}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to 0.

Proof.

Consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}(\partial A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(\partial A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

The first two rows are exact by Lemma
4.34
and Corollary
4.41
, respectively. The middle column is exact by formula (
5.22
). The first column is exact by the same formula and the fact that the canonical map
H^{0}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{0}(\partial A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
-equivariantly split.

It suffices now to show that the third column is exact, the map
f_{1}
is injective, and the map
f_{2}
is surjective. To see that, use Lemma
5.20
and note that,
by Lemma
4.30
and Corollary
4.40
, the map
f_{1}
can be rewritten as the canonical map

H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(A_{C})}{C}\oplus{\bf Q}_{p})(j-1))\to H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C}\oplus{\bf Q}_{p})^{\oplus 2}(j-1)),

which, by the generalized Tate’s formula (
3.16
), is either a map between trivial objects or is isomorphic (for
j=1
and
i=2,3
) to the canonical map

\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(A)}{K}\oplus{\bf Q}_{p}\to\big(\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y)}{K}\oplus{\bf Q}_{p}\big)^{\oplus 2}.

And that map is clearly injective. We note that this also proves that the map
f_{2}
is surjective, as wanted.
∎

6.
Trace maps and pairings

In this chapter we will discuss pro-étale and coherent trace maps and pairings.

6.1.
Pro-étale trace maps.

We start with pro-étale trace maps.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
X
be a smooth Stein variety, a smooth dagger affinoid, or a proper variety over
K
of dimension
1
. Assume that it is geometrically irreducible. Then

(1)

There exists a natural geometric trace map

(6.2)

{\rm Tr}_{X_{C}}:H^{2}_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to{\bf Q}_{p}.

It is an isomorphism if
X
is proper.

(2)

There exists a natural arithmetic trace map

(6.3)

{\rm Tr}_{X}:H^{4}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\to{\bf Q}_{p}.

It is an isomorphism.

(3)

The above trace maps are functorial for open immersions and compatible with the Hyodo-Kato and de Rham trace maps.

Proof.

In the case
X
is partially proper, the arithmetic trace map is defined using the geometric trace map and the Galois cohomology trace map

{\rm Tr}_{X}:H^{4}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\simeq H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2)))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\rm Tr}_{X_{C}}(1)}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\rm Tr}_{K}}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

It was shown to be an isomorphism in
[
1
, Sec. 8.3]
.

In the case
X
is a dagger affinoid, the arithmetic trace map is defined as the composition

{\rm Tr}_{X}:\quad H^{4}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\lim_{h}H^{4}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\lim_{h}{\rm Tr}_{X^{0}_{h}}}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

Here the first isomorphism follows from the fact that
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{h}H^{3}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(2))=0
, which was shown in the proof of Proposition
5.14
.
∎

We will also need a derived version of the arithmetic trace map:

{\rm Tr}_{X}:\quad{\rm R}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))[4]\to{\bf Q}_{p}.

We define it as the composition

{\rm R}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))[4]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}(\tau_{\leq 4}{\rm R}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2)))[4]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{can}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{4}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\rm Tr}_{X}}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

6.2.
Pro-étale pairings for partially proper varieties

Let
X
be a partially proper rigid anaytic variety over
K,C
. Cup product on pro-étale cohomology induces pairings

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p})

as the composition

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p})={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(\operatorname{colim}_{Z}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X\mathbin{\fgebackslash}Z,{\bf Q}_{p}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\operatorname{colim}_{Z}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X\mathbin{\fgebackslash}Z,{\bf Q}_{p}))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\operatorname{colim}_{Z}\cup}}\operatorname{colim}_{Z}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X\mathbin{\fgebackslash}Z,{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p}).

These pairings are compatible with the pairings

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}).

They yield (Galois equivariant over
C
) pairings

(6.4)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}),

which are compatible with the passage from
K
to
C
.

Let now
X
be partially proper and as in Proposition
6.1
. The (twisted) pairing (
6.4
) composed with the trace map (
6.2
) yields a geometric pairing:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1-j))[2]{\to}{\bf Q}_{p}.

Similarly, using the trace map (
6.3
), we obtain an arithmetic pairing

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4]{\to}{\bf Q}_{p}.

These pairings are compatible and are also compatible with pro-étale pairings on
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
and
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
, respectively.

6.3.
Pro-étale pairings for dagger affinoids

Let
X
be a smooth dagger affinoid over
K,C
. Cup product on pro-étale cohomology

(6.5)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})

is defined by the composition

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{colim}_{h}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}))\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\widehat{X}}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{colim}_{h,n}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\widehat{X}}(X_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{colim}_{h}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\widehat{X}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}))

\displaystyle\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\operatorname{colim}_{h}\cup}}\operatorname{colim}_{h}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\widehat{X}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{p})={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}).

Here
\{X_{h}\}_{h\in{\bf N}}
is the dagger presentation of
X
.

These pairings are Galois equivariant over
C

and compatible with the passage from
K
to
C
.

The (twisted) pairing (
6.5
) composed with the trace map (
6.2
) yields a geometric pairing:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1-j))[2]{\to}{\bf Q}_{p}.

Similarly, using the trace map (
6.3
), we obtain an arithmetic pairing

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{{\rm L}\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4]{\to}{\bf Q}_{p}.

These pairings are compatible and are also compatible
with pro-étale pairings on
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
and
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
, respectively.

6.4.
Coherent pairings

We will list now the coherent pairings that we will use.

6.4.1.
Ghost circle

Let
D
be an open disc over
K
; let
Y:=\partial D
be the boundary of
D
, a ghost circle. Let
Y_{C}:=Y\times C
.
The ring
{\mathscr{O}}(Y)
(resp.
{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})
) is the Robba ring with coefficients in
K
(resp.
C
). Topologically,
{\mathscr{O}}(Y)
is a direct sum of a nuclear Fréchet space and a space of compact type (both over
{\bf Q}_{p}
).

The map

(6.6)

(f,g)\mapsto{\rm Tr}_{K}{\rm res}(fdg)

induces a perfect pairing
21
21

21

We used here the fact that, for nuclear Fréchet spaces and spaces of compact type, the passage between locally convex topological vector spaces and solid vector spaces works well on the level of tensor products and
{\rm{Hom}}
s, see Section
2.2.5
.

(6.7)

\cup:{\mathscr{O}}(Y)/K\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathscr{O}}(Y)/K\to{\bf Q}_{p}

between the solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces
{\mathscr{O}}(Y)/K
and
{\mathscr{O}}(Y)/K
.

Similarly, for
L=K,C
, the map

(6.8)

(f,g)\mapsto{\rm res}(fdg)

induces a perfect pairing

(6.9)

\cup:{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{L})/L\otimes^{\Box}_{L}{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{L})/L\to L

between the solid
L
-vector spaces
{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{L})/L
