and
{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{L})/L
.

Remark 6.10
.

We can see one of the copies of
{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{L})/L

as the space
\Omega^{1}(Y_{L})_{0}
of differential forms with residue equal to
0

(via
h\mapsto dh
). The map
(f,\omega)\mapsto{\rm res}(f\omega)

induces a perfect duality between the
L
-spaces
{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{L})
and
\Omega^{1}(Y_{L})

and
\Omega^{1}(Y_{L})_{0}
is exactly the orthogonal of
L\subset{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{L})
.

6.4.2.
Open disc

Let
D
and
Y
be as above. We see easily that
{\mathscr{O}}(Y)/{\mathscr{O}}(D)
is the
dual of
{\mathscr{O}}(D)/K
for the pairing (
6.6
), i.e., that we have a
perfect pairing

\cup:{\mathscr{O}}(Y)/{\mathscr{O}}(D)\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathscr{O}}(D)/K\to{\bf Q}_{p}.

Similarly, we check that
{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{L})/{\mathscr{O}}(D_{L})
is the
dual of
{\mathscr{O}}(D_{L})/L
for the pairing (
6.8
), i.e., that we have a
perfect pairing

\cup:{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{L})/{\mathscr{O}}(D_{L})\otimes^{\Box}_{L}{\mathscr{O}}(D_{L})/L\to L.

This pairing can be thought of as Serre duality pairing

\displaystyle\cup:H^{1}_{c}(D_{L},{\mathscr{O}})\otimes^{\Box}_{L}H^{0}(D_{L},\Omega^{1})\to L.

This is because we have isomorphisms (the second is just
d:{\mathscr{O}}\to\Omega^{1}
)

{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{L})/{\mathscr{O}}(D_{L})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{1}_{c}(D_{L},{\mathscr{O}}),\quad{\mathscr{O}}(D_{L})/L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{0}(D_{L},\Omega^{1}).

6.4.3.
Open annulus

Let
A
be an open annulus over
K
; let
\partial A
be the boundary of
A
, a disjoint union
Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{b}
of two ghost circles.
We see easily that
{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A)/({\mathscr{O}}(A)\oplus K)
is the
dual of
{\mathscr{O}}(A)/K
for the pairing (
6.6
) taken twice and followed by the addition map
{\bf Q}_{p}^{\oplus 2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+}}{{\to}}{\bf Q}_{p}
. That is, that we have a
perfect pairing

\cup:{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A)/({\mathscr{O}}(A)\oplus K)\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathscr{O}}(A)/K\to{\bf Q}_{p}.

Similarly, we check that
{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A_{L})/({\mathscr{O}}(A_{L})\oplus L)
is the
dual of
{\mathscr{O}}(A_{L})/L
for the pairing (
6.8
), i.e., that we have a
perfect pairing

\cup:{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A_{L})/({\mathscr{O}}(A_{L})\oplus L)\otimes^{\Box}_{L}{\mathscr{O}}(A_{L})/L\to L.

This pairing can be seen as induced by the Serre duality pairing

\displaystyle\cup:H^{1}_{c}(A_{L},{\mathscr{O}})\otimes^{\Box}_{L}H^{0}(A_{L},\Omega^{1})\to L.

This is because we have an isomorphism

{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A_{L})/{\mathscr{O}}(A_{L})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{1}_{c}(A_{L},{\mathscr{O}})

and the exact sequence

0\to{\mathscr{O}}(A_{L})/L\overset{d}{\to}H^{0}(A_{L},\Omega^{1})\to L\tfrac{dz}{z}\to 0.

7.
Poincaré duality for a ghost circle

Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\bf Q}_{p}
. We will prove in this chapter arithmetic Poincaré
duality for a ghost circle
Y
over
K
that will be essential in later computations. The numerology
suggests that
Y
is a “proper” variety of total dimension
\frac{3}{2}
,
hence
Y_{C}
is of dimension
\frac{1}{2}
.

7.1.
Arithmetic duality theorem

We start with a proof that assumes an Explicit Reciprocity Law (which will be proved in the next section).

Theorem 7.1
.

(Arithmetic duality)
Let
Y
be a ghost circle over
K
.

(i)
There is a trace map isomorphism

{\rm Tr}_{Y}:\,H^{3}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

(ii)
Let
i,j\in{\bf Z}
.
The pairing

(7.2)

\cup:\quad H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}^{\Box}H^{3-i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cup}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{3}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{Y}}{\bf Q}_{p}

is a perfect duality, i.e., we have an induced isomorphism

\displaystyle\gamma_{Y,i}:\quad H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{3-i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}.

Proof.

Define geometric and arithmetic trace maps as follows:

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{Y_{C}}:H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1)){\to}{\bf Q}_{p},

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{Y}:H^{3}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(2)))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{Y_{C}}(1)}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr_{K}}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

Here, the first trace map comes from the exact sequence in (
4.37
)
and
{\rm Tr}_{Y_{C}}(1)
is an isomorphism because
H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C})=0
.
This proves (i);
let us turn to (ii).

\bullet

Filtration on cohomology.

Let
i,j\in{\bf Z}
. There exists an ascending filtration on
H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
:

(7.3)

F^{2}_{i,j}=H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\supset F^{1}_{i,j}\supset F^{0}_{i,j}\supset F^{-1}_{i,j}=0,

such that we have isomorphisms

\displaystyle F^{2}_{i,j}/F^{1}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)),

\displaystyle F^{1}_{i,j}/F^{0}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C}(j-1)),

\displaystyle F^{0}_{i,j}/F^{-1}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

This follows from Section
4.4
, Lemma
4.38
, and Lemma
4.36
.

\bullet

Identification of pairings on graded pieces.
Assume the following:

Theorem 7.4
.

(Explicit Reciprocity Law)

(i)
The pairing (
7.2
) is compatible with the above filtration.
In particular
F^{a}_{i,j}
and
F^{b}_{3-i,2-j}
are orthogonal if
a+b\leq 1
.

(ii)

On the associated grading the pairing (
7.2
)
yields a pairing induced by the Galois cohomology pairing and pairing (
6.9
).

From claim (i), we obtain the following commutative diagram with exact rows (all the vertical maps are induced from pro-étale cup products and the trace
{\rm Tr}_{Y}
):

(7.5)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C}(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{Y,i}}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C}(1-j))^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(F^{1}_{3-i,2-j})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{3-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

By Theorem
7.4
, claim (ii),
the left and the right vertical arrows are isomorphisms.
It follows that we have an isomorphism (we skipped the indices to lighten the notation)

\beta_{Y,i}:\quad(F^{2}/F^{0})H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(F^{1}H^{3-i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j)))^{*}.

Similarly, we obtain the following commutative diagram with exact rows (again, all the vertical maps are induced from pro-étale cup products and the trace
{\rm Tr}_{Y}
)

(7.6)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{Y,i}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{Y,i}}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{Y,i}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1-j))^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{3-i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(F^{1}_{3-i,2-j})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

By Theorem
7.4
, the map
\alpha_{Y,i}
is induced by the Galois pairing hence is an isomorphism. It follows that so is the map
\gamma_{Y,i}
, as wanted.
∎

7.2.
Proof of the Explicit Reciprocity Law

The goal of the rest of the chapter is to prove Theorem
7.4
. The result is immediate
for
i\geq 4
since then all the terms are
0
.

For
i=0,3
, by symmetry, we may assume that
i=0
. We have

H^{0}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Q}_{p}&\mbox{ if }j=0,\\
0&\mbox{ if }j\neq 0;\end{cases}\quad H^{3}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Q}_{p}&\mbox{ if }j=0,\\
0&\mbox{ if }j\neq 0.\end{cases}

Here, to compute,
H^{3}
we have used Lemma
4.38
and Lemma
4.36
.
Now we need to study the pairing

\cup:\quad H^{0}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}^{\Box}H^{3}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\to H^{3}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{Y}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

We have

\displaystyle F^{0}_{0,0}=F^{1}_{0,0}=F^{2}_{0,0}=H^{0}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}),

\displaystyle F^{0}_{3,2}=F^{1}_{3,2}=0,\quad F^{2}_{0,0}=H^{3}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2)).

Claim (i) of Theorem
7.4
follows immediately. Claim (ii)
is easy to check by following the isomorphisms appearing in Lemma
4.38
, Lemma
4.36
and the definition of the trace map
{\rm Tr_{Y}}
, and by compatibility of the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence with products. (The Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence for
Y
is obtained by taking
\operatorname{colim}
of the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequences for annuli (
4.33
). The fact that
\operatorname{colim}
commutes with Galois cohomology is proved as in the proof of Lemma
4.36
.)

So it remains to look at

i=1,2
and, by symmetry, we may assume that
i=1
. That is, we are studying the pairing

(7.7)

\cup:\quad H^{1}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}^{\Box}H^{2}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))\to H^{3}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{Y}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

7.3.
Descent to
\widehat{K}_{\infty}

Since
\overline{\bf Q}_{p}/K_{\infty}
is almost étale,
we can compute
the pro-étale cohomology of
Y_{K}:=Y
using
Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}}
and the latter is computed via syntomic methods as in
[
16
]
or in
[
21
]
.

7.3.1.
(\partial,\varphi,\gamma)
-Koszul complexes

Let
\widetilde{Y}^{[u,v]}=Y_{{\bf Q}_{p}}\times U^{[u,v]}
(see section
3.2.1
). Then we have

\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}:={\mathscr{O}}(\widetilde{Y}^{[u,v]})\simeq{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{{\bf Q}_{p}})\widehat{\otimes}{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}},\quad\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}/t\simeq{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}}).

The algebra
{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{{\bf Q}_{p}})
is a direct sum of a nuclear Fréchet space
and a space of compact type and
{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{K_{\infty}}
is a Banach space.
Let
j\in{\bf Z}
. Let us choose a uniformizer
T
of
{\mathscr{O}}({Y_{{\bf Q}_{p}}})
and define
Frobenius
\varphi
by sending
T
to
T^{p}
and let
\partial=T\frac{d}{dT}
. We denote by

(7.8)

{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))\quad{\rm and}\quad{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))^{\Delta_{K}}

the total complex of the double complex

(7.9)

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{(t\partial,\varphi-1)}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j-1)\oplus\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v/p]}(j)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{K}-1}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{K}-1}
\scriptstyle{(\varphi-1)-t\partial}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v/p]}(j-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{K}-1}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

of each of its terms

22

In what follows, a superscript

means taking fixed points by
\Delta_{K}

of each terms; we don’t need to take derived fixed points since

but we will not state them explicitly.
.
Since they are based on Fontaine-Messing syntomic cohomology (see Remark

.

They sometimes appear in a different form

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j-1)\oplus\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v/p]}(j-1)\oplus(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}/t)(j-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The two Koszul complexes are related by a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})

(7.11)

\beta_{j}:{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Kos}^{\rm HK}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))

given by the maps

in the top and bottom rows (same for

-fixed points).

Remark 7.12
.

(
Relation to syntomic cohomology
.)

(i) One can easily show that the horizontal complexes in (
7.9
) compute the syntomic cohomology of

Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}}
(more exactly,
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\rm syn}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}},1)(j-1)
; the
twist
(j-1)
does not play a role at the level of
K_{\infty}
but intervenes in the computation of the arithmetic cohomology).
The complex
{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))
is then given by the mapping fiber

(7.13)

[\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 47.87726pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-47.87726pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\rm syn}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}},1)(j-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.16931pt\raise 5.93611pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.57501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\gamma_{K}-1}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.87726pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 71.87726pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\rm syn}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}},1)(j-1)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces].

By applying
{\rm Res}_{p^{-n}{\bf Z}_{p}}
, for
n
big enough,
we obtain a quasi-isomorphic complex in which

{\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}
is replaced by
{\bf B}_{K_{n}}^{[u,v]}

and which looks like
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\rm syn}(Y_{K},1)(j-1)
except for the arithmetic variable
which is treated a little bit differently
(there is an action of the entire
\Gamma_{K}
and not just of its Lie algebra).

(ii) More precisely, the double complex (
7.8
) can be rewritten in the following way:

(7.14)

\textstyle{F^{1}\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\widetilde{\Omega}^{[u,v/p]}(j-1).}

We remind the reader that
F^{1}\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}=t\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}
,
and that multiplication by
t
is equivalent to a Tate twist by
(1)
.
An isomorphism from the complex (
7.8
) to (
7.14
)
can be given by the map

\left[{\begin{array}[]{cccc}a&x&y&z\\
a^{\prime}&x^{\prime}&y^{\prime}&z^{\prime}\\
\end{array}}\right]\Rightarrow\left[{\begin{array}[]{cccc}ta&x\frac{dT}{T}&ty&z\frac{dT}{T}\\
ta^{\prime}&x^{\prime}\frac{dT}{T}&ty^{\prime}&z^{\prime}\frac{dT}{T}\\
\end{array}}\right]

(iii) We have chosen to twist everything in (i) by
(1)

but one could twist by
(r)
for any
r\geq 1
.
That is, take
F^{r}
,
F^{r-1}\Omega^{1}
,
\frac{\varphi}{p^{r}}
, etc.

7.3.2.
Products on mapping fibers

We will recall here the well-known formulas for products on mapping fibers that we will need (for details, see, for example,
[
26
, Prop. 3.1]
).

Let
A_{i}^{\bullet}
,
C_{i}^{\bullet}
complexes of condensed
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces (for
i=1,2,3
) and
f_{i},g_{i}
morphisms of complexes
A_{i}^{\bullet}\to C_{i}^{\bullet}
. Assume that, for all
\alpha
in
{\bf Q}_{p}
, there are maps

\cup_{\alpha}:A^{\bullet}_{1}\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}A^{\bullet}_{2}\to A^{\bullet}_{3}\text{ and }\cup_{\alpha}:C^{\bullet}_{1}\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}C^{\bullet}_{2}\to C^{\bullet}_{3}

such that the
\cup_{\alpha}
’s are morphisms of complexes which commute with the
f_{i}
’s and
g_{i}
’s, all the
\cup_{\alpha}
’s are homotopic and we can choose the homotopies such that they commute with the
f_{i}
’s and
g_{i}
’s.

If we take the mapping fiber

D_{i}^{\bullet}:=[A_{i}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{f_{i}-g_{i}}C_{i}^{\bullet}]

and, for all
\alpha\in{\bf Q}_{p}
, the products

\cup_{\alpha}:D^{\bullet}_{1}\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}D^{\bullet}_{2}\to D^{\bullet}_{3}

can be defined (on the level of sections) by the formula

(7.15)

\gamma_{1}\cup_{\alpha}\gamma_{2}=(a_{1}\cup_{\alpha}a_{2},c_{1}\cup_{\alpha}w_{\alpha}(a_{2})+(-1)^{\deg(a_{1})}w_{1-\alpha}(a_{1})\cup_{\alpha}c_{2}),

where
(a_{i},c_{i})\in A^{\bullet}_{i}\oplus C^{\bullet-1}_{i}
represents
\gamma_{i}
, and, for
\beta\in{\mathrm{R}}
,
w_{\beta}=\beta f_{i}(a_{i})+(1-\beta)g_{i}(a_{i})
.

Then:

(1)

The
\cup_{\alpha}
’s are morphisms of complexes, which commute with the projections
D^{\bullet}_{i}\to A^{\bullet}_{i}
.

(2)

The
\cup_{\alpha}
’s are homotopic.

(3)

If
\tilde{A}^{\bullet}_{1}
,
\tilde{C}^{\bullet}_{i}
,
\tilde{f}_{i}
,
\tilde{\cup}_{\alpha}
are another set of data as above and
A_{i}^{\bullet}\to\tilde{A}_{i}^{\bullet}
,
C_{i}^{\bullet}\to\tilde{C}_{i}^{\bullet}
are morphisms of complexes which commute with
\cup_{\alpha}
and
\tilde{\cup}_{\alpha}
, then the induced morphism
D^{\bullet}_{i}\to\tilde{D}^{\bullet}_{i}
commutes with
\cup_{\alpha}
and
\tilde{\cup}_{\alpha}
defined by (
7.15
).

(4)

If
g_{i}=0
, then the products

\cup_{0},\cup_{1}:D_{1}^{\bullet}\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}D_{2}^{\bullet}\to D_{3}^{\bullet}
induce products
\tilde{\cup}_{0},\tilde{\cup}_{1}:A_{1}^{\bullet}\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}D_{2}^{\bullet}\to D_{3}^{\bullet}
such that the following diagrams are commutative:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 25.78123pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-25.25276pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A_{1}^{\bullet}\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}D_{2}^{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.7878pt\raise 7.36111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.86111pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\cup}_{0}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.78123pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 49.78123pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D_{3}^{\bullet}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-25.78123pt\raise-24.4575pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 29.35754pt\raise-15.95366pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.44446pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cup_{1}}$}}}\kern Explicitly, for
a_{i}\in A^{\bullet}_{i}
and
c_{i}\in C^{\bullet}_{i}
, we have

a_{1}\tilde{\cup}_{0}(a_{2},c_{2})=(a_{1}\cup_{0}a_{2},(-1)^{\deg(a_{1})}f_{1}(a_{1})\cup_{0}c_{2}),

(a_{2},c_{2})\tilde{\cup}_{1}a_{1}=(a_{2}\cup_{1}a_{1},c_{2}\cup_{1}f_{1}(a_{1})).

7.3.3.
Structures on
(\partial,\varphi,\gamma)
-Koszul complexes

We equip the complexes
{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))
with the following structures:

(
\bullet
)
Products.
Using (twice) the formulas from Section
7.3.2
, we define cup products (
\alpha\in{\bf Q}_{p}
)

\cup_{\alpha}:{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j_{1}))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j_{2}))\to{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j_{1}+j_{2})).

(
\bullet
)
Filtration.

We note that the operators
\partial,\gamma_{K}
do not change the exponents in the powers of
T
(and that the operator
\varphi
sends
T
to
T^{p}
). In particular, we can separate the constant term from the rest, which allows us to write

(7.16)

{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))={\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j))\oplus{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j))\oplus{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j-1))[-1].

Here the subscript
0
in
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}
in the first term denotes the series for which the constant term is
0
. The complex
{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(s))

(for
s=j,j-1
)
is the one defined in Section
3.2
and is quasi-isomorphic to
{\rm R}\Gamma({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(s))
(see (
3.5
)).

We define an ascending filtration on the complex
{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))
:

\displaystyle F^{-1}:=0,\hskip 5.69054pt

\displaystyle F^{0}:={\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j)),

\displaystyle F^{1}:={\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j))\oplus{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j)),

\displaystyle F^{2}:={\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j)).

This filtration does not depend on the choice of
T
(but the splitting does):
this is obvious for
F^{0}
and,
on the diagram (
7.14
),
F^{1}
corresponds to the kernel of the residue map
on differential forms.
We have canonical quasi-isomorphisms in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})
:

\displaystyle(F^{0}/F^{-1}){\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))\simeq{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j)),

\displaystyle(F^{1}/F^{0}){\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))\simeq{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j)),

\displaystyle(F^{2}/F^{1}){\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))\simeq{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j-1))[-1].

(
\bullet
)
Trace map.
We define the trace map

{\rm Tr_{\rm Kos}}:\quad H^{3}{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(2))\overset{\sim}{\to}{\bf Q}_{p}

as the composition

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{\rm Kos}:\quad H^{3}{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(2))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{3}\big((F^{2}/F^{1}){\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(1))\big)

\displaystyle\simeq H^{2}{\rm Kos}_{\varphi,\gamma}({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(1))\overset{\sim}{\to}{\bf Q}_{p},

where the last map is
\frac{1}{|\Delta_{K}|}{\rm res}_{\pi}\circ{\rm Tr}_{K_{\infty}/F_{\infty}}

(see Proposition
3.11
).

7.3.4.
Quasi-isomorphism with pro-étale cohomology

Recall that, if we start with the complex computing the cohomology
of the
\pi_{1}
via perfectoid methods, we get the same complex
23
23

23

More precisely, one needs to do it for each of the annuli converging to the ghost circle and then go to the limit.
(but with slightly
different period rings) as

{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))

and with
t\partial
replaced by
\tau-1
, where

\tau
is the generator of the geometric part
of
{\rm Aut}({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}}[T^{1/p^{\infty}}])/{\mathscr{O}}(Y))
(this group
is the semi-direct product of
{\bf Z}_{p}(1)
and
\Gamma_{K}
, and
\tau
is the generator
of
{\bf Z}_{p}(1)
given by our fixed choice of compatible system of roots of unity).
Passing from
\tau-1
to
t\partial

corresponds to passing from
{\bf Z}_{p}(1)
to its Lie algebra as in
[
16
]
or
[
21
]
;
change of period rings is done as in
[
16
]
or
[
21
]
.
It follows that we have a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({{\bf Q}_{p,\Box}})

(7.17)

\alpha_{j}:{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))^{\Delta_{K}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm R}\Gamma(Y_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

Remark 7.18
.

(i)
It is easy to see that the maps (
7.17
) and (
3.5
) are compatible.

(ii) The following commutative diagram
24
24

24

In which
L
has the same meaning as in
Section
3.2.1
.
makes it possible to assume
that
\Delta_{K}=1
in the proofs:

\textstyle{{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{[\Delta_{K}]}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma(Y_{L},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm cor}_{L}^{K}}
\textstyle{{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))^{\Delta_{K}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\rm id}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma(Y_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm res}_{K}^{L}}

Lemma 7.19
.

On cohomology level, the quasi-isomorphism
\alpha_{j}
from (
7.17
) is compatible with
products, filtrations, and trace maps.

Proof.

(i)
Products.
The easiest way to see this is to trace the geometric part of the quasi-isomorphism
\alpha_{j}
via the analog of the big commutative diagram used in the proof of Theorem 7.5 in
[
21
]
. We choose
r
large enough (see Remark
7.12
). The best path is via the top row (right-to-left), then all the way down, and then to the right along the bottom row. All the morphisms used along the way are clearly compatible with cup products. That treats the case of the cohomology of the geometric
\pi_{1}
.

Now we apply continuous group cohomology for the Galois group of the base field. This and the subsequent almost étale descent are clearly compatible with products. What remains is the passage from
(nonhomogeneous) continuous cochains of
<\gamma_{K}>
to the Koszul complexes for
\gamma_{K}
. But a map from the former to the latter can be given by the identity in degree
0
and the evaluation at
\gamma_{K}
in degree
1
; this is easily checked to be compatible with products.

(ii)
Filtrations.
This is easy to see for
F^{0}
: both the pro-étale
F^{0}
and the
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module
F^{0}
come from the corresponding complexes of a point (given by
K
). The required compatibility follows then easily from functoriality of the comparison map
\alpha_{j}
(see Remark
7.18
). Moreover,
\alpha_{j}
restricted to
F^{0}
is an isomorphism (on cohomology level).

It remains to check compatibility for
F^{1}
. Or, in light of the above, for
F^{1}/F^{0}
. Note that after moding out
F^{0}
from both sides of the map
\alpha_{j}
, the pro-étale
F^{1}
arises from the Galois (for the group
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
) kernel of the pro-étale residue map

{\rm res}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}:H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)

(see (
7.3
)).
Recall that this map comes from the syntomic residue map

(7.20)

{\rm res}_{\operatorname{syn}}(j-1):H^{1}_{\operatorname{syn}}(Y_{C},1)(j-1)\to{\bf B}_{\operatorname{cr}}^{+,\varphi=1}(j-1)

(See diagram (
4.8
), take
i=1
, and note that the slope of Frobenius on the Hyodo-Kato cohomology is
1
.)
By changing the period rings, the Galois cohomology of the map (
7.20
) can be seen as the residue map from diagram (
7.13
) to diagram

\textstyle{{\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{K}-1}
\scriptstyle{\varphi-1}
\textstyle{{\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v/p]}(j-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{K}-1}
\textstyle{{\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi-1}
\textstyle{{\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v/p]}(j-1),}

which is the Koszul complex representing the Galois cohomology of
{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)
shifted by
[-1]
. It is clear that the kernel of this map is
F^{1}/F^{0}
, as wanted.

(iii)
Trace maps
.
The
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module trace
{\rm Tr}_{\rm Kos}
is defined as the “Galois” cohomology
of the
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module residue map followed by

\frac{1}{|\Delta_{K}|}{\rm res}_{\pi}\circ{\rm Tr}_{K_{\infty}/F_{\infty}}
.
On the other hand, the pro-étale trace is defined as the Galois cohomology of the pro-étale residue map followed by Galois cohomology trace.
By point (ii) the first maps of the compositions agree.
Hence it suffices to show the compatibility of

\frac{1}{|\Delta_{K}|}{\rm res}_{\pi}\circ{\rm Tr}_{K_{\infty}/F_{\infty}}
with the Galois trace.
But this was done in Proposition
3.11
.
∎

7.3.5.
Identification of graded pieces

Via the comparison morphism
\alpha_{j}
from (
7.17
), and using Lemma
7.19
, we get isomorphisms:

(7.21)

\displaystyle\alpha^{3}_{j}:H^{i}(F^{2}/F^{1}){\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)),

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}_{j}:H^{i}(F^{1}/F^{0}){\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C}(j-1)),

\displaystyle\alpha^{1}_{j}:H^{i}(F^{0}/F^{-1}){\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

Recall that
(F^{1}/F^{0}){\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))\simeq H^{i}{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j))

Lemma 7.22
.

We have isomorphisms

H^{i}{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j))\xleftarrow{\sim}\begin{cases}{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})_{0}&{\text{if $j=1$ and $i=1,2$,}}\\
0&{\text{if $j\neq 1$ or $i\neq 1,2$.}}\end{cases}

Proof.

We have (in
{\mathscr{D}}({{\bf Q}_{p,\Box}})
)

{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j))\simeq\big[\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 38.5445pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-38.5445pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.83655pt\raise 5.93611pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.57501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\gamma_{K}-1}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.5445pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 62.5445pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\big]

and since
t\partial:\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v/p]}_{0}\to\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v/p]}_{0}
is
an isomorphism, we get a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({{\bf Q}_{p,\Box}})
:

{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j))\simeq\big(\,\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j)\overset{t\partial}{\longrightarrow}\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j-1)\,\big).

(The twist is
(j-1)
and not
(j)
because of the
\chi(\gamma_{K})^{-1}
appearing in the vertical arrow,
necessary to have a commutative diagram as
\gamma_{K}\cdot t\partial=\chi(\gamma_{K})t\partial\cdot\gamma_{K}
.)

Since
\partial:\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}\to\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}
is an isomorphism,
and since
\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}/t\simeq{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})_{0}
,
we get an isomorphism

(7.23)

H^{i}{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\begin{cases}{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})_{0}(j-1)&{\text{if $i=1$,}}\\
0&{\text{if $i\neq 1$.}}\end{cases}

Finally, we have isomorphisms (the first one is the natural injection, the second one
is the cup-product with
\frac{\log\chi}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}
):

H^{i}(\Gamma,{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})_{0}(j-1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\begin{cases}{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})_{0}&{\text{if $j=1$ and $i=0,1$,}}\\
0&{\text{if $j\neq 1$ or $i\neq 0,1$.}}\end{cases}

This is enough to prove our lemma.
∎

Remark 7.24
.

(i) In what follows we will use a convenient choice for the isomorphisms
in Lemma
7.22
which we will call
\omega_{i}
, for
i=1,2
, and call
\omega

a lift to the derived category.
Set

\displaystyle{\rm Kos}_{\gamma}({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})_{0}):=({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma_{K}-1}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})_{0})=({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})_{0}),

\displaystyle{\rm Kos}_{\gamma}({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})_{0}):=({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma_{K}-1}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})_{0}).

We take the following composition (where the second map is induced by the isomorphism

\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}/t\simeq{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})
)

\displaystyle\kappa:{\rm Kos}_{\gamma}({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})_{0})[-1]\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\operatorname{can}}}{\rm Kos}_{\gamma}({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})_{0})[-1]\longrightarrow{\rm Kos}^{\rm HK}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1)).

The isomorphism in Lemma
7.22
comes from the map
\omega:=\beta_{1}^{-1}\kappa
, where

\beta_{1}
is the map (
7.11
):

\beta_{1}:{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Kos}^{\rm HK}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1)).

(ii) Similarly, the isomorphism from (
7.23
) can be lifted to the derived category. We define it as
\omega_{\infty}:=\beta_{1,\infty}^{-1}\kappa_{\infty}
, where

\displaystyle\kappa_{\infty}:{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})_{0}[-1]\longrightarrow{\rm Kos}^{\rm HK}_{\partial,\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1)),

\displaystyle\beta_{1,\infty}:{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Kos}^{\rm HK}_{\partial,\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1)),

and the second map is the restriction of
\beta_{1}
.

From the above, we can deduce the following result
(compare with Lemma
4.38
; of course the splittings depend on the choice of
T
):

Proposition 7.25
.

Let
j\in{\bf Z}
.
We have
H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))=0
if
i\geq 4
and, if
i\leq 3
, then we have isomorphisms

H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathscr{O}}(Y)_{0}\oplus H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\oplus H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))&{\text{if $j=1$ and $i=1,2$,}}\\
H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\oplus H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))&{\text{if $j\neq 1$ or $i\neq 1,2$.}}\end{cases}

7.4.
Identification of the cup-product

7.4.1.
Formula for the cup-product

We are going to use a “basis”
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
to
write the differentials in the Koszul complex

{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))

in a way reminiscent
to differentials on differential forms (i.e,
e_{i}
behaves as
dx_{i}
in computations;
we don’t explicit the powers of
\chi(\gamma_{K})
involved in the different Tate twists)

\displaystyle dx

\displaystyle=(\varphi-1)x\cdot e_{1}+(\gamma_{K}-1)x\cdot e_{2}+t\partial x\cdot e_{3},

\displaystyle d(ae_{1}+be_{2}+ce_{3})

\displaystyle=(t\partial b-(\gamma_{K}-1)c)\cdot e_{2}\wedge e_{3}+((\varphi-1)c-t\partial a)\cdot e_{1}\wedge e_{3}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+((\varphi-1)b-(\gamma_{K}-1)a)\cdot e_{1}\wedge e_{2},

\displaystyle d(a\cdot e_{2}\wedge e_{3}+b\cdot e_{1}\wedge e_{3}+c\cdot e_{1}\wedge e_{2})

\displaystyle=((\varphi-1)a+(\gamma_{K}-1)b-t\partial c)\cdot e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge e_{3}.

The cup-product (we use, twice, the formulas from Section
7.3.2
with
\alpha=1
)

\cup^{\rm Kos}:{\rm Kos}^{1}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\rm Kos}^{2}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(2-j))\to{\rm Kos}^{3}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(2))

between the terms of degree
1
and
2

is then given by:

(7.26)

(a\cdot e_{1}+b\cdot e_{2}+c\cdot e_{3})\cup^{\rm Kos}(a^{\prime}\cdot e_{2}\wedge e_{3}+b^{\prime}\cdot e_{1}\wedge e_{3}+c^{\prime}\cdot e_{1}\wedge e_{2})=(-a\cup\varphi a^{\prime}+b\cup\gamma_{K}b^{\prime}+c\cup c^{\prime})\cdot e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge e_{3}.

7.4.2.
Orthogonality and reduction to the Poitou-Tate pairing

Consider now the trace map

{\rm Tr}_{Y}:H^{3}({\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(2)))\to{\bf Q}_{p}.

Then
{\rm Tr}_{Y}(x\cup^{\rm Kos}y)
can be computed in the following way:

\bullet
write
x\cup^{\rm Kos}y
as
a\cdot e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge e_{3}
, with

a\in\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v/p]}
;

\bullet
consider the constant term
a_{0}\in{\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v/p]}
of
a
;

\bullet
we have
{\rm Tr}_{Y}(x\cup^{\rm Kos}y)={\rm Tr}_{K}\circ h_{K}^{2}(a_{0})
,
where
{\rm Tr}_{K}
is the trace map
defined in Section
3.3
.

The restriction of the pairing
(x,y)\mapsto{\rm Tr}_{Y}(x\cup^{\rm Kos}y)

to
1
- and
2
-cocycles, respectively,
factors through

H^{1}(Y_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{2}(Y_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))
and gives the pairing from Theorem
7.1
.
Since only the constant term plays a role in the computation of the trace map,
we have the following orthogonalities:

\displaystyle{\rm Kos}^{1}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j))

\displaystyle\perp\big({\rm Kos}^{2}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(2-j))\oplus{\rm Kos}^{1}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(1-j))\big),

\displaystyle\big({\rm Kos}^{1}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j))\oplus{\rm Kos}^{0}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j-1))\big)

\displaystyle\perp{\rm Kos}^{2}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(2-j))

(because the product
of a series with constant term
0
by a constant gives a series with a constant term
0
).
We also have an orthogonality:

{\rm Kos}^{1}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j))\perp{\rm Kos}^{2}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(2-j))

because all the terms

a\cup\varphi a^{\prime}
,
b\cup\chi(\gamma_{K})^{-1}\gamma_{K}b^{\prime}
,
c\cup c^{\prime}
are
0
.
This proves claims (i) of Theorem
7.4

(because the only statement that needs to be checked is that

F^{0}H^{1}(Y_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}F^{0}H^{3}(Y_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
maps to
F^{1}H^{3}(Y_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
. But, in fact, we have just shown that this pairing is
0
).

Finally, the restriction to the cocycles in

\displaystyle{\rm Kos}^{1}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j))

\displaystyle\times{\rm Kos}^{1}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(1-j)),

\displaystyle{\rm Kos}^{0}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j-1))

\displaystyle\times{\rm Kos}^{1}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(1-j)),

\displaystyle{\rm Kos}^{0}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(j-1))

\displaystyle\times{\rm Kos}^{2}_{\varphi,\gamma}(({\bf B}_{K_{\infty}}^{[u,v]}(2-j))

is the usual cup-product coming from the theory of
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules and hence is equal to the one from the Poitou-Tate duality. This proves the Galois part of claim (ii) from Theorem
7.4
.

7.4.3.
The perfection of the coherent part of the pairing

It remains to understand the restriction of the pairing to the cocycles in

(7.27)

{\rm Kos}^{1}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(j))\times{\rm Kos}^{2}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(2-j)).

If
j\neq 1
, the cohomology groups of both terms in (
7.27
) are
0
and so the pairing is identically
0
. We can thus assume that
j=1
and in that case we have, by Lemma
7.22
, the isomorphisms:

\omega_{1}:{\mathscr{O}}(Y)_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{1}({\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1))),\quad\omega_{2}:{\mathscr{O}}(Y)_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1))).

Lemma 7.28
.

The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{{\mathscr{O}}(Y)_{0}\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathscr{O}}(Y)_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup^{\rm coh}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1)))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{2}({\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{Y}\circ\cup^{\rm Kos}}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}}

In particular, since the top pairing is perfect so is the bottom pairing.

Proof.

We start with
f,g\in{\mathscr{O}}(Y)_{0}
, and we will construct cocycles

z^{1}(g)\in{\rm Kos}^{1}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1)),\quad z^{2}(f)\in{\rm Kos}^{2}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1))

representing
\omega_{1}(g)
and
\omega_{2}(f)
.
Then we are going to show that:

(7.29)

{\rm Tr}_{Y}\big({\rm cl}(z^{1}(g))\cup^{\rm Kos}{\rm cl}(z^{2}(f))\big)=\tfrac{1}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}{\rm Tr}_{K/{\bf Q}_{p}}(g\cup^{\rm coh}f).

\bullet
For
g
, take its image
\kappa(g)\in{\rm Kos}^{{\rm HK},1}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1))

(see remark
7.24
for the definition of the map
\kappa
).
Now, take
\tilde{g}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}
that lifts
g

and consider the cocycle (in
{\rm Kos}^{1}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1))
)

z^{1}(g):=-((\varphi-1)\tfrac{\tilde{g}}{t}(1)\cdot e_{1}+(\gamma_{K}-1)\tfrac{\tilde{g}}{t}(1)\cdot e_{2}+t\partial\tfrac{\tilde{g}}{t}\cdot e_{3}).

(The twist (1) plays a role only in the action of
\gamma_{K}
and compensates for the action on
t
;
it follows that
(\gamma_{K}-1)\tfrac{\tilde{g}}{t}(1)\in\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}
since

g
is fixed by
\gamma_{K}
. The cocycle
z^{1}(g)
has then values in the desired group
and is not a coboundary as
\tfrac{\tilde{g}}{t}\notin\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}
when
g\neq 0
.)
We easily check
that
{\rm cl}(\beta_{1}(z^{1}(g)))={\rm cl}(\kappa(g))

(see formula (
7.11
) for the definition of the map
\beta_{1}
); hence

\omega_{1}(g)
is represented by
z^{1}(g)
.

\bullet
For
f
, take its image
\kappa(f)\in{\rm Kos}^{{\rm HK},2}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1))
. Now, take

\tilde{f}\in\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}
that lifts
f

and consider the cocycle (in
{\rm Kos}^{2}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1))
)

z^{2}(f):=-\partial\tilde{f}\cdot e_{2}\wedge e_{3}-(\varphi-1)\tfrac{\tilde{f}}{t}(1)\cdot e_{1}\wedge e_{2}.

We easily check
that
{\rm cl}(\beta_{1}(z^{2}(f)))={\rm cl}(\kappa(f))
; hence

\omega_{2}(f)
is represented by
z^{2}(f)
.

\bullet
Then (use formula (
7.26
))

z^{1}(g)\cup^{\rm Kos}z^{2}(f)=[-(\varphi-1)\tfrac{\tilde{g}}{t}\cdot\varphi(\partial\tilde{f})+t\partial\tfrac{\tilde{g}}{t}\cdot(\varphi-1)\tfrac{\tilde{f}}{t}]e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge e_{3}.

We can write
\tilde{f}
and
\tilde{g}
as series in
T
and reduce to the
case
\tilde{f}=\alpha T^{i}
and
\tilde{g}=\beta T^{j}
, for
i,j\neq 0
, (and so
f=\theta(\alpha)T^{i}
,
g=\theta(\beta)T^{j}
).
Using
\varphi(T)=T^{p}
,
\varphi(t)=pt
,
\partial T^{k}=kT^{k}
, we obtain the formula

z^{1}(g)\cup^{\rm Kos}z^{2}(f)=-\big[\big(\tfrac{\varphi(\beta)T^{pj}}{p\,t}-\tfrac{\beta T^{j}}{t}\big)i\varphi(\alpha)T^{pi}-j\beta T^{j}\big(\tfrac{\varphi(\alpha)T^{pi}}{p\,t}-\tfrac{\alpha T^{i}}{\,t}\big)\big]e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge e_{3}.

In order to get a nonzero constant term we need
j+pi=0
or
i+j=0
.

— If
j+pi=0
, the constant term is

-(-\beta i\varphi(\alpha)-\frac{j\beta\varphi(\alpha)}{\,p})\frac{1}{t}=0
.

— If
i+j=0
, the constant term is
-\frac{i\varphi(\alpha)\varphi(\beta)}{p\,t}-\frac{j\alpha\beta}{\,t}=j(\varphi-1)\frac{\alpha\beta}{t}
.

It follows from Proposition
3.12
that

{\rm Tr}_{Y}(z^{1}(g)\cup^{\rm Kos}z^{2}(f))=\begin{cases}\frac{-[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}\,j\,{\rm Tr}(\theta(\alpha)\theta(\beta))&{\text{if $i+j=0$,}}\\
0&{\text{if $i+j\neq 0$,}}\end{cases}

Using that
{\rm Tr}_{K/{\bf Q}_{p}}=[K:{\bf Q}_{p}]\,{\rm Tr}
on
K
,
we deduce that (see (
6.6
))

{\rm Tr}_{Y}(z^{1}(g)\cup^{\rm Kos}z^{2}(f))=\tfrac{-1}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}{\rm Tr}_{K/{\bf Q}_{p}}({\rm res}(fdg))=\tfrac{1}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}{\rm Tr}_{K/{\bf Q}_{p}}(g\cup^{\rm coh}f).

This proves equality (
7.29
), which we wanted.
∎

We have proved that the pairing

{\rm Tr}_{Y}\circ\cup^{\rm Kos}:H^{1}((F^{1}/F^{0}){\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(j)))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{2}((F^{1}/F^{0}){\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}(2-j)))\to{\bf Q}_{p}

is
perfect since or it is trivial or a multiple (for
j=1
) of the coherent pairing

{\rm Tr}_{K/{\bf Q}_{p}}\circ\cup^{\rm coh}:\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})}{K}\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})}{K}\to{\bf Q}_{p}.

It follows, by (
7.21
), that the pairing

(7.30)

\cup^{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}:H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C})(j-1))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C})(1-j))\to{\bf Q}_{p}

induced from pro-étale pairing is also perfect.

7.4.4.
Identification of the pairing (
7.30
)

To finish the proof of Theorem
7.4
,
it remains to show that, for
j=1
,
the pairing (
7.30
) is equal to the one induced from Galois pairing and coherent pairing:

\displaystyle\cup^{\rm Gal}:H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C})\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cup}}{{\to}}H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},C)\xleftarrow[\sim]{\tfrac{\log\chi}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}}K\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\rm Tr}_{K}}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

But, since both Galois cohomology groups
H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C})
and
H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C})
are isomorphic to
\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})}{K}
,
this can be checked by pulling back these pairings to
\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})}{K}
.
By Proposition
7.32
below,
the pro-étale pairing pullbacks to the coherent pairing
\cup^{\rm coh}
;
by Lemma
7.31
below,
so does the Galois-coherent pairing
\cup^{\rm Gal}
.
This proves that
\cup^{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}=\cup^{\rm Gal}
, as wanted.

Lemma 7.31
.

The following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{H^{0}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C})\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},C)}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\frac{\log\chi}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}}
\scriptstyle{\rm Tr_{K}}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y)}{K}\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y)}{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{can}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{\cup\frac{\log\chi}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm Tr_{K}}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}.}

In particular, the Galois-coherent pairing defined by the top row is perfect.

Proof.

We compute. Going up and then right in the above diagram (and stopping at
K
) we get:

\displaystyle\{f,g\}

\displaystyle\to\{f,\{\sigma\mapsto\tfrac{\log\chi(\sigma)}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}g\}\}\xrightarrow{\cup}\{\sigma\mapsto{\rm res}(fd(\tfrac{\log\chi(\sigma)}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}g))\}

\displaystyle=\{\sigma\mapsto\tfrac{\log\chi(\sigma)}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}{\rm res}(fdg)\}.

Going right and then up we get:

\displaystyle\{f,g\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cup}}{{\to}}{\rm res}(fdg)\to\{\sigma\mapsto\tfrac{\log\chi(\sigma)}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}{\rm res}(fdg)\},

as wanted.
∎

Proposition 7.32
.

Let
i=1,2
. The following diagram commutes

\textstyle{H^{i}{\rm Kos}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{2}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C})}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})/K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}

where
f_{1}=\operatorname{can},f_{2}=\tfrac{\log\chi}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}
.

Proof.

Consider the commutative diagram (we shortened
{\rm Kos}
to
{\rm K}
,
{\rm res}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}
to
{\rm res}
; removed subscripts from pro-étale cohomology; and set
s:=i-1
):

\textstyle{H^{s}{\rm K}_{\gamma}({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{can}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{K})_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\textstyle{H^{s}{\rm K}_{\gamma}({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{H^{s}{\rm K}_{\gamma}({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{H^{s}(\Gamma,{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{\widehat{K}_{\infty}})_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\wr}

\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{H^{s+1}{\rm K}^{\rm HK}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{s}{\rm K}_{\gamma_{K}}(H^{1}{\rm K}^{\rm HK}_{\partial,\varphi}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1)))}
\textstyle{H^{s+1}{\rm K}_{\partial,\varphi,\gamma}(\widetilde{\mathscr{O}}^{[u,v]}_{0}(1)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
