\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}^{2}}
\textstyle{H^{s}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))^{{\rm res}=0})}
\textstyle{H^{s}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}

The bottom square commutes by the proof of Lemma
7.19
. The rest commutes basically by constructions of the involved maps. Tracing this diagram proves our proposition.
∎

8.
Arithmetic Poincaré duality

Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\bf Q}_{p}
. This chapter is devoted to the proof of the arithmetic Poincaré duality for smooth dagger curves over
K
. We start with stating the duality, then we prove it for proper curves, where it is an easy consequence of the geometric Poincaré duality. After that we prove it for an open disc and an open annulus over
K
(via a reduction to the Poincaré duality for the ghost circle proved earlier). This then allows us to treat the case of wide open curves (a special type of Stein curves) and we treat the case of general Stein curves by a limit argument. Finally, an analogous limit argument yields the duality for a dagger affinoid.

8.1.
The statement of arithmetic Poincaré duality

The goal of this paper is to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 8.1
.

(Arithmetic Poincaré duality)
Let
X
be a smooth geometrically irreducible dagger variety of dimension
1
over
K
. Assume that
X
is proper, Stein, or affinoid. Then:

(1)

There is a natural trace map isomorphism

{\rm Tr}_{X}:\,H^{4}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

(2)

For
i,j\in{\bf Z}
,
the pairing

H^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}^{\Box}H^{4-i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{4}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{X}}{\bf Q}_{p}

is a perfect duality, i.e., we have induced isomorphisms

\displaystyle\gamma_{X,i}:H^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{4-i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*},

\displaystyle\gamma_{X,i}^{c}:H^{i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{4-i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}.

Here we wrote
(-)^{*}
for the internal
{\rm{Hom}}
in the category of solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces.

8.2.
The case of proper curves

A proper smooth curve is the analytification of an algebraic smooth curve for which
Theorem
8.1
has been known for quite a while
25
25

25

Of course, modulo certain identifications.
.
But, actually,
Theorem
8.1
holds for any smooth proper geometrically irreducible rigid analytic variety
X
over
K
of dimension
d
(see Corollary
8.3
). This follows from the recently proved
geometric Poincaré duality (Theorem
8.2
)
and local Galois duality.

Recall that, if
X
is a smooth proper variety over
K
, geometrically irreducible, then its pro-étale cohomology complex

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{L},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
, for
L=K,C
, has classical cohomology and the cohomology groups
H^{i}(X_{L},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
are finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces with their canonical Hausdorff topology (see Section
5.1.1
). Over
C
, it satisfies Poincaré duality:

Theorem 8.2
.

(Zavyalov, Mann
[
34
, 5.5.7]
,
[
25
, Th. 1.1.1]
)

Let
{X}
be a smooth proper geometrically irreducible rigid analytic variety of pure dimension
d
over
K
. Then:

(i)

There is a Galois-equivariant
{\bf Q}_{p}
-linear trace map isomorphism

{\rm Tr}_{X_{C}}:\,H^{2d}({X}_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(d))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

(ii)

For
i\in{\bf N},j\in{\bf Z}
, the trace map
{\rm Tr}_{X_{C}}
induces a perfect Galois-equivariant pairing of finite rank
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces:

H^{i}({X}_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}^{\Box}H^{2d-i}({X}_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(d-j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{2d}({X}_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(d))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{X_{C}}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

Combining it with local Galois duality we obtain:

Corollary 8.3
.

(
Arithmetic Poincaré duality
)
Let
{X}
be a smooth proper geometrically irreducible rigid analytic variety of pure dimension
d
over
K
. Then:

(i)

There is a natural
{\bf Q}_{p}
-linear trace map isomorphism

{\rm Tr}_{X}:\,H^{2d+2}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

(ii)

For
i\in{\bf N},j\in{\bf Z}
, the pairing

H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}^{\Box}H^{2d+2-i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1-j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{2d+2}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{X}}{\bf Q}_{p}

is a perfect duality of finite rank
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
8.2
and from the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence:

(8.4)

E^{a,b}_{2}(j)=H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b}({X}_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\Rightarrow H^{a+b}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

The trace map
{\rm Tr}_{X}
comes from the composition:

{\rm Tr}_{X}:\,H^{2d+2}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1))\simeq H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2d}({X}_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1)))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{X_{C}}(1)}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\xrightarrow{\sim}{\bf Q}_{p}.

By Theorem
8.2
, it is an isomorphism.

To prove the duality, note that the only nonzero terms of
E^{a,b}_{2}(j)
are those with degrees
0\leq a\leq 2
and
0\leq b\leq 2d
; hence the spectral sequence degenerates at
E_{3}
.
As the cup product commutes with the differentials
d_{2}^{a,b}:E_{2}^{a,b}\to E_{2}^{a+2,b-1}
of (
8.4
), we get a commutative diagram with exact rows:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{3}^{a,b}(j)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}

\sim

\textstyle{H^{a+2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{b-1}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\sim

\textstyle{E_{3}^{a+2,b-1}(j)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{3}^{2-a,2d-b}(j^{*})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2-a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2d-b}(j^{*}))^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{d}^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{-a}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2d{+}1{-}b}(j^{*}))^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{3}^{-a,2d{+}1{-}b}(j^{*})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where
d:=d_{2}^{a,b}
,
\tilde{d}:=d_{2}^{-a,2d+1-b}
and we set
j^{*}:=d+1-j
and
H^{b}(j):=H^{b}({X}_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
.
The second and third vertical arrows are isomorphisms by Theorem
8.2
and Tate’s duality. We deduce that the two other vertical maps are isomorphisms as well.

The Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence (
8.4
) induces a descending filtration on
H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
:

H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))=F^{0}_{i,j}\supset F^{1}_{i,j}\supset F^{2}_{i,j}\supset F^{3}_{i,j}=0.

Since it degenerates at
E_{3}
, i.e.,
E_{3}=E_{\infty}
, the above diagram gives a perfect duality

{\rm gr}^{\bullet}_{F}H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}^{\Box}{\rm gr}^{2-\bullet}_{F}H^{2d+2-i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1-j))\to{\rm gr}^{2}_{F}H^{2d+2}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

By devissage (via the filtration F), this perfect duality lifts to the following perfect pairings (in the presented order):

\displaystyle(F^{0}/F^{2})H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}^{\Box}F^{1}H^{2d+2-i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1-j))\to{\bf Q}_{p},

\displaystyle F^{0}H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\otimes_{{\bf Q}_{p}}^{\Box}F^{0}H^{2d+2-i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(d+1-j))\to{\bf Q}_{p}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

We specialize now to curves. Let
X
be a proper smooth geometrically irreducible curve over
K
.
By
[
1
, Rem. 7.16]
, our geometric trace map
{\rm Tr}_{X_{C}}:H^{2}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to{\bf Q}_{p}
is equal to the trace map used by Zavyalov in
[
34
]
, i.e., to the rigid analytic version of the Berkovich trace map
(see
[
34
, Sec. 5.3]
,
[
4
, Sec. 7.2]
). Hence in this case Theorem
8.1
follows from Zavyalov’s arithmetic duality stated in Corollary
8.3
.

8.3.
The case of an open disc

We will now prove Theorem
8.1
for an open disc.
Let
D
be an open disc
D
over
K
.

Proposition 8.5
.

(Arithmetic duality for an open disc)
Theorem
8.1
holds for
D
.

Proof.

(i) The trace map

{\rm Tr}_{D}:\,H^{4}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2)){\to}{\bf Q}_{p}

was defined in Section
6.1
. It will be convenient to have a different description of this map. Let
Y:=\partial D
be the boundary of
D
, a ghost circle. Consider the composition

t_{D}:H^{4}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\xleftarrow[\sim]{\partial}H^{3}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{Y}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

The first strict isomorphism holds because
H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))=0
, for
i\geq 3
, by Lemma
4.27
.
An alternative definition of
t_{D}
is the following. First, we define the geometric trace zig-zag:

t_{D_{C}}:H^{2}_{c}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\xleftarrow{\partial}H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\xrightarrow{{\rm Tr}_{Y_{C}}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

Then the arithmetic trace
t_{D}
is obtained as a composition of
H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},t_{D_{C}}(1))
with
{\rm Tr}_{K}:H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\bf Q}_{p}
via the identifications coming from (
5.19
) and Lemma
4.38
.

We claim that
t_{D}={\rm Tr}_{D}
. For that, it is enough to prove that the geometric traces
t_{D_{C}},{\rm Tr}_{D_{C}}
are equal after we apply
H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},(-)(1))
. This will follow if we prove that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{H^{1}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{Y_{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{c}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{D_{C}}}

Or, as can be seen by unwinding the definitions of trace maps, that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{H^{1}_{\operatorname{syn}}(Y_{C},2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{(H^{1}_{{\rm HK}}(Y_{C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{\operatorname{syn},c}(D_{C},2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(H^{2}_{{\rm HK},c}(D_{C}){\otimes}^{\Box}_{\breve{F}}\widehat{{\bf B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}})^{N=0,\varphi=p^{2}}}

But this follows easily from the definitions.

(ii) We will first show that the pairing

(8.6)

H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{4-i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cup}}{{\to}}H^{4}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{D}}{\bf Q}_{p}

induces the isomorphism

(8.7)

\displaystyle\gamma_{D,i}:\quad H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{4-i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}.

(
\bullet
)
Compatibility of pairings.
The pairing (
8.6
)
is compatible with the pairing (
7.2
), i.e., the diagram

(8.8)

\textstyle{H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{i^{\prime}}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{can}}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H^{i+i^{\prime}}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j+j^{\prime}))}
\textstyle{H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{i^{\prime}-1}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{H^{i+i^{\prime}-1}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j+j^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}

commutes (up to a sign):
for
a\in H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))(S)
and
b\in H^{i^{\prime}-1}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j^{\prime}))(S)
, where
S
is an extremally disconnected set, we have

\partial(\operatorname{can}(a)\cup b)=(-1)^{i}a\cup\partial(b).

This follows easily from the formula (
7.15
) (take
\alpha=0
). The injectivity and surjectivity of the vertical maps in the diagram follow from (
5.16
).

(
\bullet
)
Filtration on cohomology.
By Section
4.4
and Lemma
4.27
, there exists an ascending filtration on
H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
:

F^{2}_{i,j}=H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\supset F^{1}_{i,j}\supset F^{0}_{i,j}\supset F^{-1}_{i,j}=0,

such that

\displaystyle F^{1}_{i,j}=F^{2}_{i,j}=H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),\quad F^{1}_{i,j}/F^{0}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})}{C}(j-1)),

\displaystyle F^{0}_{i,j}/F^{-1}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

Lemma 8.9
.

The injection

\operatorname{can}:H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\hookrightarrow H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

is strict for the given filtrations, i.e., the induced map

(8.10)

(F^{s+1}/F^{s})H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to(F^{s+1}/F^{s})H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),\quad s\geq-1,

is injective.

Proof.

Since
(F^{s+1}/F^{s})H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))=0
for
s\neq-1,0
, it suffices to check the statement of the lemma for
s=-1,0
.
For
s=-1
, it is clear. For
s=0
, we can write the map (
8.10
) as:

(8.11)

\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})}{C}(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C}(j-1)).}

Our claim now follows from the compatible isomorphisms (
3.16
).
∎

(
\bullet
)
Filtration on cohomology with compact support.
There exists an ascending filtration on
H^{i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
:

F^{2}_{c,i,j}=H^{i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\supset F^{1}_{c,i,j}\supset F^{0}_{c,i,j}=0,

such that

\displaystyle F^{2}_{c,i,j}/F^{1}_{c,i,j}\simeq H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)),\quad F^{1}_{c,i,j}/F^{0}_{c,i,j}\simeq H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})}(j-1)).

We can visualize it in the following way (to simplify the notation we removed the subscripts from cohomology):

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{1}_{c,i,j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},({\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})/{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C}))(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{2}_{c,i,j}:=H^{i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Hence the filtration comes basically only from the syntomic sequence. The right column is exact by Lemma
5.15
.

Lemma 8.12
.

The canonical maps

\partial:F^{s}H^{i-1}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to F^{s}H^{i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),\quad s\geq-1,

are surjective.

Proof.

This is clear for
s=-1,0
and
s\geq 2
. For
s=1
, we use the computations done earlier, to rewrite the map in the lemma as the canonical map:

(8.13)

\partial:H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C}(1-j))\to H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})}(1-j)).

Using the generalized Tate’s isomorphisms (
3.16
)
we see that the map in (
8.13
) is surjective.
∎

(
\bullet
)
Pairings on the graded pieces.
From diagram (
8.8
), Lemma
8.12
, and Theorem
7.4
, it follows that the cup product pairing

\cup:\quad H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{i^{\prime}}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j^{\prime}))\to H^{i+i^{\prime}}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j+j^{\prime}))

is compatible with the above filtrations. In particular,
the subgroups

\displaystyle F^{0}_{i,j}=H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\subset H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),

\displaystyle F^{1}_{c,4-i,2-j}=H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})}(1-j))\subset H^{4-i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))

are orthogonal.
That orthogonality induces the following map of exact sequences (all the vertical maps are induced from cup products and the trace map
{\rm Tr}_{D}
):

(8.14)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{D,i}}
\textstyle{H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{D,i}}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})}{C}(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{D,i}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1-j))^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{4-i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})}(1-j))^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

It suffices now to show that maps
\alpha_{D,i}
and
\beta_{D,i}
are isomorphisms. This is easy to prove for the first map because this map
is induced from Galois cohomology pairing

H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1-j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cup}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{K}}{\bf Q}_{p}

as can be seen by comparing
\alpha_{D,i}
with the map
\alpha_{Y,i}
– an analog for the ghost circle
Y
– and evoking Theorem
7.4
.

To identify map
\beta_{D}
, consider the commutative diagram (we omitted the indices of filtrations and subscripts from cohomology):

\textstyle{(F^{1}/F^{0})H^{i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(F^{1}_{c}/F^{0}_{c})H^{4-i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{can}}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{(F^{2}_{c}/F^{1}_{c})H^{4}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}}
\textstyle{(F^{1}/F^{0})H^{i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(F^{1}/F^{0})H^{3-i}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{(F^{2}_{c}/F^{1}_{c})H^{3}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}

Map
\beta_{D,i}
is induced by the top pairing in this diagram and we want to show that that this pairing is perfect. Map
\operatorname{can}
is injective by Lemma
8.9
and maps
\partial
are surjective by Lemma
8.12
. Identifying the graded pieces, the above commutative diagram can be rewritten as:

(8.15)

\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})}{C}(j-1))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{{\mathscr{O}}(D_{C})}(1-j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{can}}
\scriptstyle{\cup^{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}}
\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C}(j-1))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{2-i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y_{C})}{C}(1-j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup^{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}}
\scriptstyle{\cup^{G-coh}}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}^{\prime}_{K}}

where
{\rm Tr}^{\prime}_{K}
is the composition

H^{1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},C)\xleftarrow[\sim]{\frac{\log\chi}{\log\chi(\gamma_{K})}}K\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\rm Tr}_{K/{\bf Q}_{p}}}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

The right triangle commutes by Theorem
7.4
.
Going back to the definition of cohomology with compact support, it is easy to check that the vertical maps (
\operatorname{can}
and
\partial
) are induced from the canonical coherent maps. Moreover, using the generalized Tate’s isomorphisms (
3.16
), we can rewrite the nontrivial cases of the above commutative diagram further as:

(8.16)

\textstyle{\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(D)}{K}\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y)}{{\mathscr{O}}(D)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{can}}
\scriptstyle{\cup^{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}}
\textstyle{\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y)}{K}\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}\frac{{\mathscr{O}}(Y)}{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup^{\rm coh}}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{K}}

It is now clear that the top product in this diagram is the coherent product. Since the latter
is perfect, it follows that so is the top product in diagram (
8.15
), as wanted.

The argument for the map

\displaystyle\gamma^{c}_{D,i}:\quad H^{i}_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{4-i}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{.}

is analogous.
∎

8.4.
The case of an open annulus

We will now prove Theorem
8.1
for an open annulus.
Let
A
be an open annulus over
K
.

Proposition 8.17
.

(Arithmetic duality for an open annulus)
Theorem (
8.1
) holds for
A
.

Proof.

Let
Y:=\partial A
be the boundary of
A
, a disjoint union of two ghost circles
Y_{a}
,
Y_{b}
over
K
.

(i) The geometric and arithmetic trace maps are defined as follows:

(8.18)

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{A_{C}}:H^{2}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to{\bf Q}_{p},

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{A}:H^{4}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{2}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(2)))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\rm Tr}_{A_{C}})}}{\bf Q}_{p},

where
{\rm Tr}_{A_{C}}
is the map coming from (
5.21
). The map
{\rm Tr}_{A}
is an isomorphism by Lemma
5.23
and the vanishing of
H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A_{C})/({\mathscr{O}}(A_{C})\oplus C)(1))
(see (
5.21
)).

Alternatively, we have an exact sequence

(8.19)

0\to H^{1}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to H^{1}(Y_{a,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\oplus H^{1}(Y_{b,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial}}{{\to}}H^{2}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to 0.

The trace map
{\rm Tr}_{A_{C}}
is induced from the trace map

{\rm Tr}_{Y_{a,C}}+{\rm Tr}_{Y_{b,C}}:H^{1}(Y_{a,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\oplus H^{1}(Y_{b,C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to{\bf Q}_{p}.

This works because
H^{1}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\bf Q}_{p}
compatibly with the maps
{\rm Tr}_{Y_{a,C}}
and
{\rm Tr}_{Y_{b,C}}
.
Applying
H^{2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},-)
and
{\rm Tr}_{K}
to the exact sequence (
8.19
), we obtain that the arithmetic trace
{\rm Tr}_{A}
can be defined via the maps

{\rm Tr}_{A}:\,H^{4}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\partial}}{{\twoheadleftarrow}}H^{3}(Y_{a},{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\oplus H^{3}(Y_{b},{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\rm Tr}_{Y_{a}}+{\rm Tr}_{Y_{b}}}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

We used here the fact that the composition

H^{3}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\to H^{3}(\partial A,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{{\rm Tr}_{Y_{a}}+{\rm Tr}_{Y_{b}}}}{\bf Q}_{p}

is
0
.

(ii) We will first show that
the pairings

(8.20)

H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{4-i}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cup}}{{\to}}H^{4}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm Tr}_{A}}{\bf Q}_{p}

induce isomorphisms

\displaystyle\gamma_{A,i}:\quad H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{4-i}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}.

(
\bullet
)
Compatibility of pairings.
The pairing (
8.20
) is compatible with the pairing (
7.2
), i.e., the diagram

(8.21)

\textstyle{H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{i^{\prime}}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{can}}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H^{i+i^{\prime}}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j+j^{\prime}))}
\textstyle{H^{i}(Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{b},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{i^{\prime}-1}(Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{b},{\bf Q}_{p}(j^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{H^{i+i^{\prime}-1}(Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{b},{\bf Q}_{p}(j+j^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}

commutes (up to a sign). That is, for
a\in H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))(S)
and
b\in H^{i^{\prime}-1}(Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{b},{\bf Q}_{p}(j^{\prime}))(S)
, where
S
is an extremally disconnected set, we have

\partial(\operatorname{can}(a)\cup b)=(-1)^{i}a\cup\partial(b).

This follows easily from the formulas in Section
7.3.2
. The injectivity and surjectivity of the vertical maps in diagram (
8.21
) follows from the computations in Section
5.5
.

(
\bullet
)
Filtration on cohomology.
By Section
4.4
and Lemma
4.34
, there exists an ascending filtration on
H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
:

F^{2}_{i,j}=H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\supset F^{1}_{i,j}\supset F^{1}_{i,j}\supset F^{-1}_{i,j}=0,

such that

\displaystyle F^{2}_{i,j}/F^{1}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)),\quad F^{1}_{i,j}/F^{0}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(A_{C})}{C}(j-1)),

\displaystyle F^{0}_{i,j}/F^{-1}_{i,j}\simeq H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

Lemma 8.22
.

The canonical injection

\operatorname{can}:H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\hookrightarrow H^{i}(Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{b},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

is strict for the filtrations, i.e., the induced map

(8.23)

(F^{s+1}/F^{s})H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to(F^{s+1}/F^{s})H^{i}(Y_{a}\sqcup Y_{b},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),\quad s\geq-1,

is injective.

Proof.

This is clear for
s=-1
and
s\geq 1
. For
s=0
, the argument is analogous to the one used in the proof of Lemma
8.9
.
∎

(
\bullet
)
Filtration on cohomology with compact support.
Similarly, there exists an ascending filtration on
H^{i}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
:

F^{2}_{c,i,j}=H^{i}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\supset F^{1}_{c,i,j}\supset F^{0}_{c,i,j}\supset F^{-1}_{c,i,j}=0,

such that

\displaystyle F^{2}_{c,i,j}/F^{1}_{c,i,j}\simeq H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1)),\quad F^{1}_{c,i,j}/F^{0}_{c,i,j}\simeq H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A_{C})}{{\mathscr{O}}(A_{C})\oplus C}(j-1)),

\displaystyle F^{0}_{c,i,j}/F^{-1}_{c,i,j}\simeq H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\simeq H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

We will visualize this filtration in the following way (to simplify the notation we removed the subscripts from cohomology):

(8.24)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{0}_{c,i,j}:=H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{1}_{c,i,j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

The diagram is a map of exact sequences with exact columns. The middle exact row comes from the filtration induced by the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence (see Lemma
5.23
).
The right exact column is induced by the syntomic filtration from (
5.21
). The term
F^{1}_{i,j}
is defined as the pullback of the top right square.

Lemma 8.25
.

The map

\partial:F^{s}H^{i-1}(\partial A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to F^{s}H^{i}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),\quad s\geq-1,

is surjective.

Proof.

This is clear for
s=-1
and
s\geq 2
. For
s=0
, we need to check strict surjectivity of the canonical map

H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{0}(\partial A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},H^{1}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))).

But, as follows from (
5.21
), the canonical map

H^{0}(\partial A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{1}_{c}(A_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

is surjective with a Galois equivariant section.

For
s=1
, having done the case of
s=0
, it suffices to show that the map

\partial:\quad(F^{2}/F^{1})H^{i-1}(\partial A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to(F^{2}/F^{1})H^{i}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

is surjective. This amounts to showing that the canonical map

H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A_{C})}{C}(j-1))\to H^{i-2}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A_{C})}{{\mathscr{O}}(A_{C})\oplus C}(j-1))

is surjective. But, by formula (
3.16
) and its suitable analog, or both the domain and the target of this map are trivial or this map is isomorphic to the canonical map

\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A)}{K}\to\tfrac{{\mathscr{O}}(\partial A)}{{\mathscr{O}}(A)\oplus K}

whose strict surjectivity is clear.
∎

(
\bullet
)
Pairings on the graded pieces.
From diagram (
8.21
), Lemma
8.25
, and Theorem
7.4
, it follows that the cup product pairing

\cup:\quad H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}H^{i^{\prime}}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j^{\prime}))\to H^{i+i^{\prime}}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j+j^{\prime}))

is compatible with the above filtrations. In particular,
the subgroups

\displaystyle F^{0}_{i,j}=H^{i}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\subset H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),

\displaystyle F^{1}_{c,4-i,2-j}\subset H^{4-i}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))

are orthogonal and so are

F^{0}_{c}
and
F^{1}
. Hence
we obtain the following commutative diagram with exact rows (all the vertical maps are induced from cup products and the trace map
{\rm Tr}_{A}
)

(8.26)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\gamma_{A,i}}

\textstyle{H^{i-1}({\mathscr{G}}_{K},{\bf Q}_{p}(j-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{0}

Using Theorem

is induced by the Galois pairing; hence it is an isomorphism.

To identify map

, consider the commutative diagram (we omitted the indices of filtrations and the subscripts of cohomology):

\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{{\bf Q}_{p}}

and maps

. Identifying the graded pieces, the above commutative diagram can be rewritten as:

and

) are induced from the canonical coherent maps. Moreover, using the generalized Tate’s isomorphisms (

), we can rewrite the nontrivial cases of the above commutative diagram further as:

It is now clear that the top product in this diagram is also the coherent product. Since the latter

We have shown that we have an isomorphism

\beta_{A,i}:\quad(F^{2}/F^{0})H^{i}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(F^{1}H^{4-i}_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j)))^{*}.

The arguments for the map

∎

8.5.
The case of wide open curves

Now we pass to a special kind of Stein curves.

8.5.1.
Definition of wide opens

A
wide open
(see
[
10
, Sec. III]
for a brief study) is a rigid analytic space isomorphic to the complement in a proper, geometrically connected, and smooth curve
of finitely many closed discs. Examples of wide opens include
open discs and annuli. A
K
-wide open
is a rigid analytic space over
K
isomorphic to the complement in a proper, geometrically connected, and smooth curve over
K

of finitely many closed discs over
K
.

We will need the following fact:

Lemma 8.30
.

Let
X
be a wide open over
K
. Then one can embed
X

into a proper, geometrically connected, and smooth curve
\overline{X}
over
K
such that we have an admissible covering

\overline{X}=X\cup_{i=1}^{m}\{D_{i}\},

where
D_{i}
’s are disjoint discs
over
K
with centers
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
,
x_{i}\in\overline{X}(K)
, such that the intersections
A_{i}:=X\cap D_{i}
are open annuli.

Proof.

By definition, we can embed
X
into
\overline{X}
as in the lemma with complementary disjoint closed discs
\overline{D}_{j}
. We embed these discs
\overline{D}_{j}
into open discs
D_{j}
. By shrinking if necessary,
we may insure that the open discs are disjoint as well. It is then clear that the intersections
A_{j}:=X\cap D_{j}
are open annuli, as wanted.
∎

8.5.2.
Theorem
8.1
for wide opens

Proposition 8.31
.

(Arithmetic duality for wide opens)

Let
X
be a wide open over
K
. Theorem
8.1
holds for
X
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.30
,
X
can be embedded into a proper smooth geometrically irreducible curve
\overline{X}
over
K
. Using the same notation as in that lemma, we write
D
for the union of the open discs
D_{i}
’s and
A
for the union of the open annuli
A_{i}
’s (coming from the intersections of the
D_{i}
’s and
X
).

For
j\in{\bf Z}
, we have the Mayer-Vietoris distinguished triangles:

(8.32)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\overline{X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\oplus{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)),

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\oplus{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(\overline{X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)).

The first one comes from analytic descent of pro-étale cohomology and the second one from analytic co-descent of compactly supported pro-étale cohomology.

The following lemma will be needed later to pass from derived duality to classical duality. We will write
{\mathbb{D}}(-):={\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(-,{\bf Q}_{p})
for the duality functor.

Lemma 8.33
.

Let
X
be a geometrically connected smooth Stein curve over
K
. Let
j,s\in{\bf Z}
. We have a natural isomorphism

H^{j}{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(s)))\simeq H^{-j}_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(s))^{*},

Proof.

We have the spectral sequence

E_{2}^{i,j}=\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{i}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(H^{-j}_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(s)),{\bf Q}_{p})\Rightarrow H^{i+j}{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(s))).

Hence, it suffices to show that

(8.34)

\displaystyle\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{i}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(H^{j}_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(s)),{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq\begin{cases}H^{j}_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(s))^{*}&\mbox{ if }i=0,\\
0&\mbox{ if }i>0.\end{cases}

Since, by Theorem
5.8
, the solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector space
V^{j}:=H^{j}_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(s))
is of compact type, it is an LS of compact type (by
[
29
, Cor. 3.38]
). That is, it can be written as a countable colimit of Smith spaces with injective trace class transition maps (see
[
29
, Def. 3.34]
):

V^{j}\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{n}V^{j,S}_{n}
, where
V^{j,S}_{n}
’s are Smith spaces. We compute

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(V^{j},{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(\operatorname{colim}_{n}V^{j,S}_{n},{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(V^{j,S}_{n},{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(V^{j,S}_{n},{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq\lim_{n}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(V^{j,S}_{n},{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(\operatorname{colim}_{n}V^{j,S}_{n},{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle\simeq\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(V^{j},{\bf Q}_{p}).

The third quasi-isomorphism follows from the fact that Smith spaces are projective objects, the fourth one from Section
2.2.4
since the pro-system
\{\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}(V^{j,S}_{n},{\bf Q}_{p})\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
is built from Banach spaces with compact transition maps hence is equivalent to a pro-system of Banach spaces with dense transition maps (by
[
30
, discussion after Prop. 16.5]
). This proves (
8.34
), as wanted.
∎

(i)
Duality map
\gamma_{X,i}
.
Apply now the duality functor
{\mathbb{D}}(-)
to the second triangle in (
8.32
). We obtain a distinguished triangle

(8.35)

{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(\overline{X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\oplus{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(j)))\to{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))).

Here and below, we write
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(-):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma
,
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(-):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(-)
.
We have a map of distinguished triangles:

(8.36)

\textstyle{{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\overline{X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{\overline{X}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(\overline{X},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\oplus{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({D},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\gamma_{{X}}}\oplus{\gamma_{{D}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4])\oplus\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(D,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4])}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{{A}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(A,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4])}

The top horizontal arrow is a quasi-isomorphism by Section
8.2
. The bottom horizontal arrow is a quasi-isomorphism by Proposition
8.17
and Lemma
8.33
.
Since, moreover, by Proposition
8.5
and Lemma
8.33
, the arrow
\gamma_{D}
in diagram (
8.36
) is a quasi-isomorphism, it follows that the map

(8.37)

{\gamma_{{X}}}:{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\xrightarrow{}{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4]),

is a quasi-isomorphism as well. That is, for
i\in{\bf N}
, we have an induced isomorphism

(8.38)

{\gamma_{{X},i}}:H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{i}{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4]).

This, in combination with Lemma
8.33
, yields the isomorphism

{\gamma_{{X},i}}:H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{4-i}_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*},

as wanted.

(ii)
Duality map
\gamma^{c}_{X,i}
.

Write the map
\gamma_{X,i}^{c}
as the composition

(8.39)

\gamma^{c}_{X,i}:H^{i}_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm eval}}(H^{i}_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))^{*})^{*}\xrightarrow[\sim]{\gamma^{*}_{X,4-i}}H^{4-i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}.

The evaluation map is an isomorphism since
H^{i}_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is reflexive, hence LS, hence solid reflexive by
[
29
, Thm. 3.40]
. This proves that
\gamma^{c}_{X,i}
is an isomorphism, as wanted.
∎

8.6.
The case of general Stein curves

Finally, we are ready to treat general Stein curves.

Proposition 8.40
.

(Arithmetic duality for Stein curves)

Let
X
be a smooth geometrically irreducible Stein curve over
K
. Theorem
8.1
holds for
X
.

Proof.

(i)
Reduction step.
Take a Stein covering
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in{\bf N}}
of
X
by dagger affinoids with adapted naive interiors
X^{0}_{n}
of
X_{n}
,
n\geq 1
. Using
[
10
, proof of Prop. 3.3]
we may choose
X^{0}_{n}
to be wide opens over finite extensions
L_{n}
of
K
. Since the duality map

\gamma_{Y}:{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(Y,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4])

satisfies étale descent, we know from Proposition
8.31
that it is a quasi-isomorphism for each
X^{0}_{n}
.

(ii)
Trace map.
We can write the trace map as

{\rm Tr}_{X}:\quad H^{4}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\operatorname{colim}_{n}H^{4}_{c}(X^{0}_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(2))\xrightarrow[\sim]{\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\rm Tr}_{X^{0}_{n}}}{\bf Q}_{p}.

(iii)
Duality map
\gamma_{X,i}
.

The duality map

\gamma_{X}:{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4])

can be written as the composition (we set
s:=2-j
)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X^{0}_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\xrightarrow[\sim]{\gamma_{X_{n}}}{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X^{0}_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(s))[4])

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathbb{D}}(\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X^{0}_{n},{\bf Q}_{p}(s))[4])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(s))[4]).

The second quasi-isomorphism follows from (i).

On cohomology level this gives us isomorphisms

\gamma_{X,i}:H^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{i}{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))[4]).

By Theorem
5.8
,
H^{i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is of compact type hence

H^{i}{\mathbb{D}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(s)))\simeq(H^{i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(s)))^{*}
by the computation above (proving (
8.34
)). Combination of these two observations shows that the duality map

\gamma_{X,i}:H^{i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{4-i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}

is an isomorphism.

(iv)
Duality map
\gamma^{c}_{X}
.
Analogous to the argument used in the proof of Proposition
8.31
.
∎

8.7.
The case of dagger affinoid curves

Finally, we will treat dagger affinoids of dimension
1
.

Proposition 8.41
.

(Arithmetic duality for dagger affinoid curves)

Let
X
be a smooth geometrically irreducible dagger affinoid curve over
K
. Theorem
8.1
holds for
X
.

Proof.

Take a presentation
\{X_{h}\}_{h\in{\bf N}}
of the dagger structure on
X
. Denote by
X^{0}_{h}
a naive interior of
X_{h}
adapted to
\{X_{h}\}
.

(i)
Duality map
\gamma^{c}_{X,i}
.

The duality map

(8.42)

\gamma^{c}_{X,i}:H^{i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{4-i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}

can be written as the composition

\displaystyle H^{i}_{c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(j))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\lim_{h}H^{i}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\xrightarrow[\sim]{\gamma^{c}_{X^{0}_{h},i}}\lim_{h}H^{4-i}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}

\displaystyle\simeq(\operatorname{colim}_{h}H^{4-i}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j)))^{*}\simeq(H^{4-i}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j)))^{*}.

The first isomorphism follows from the fact that
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{h}H^{i-1}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))=0
by Section
2.2.4
since the pro-system
\{H^{i-1}_{c}(X^{0}_{h},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\}_{h\in{\bf N}}
is built from compact type spaces with compact transition maps (hence it is equivalent to a pro-system of Banach spaces with dense transition maps (by
[
30
, by discussion after Prop. 16.5]
). The second isomorphism is induced by the analog of the isomorphism (
8.39
). It follows that the duality map (
8.42
) is an isomorphism, as wanted.

(ii)
Duality map
\gamma_{X,i}
.
Write the duality map

\gamma_{X,i}:H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\to H^{4-i}_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}

as the composition

\gamma_{X,i}:H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))\xrightarrow[\sim]{{\rm eval}}(H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))^{*})^{*}\xrightarrow[\sim]{\gamma^{c,*}_{X,4-i}}H^{4-i}_{c}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(2-j))^{*}.

The evaluation map is an isomorphism since
H^{i}({X},{\bf Q}_{p}(j))
is reflexive by Proposition
4.23
, hence LS, hence solid reflexive by
[
29
, Thm. 3.30]
. This proves that
\gamma_{X,i}
is an isomorphism, as wanted.
∎
